## 2026-11-19 · Milestone 7za: does 7z's factor-of-two wave term survive viscous heave and pitch damping? (`experiments/run_strip_viscous.py`; plan and expectations committed before the run)
- Why: 7z's main limit and second "Next". Strip theory put the monohull's pooled head-sea motion term at 2.1 times STAWAVE-2's, but the gap is carried by a sharp heave/pitch resonance (regular-wave peak 5.6 to 6.6 times STAWAVE-2's) that the potential-flow model damps only by wave radiation. Deep, slender sections (B/T 1.46) radiate little, so any viscous damping the real hull has would cut that peak most. If a modest damping ratio closes most of the gap, the 2.5% to 4.3% range of 7z narrows toward STAWAVE-2; if not, the strip number stands.
- Method: 7z's `WigleyStrip` unchanged, with an equivalent linear viscous damping added to the heave and pitch equations, B_v33 = 2 zeta sqrt(C33 (M + A33)) and B_v55 = 2 zeta sqrt(C55 (I + A55)), A33 and A55 the strip-summed added mass and inertia at each mode's zero-speed natural frequency (fixed-point iteration). Viscous damping dissipates energy without radiating waves, so the Gerritsma-Beukelman integral keeps only the radiation damping b'; it changes R_aw only through the motions (the usual treatment of viscous roll damping in added-resistance work). No measured damping exists for this notional hull, so zeta is swept: 0, 0.025, 0.05, 0.10, 0.15, 0.20 (the range 7z named). Context number: the radiation damping's own ratio to critical at each mode's natural frequency.
- Plan: per zeta, (A) the regular-wave sigma curves of 7z (monohull Fr 0.16, 0.20; in-range Wigley Fr 0.20): peak and area ratio against STAWAVE-2; (B) the pooled head-sea motion term at 0.5, 1.0, 1.5, 2.3 m/s; (C) the monohull 30 W range loss at shares 0.25 and 1 and its best speed; (D) the 200 W scope: monohull leads the central foil to 200 W and the 972-case box at or below 200 W, H_lim unlimited and 1.0 m, share 0.25. The section tables are built once and shared across zeta.
- Expectations (before the run): (D1) zeta 0 reproduces 7z to the printed digit (pooled 1.00 m/s motion 2.95 N; 30 W loss -4.31% at share 0.25, -15.06% at share 1). (D2) The radiation damping at the monohull's zero-speed heave natural frequency is under 15% of critical. (D3) The monohull's Fr 0.16 regular-wave peak falls monotonically with zeta, and at zeta 0.10 its ratio to STAWAVE-2's peak is under 3 (7z: 5.7). (D4) At zeta 0.10 the pooled 1.00 m/s strip-to-STAWAVE-2 ratio lies in 1.2 to 2.0 (7z: 2.13). (D5) The in-range Wigley's Fr 0.20 area ratio falls by a smaller fraction than the monohull's Fr 0.16 area ratio from zeta 0 to 0.20 (its wider sections radiate more, so viscous damping matters less). (D6) The monohull 30 W loss at share 0.25 falls monotonically with zeta and stays between STAWAVE-2's 2.52% and 7z's 4.31% at every zeta > 0. (D7) The 200 W scope holds at every zeta (central and 0 of 972 box cases at or below 200 W).
- Outcome (`experiments/results_strip_viscous.txt`, 193 s plus a post-hoc calibration, stdlib, deterministic; 5 new tests in `tests/test_strip_viscous.py`): all seven held, D4 and D6 at the edge of their windows. **D1:** zeta 0 gives 7z's 2.95 N (ratio 2.13), -4.31% and -15.06%, 641 W. **D2:** zero-speed heave natural frequency 6.34 rad/s (T 0.99 s, lambda/L 0.38 at that frequency, A33/M 0.335), radiation damping 12.9% of critical (pitch 6.05 rad/s, 9.8%); the in-range Wigley radiates more, 19.2% in heave and 15.0% in pitch. **D3:** monohull Fr 0.16 peak ratio 5.70, 3.78, 2.75, 1.76, 1.29, 1.05 at zeta 0, 0.025, 0.05, 0.10, 0.15, 0.20 (monotone; 1.76 at 0.10). **D4 (edge):** pooled 1.00 m/s ratio 2.13, 1.74, 1.49, 1.20 (1.2008), 1.06, 1.00. **D5:** area ratio falls 60% on the monohull (2.23 to 0.90) and 51% on the in-range hull (1.58 to 0.78). **D6 (edge):** 30 W loss at share 0.25 -4.31, -3.69, -3.30, -2.84, -2.62, -2.52% (monotone; at zeta 0.20 it equals STAWAVE-2's 2.52%, the window's edge); share 1 -15.06 to -9.39%. **D7:** monohull leads the central foil to 200 W and 0 of 972 box cases overtake at or below 200 W at every zeta, H_lim unlimited and 1 m; central break-even 641 to 653 W (unlimited). Best speed 1.00 m/s at every zeta and share except 7z's own share-1 0.90.
- Descriptive: at 0.5 m/s the pooled ratio barely moves (2.74 to 2.45): there the energy sits in waves shorter than the resonance, where the motion term is small and damping does little, so the low-speed gap is not a resonance effect. At 2.3 m/s strip theory drops below STAWAVE-2 from zeta 0.15 (0.77, 0.63).
- Post hoc (added after the first run, marked in the script; not in the committed plan): calibrating zeta so that strip theory's Fr 0.20 area equals STAWAVE-2's on the in-range Wigley, where STAWAVE-2 is inside its fit range, gives zeta 0.091; carried to the monohull it gives a pooled 1.00 m/s ratio 1.24 (1.72 N), Fr 0.16 area ratio 1.21, and a 30 W loss of -2.90% (share 0.25) and -10.67% (share 1). This rests on two untested assumptions: that STAWAVE-2 is right in range, and that the missing damping is the same fraction of critical on both hulls.
- Reading: 7z's factor of two is a resonance effect, and it is fragile. Equivalent viscous damping of 10% of critical cuts it to 1.2, and 20% erases it. The radiation damping the model already has (12.9% in heave) is of the same order, so the swept values are not small corrections. The calibrated figure, 2.9% at 30 W, is the best single estimate the programme has. The interval 2.5% to 4.3% from 7z still brackets every case and stands as the stated range. The ranking and the 200 W scope hold at every damping level.
- Paper: one sentence after 7z's strip-theory sentences in section III-A; `check_numbers.py` gains 5 claims. Web figure `experiments/strip_viscous.svg` (`figure_strip_viscous.py`, shared `linefig`). Not compiled here (no pdflatex).
- Limits: an equivalent linear damping in fraction of critical is a sweep, not a measurement (no decay test exists for this notional hull); the same zeta in heave and pitch and at every speed; viscous damping assumed to radiate nothing, so R_aw's integral keeps only b'; the calibration takes STAWAVE-2 as truth in range and a hull-independent zeta, both untested; head seas only; still no tank data.
- Next: Journee's Wigley I to IV tank data (still the deciding test, and it would fix zeta for an L/B 10 Wigley directly); a heave/pitch decay estimate for a round-bilge 4 m hull from published small-craft free-decay tests; the method figure (energy model); the slender survey-hull curve below Fn 0.3.

## 2026-11-19 · Milestone 7z: strip-theory check of STAWAVE-2 at 4 m (`experiments/run_strip_added_resistance.py`; plan and expectations committed before the run)
- Why: 7y's first "Next" and its main limit. STAWAVE-2 is a fit to ships of 50 to 400 m with L/B 4 to 9 and B/T 2.2 to 9; the monohull is L/B 10 and B/T 1.46, so the 7y wave term is an extrapolation of a dimensionless fit. Strip theory computes the same quantity from the hull's own sections, so it needs no fit range: if it agrees with STAWAVE-2 on an in-range Wigley and disagrees on ours, the gap measures the extrapolation error.
- Method: (i) 2D heave added mass a33 and damping b33 of each Wigley section (parabolic, y = b(x)(1 - (z/T)^2)) by the Frank (1967) close-fit source method in deep water, stdlib, with the complex exponential integral by series and continued fraction. (ii) Heave and pitch in regular head waves by ordinary strip theory in relative-motion form (Gerritsma and Beukelman 1967; Journee 1992): sectional force D/Dt(a33 Ds/Dt) + b33 Ds/Dt + rho g b_w s, s the relative motion against the Smith-corrected wave, D/Dt = d/dt - U d/dx, kyy 0.25 L. (iii) Added resistance by the Gerritsma-Beukelman radiated-energy method, R_aw = k/(2 omega_e) int (b33 - U da33/dx) |V_z|^2 dx. (iv) Irregular seas exactly as 7y (ITTC 1978 spectrum, T01 = 1.086 APD, 14,758 NDBC records, bow-sector share 0.25 and 1.0); the short-wave reflection term stays STAWAVE-2's (strip theory has none).
- Plan: verification first (Green function satisfies the free-surface condition; near-field damping equals the far-field radiated energy; RAOs tend to 1 and to the wave slope in long waves; R_aw tends to 0). Comparison A: an in-range Wigley (L/B 7, B/T 2.5, CB 4/9) at Fr 0.15, 0.20, 0.25: strip-theory against STAWAVE-2 motion term over lambda/L 0.3 to 3. Comparison B: the programme's monohull (4 m, L/B 10, B/T 1.46) at the same Froude numbers, then pooled irregular head seas at 0.5 to 3 m/s. Then 7y's sections A and B rerun with the strip motion term in place of STAWAVE-2's: monohull 30 W loss, best speed, central foil break-even and the 972-case box against 200 W.
- Expectations (before the run): (S1) free-surface residual of the Green function under 1e-4 relative; near- and far-field damping within 2%; heave RAO within 2% of 1 and pitch RAO within 5% of k at lambda/L 20. (S2) In-range Wigley at Fr 0.20: strip-theory peak of R_aw/(rho g zeta^2 B^2/L) within a factor 2 of STAWAVE-2's and at lambda/L within 0.3 of it. (S3) For the 4 m monohull at 1.0 m/s the pooled head-sea motion term lies within a factor 2 of 7y's 1.39 N (0.7 to 2.8 N). (S4) Monohull 30 W range loss at share 0.25, strip motion plus STAWAVE reflection, lies in 1% to 6% (7y: 2.52%). (S5) The 200 W scope survives: monohull leads the central foil to 200 W and no box case overtakes at or below 200 W at every H_lim, share 0.25. (S6) 30 W best speed moves by at most 0.10 m/s.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_strip_added_resistance.txt`, 238 s on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 7 new tests in `tests/test_strip.py`): S1, S4, S5, S6 held; S2 refuted on the peak (lambda/L part held); S3 refuted narrowly. **S1:** E1(1) and E1(i) to 1e-10 of Abramowitz and Stegun; the closed-form PV integral matches direct quadrature to 1e-4; free-surface residual 4e-6; the wave part at 60 m is the outgoing wave to 3e-5; near- and far-field damping within 3% at 1,332 of 1,350 table points for the monohull (18, 1.3%, on irregular frequencies and interpolated; 51 of 1,350 for the wider in-range sections); in waves 10 and 20 hull lengths long heave is 0.992 to 1.001 and pitch/k 0.999 to 1.007, sigma under 2e-4; 24 panels and 60 stations move the Fr 0.16 peak by 0.8%. **S2 refuted:** on the in-range Wigley (L/B 7, B/T 2.5) strip theory peaks 2.40, 2.61, 2.82 times STAWAVE-2 at Fr 0.15, 0.20, 0.25 (area over lambda/L 0.3 to 3: 1.58 to 1.66 times), at lambda/L 0.20 to 0.25 shorter (the shift part held). **S3 refuted narrowly:** pooled head-sea motion term at 1.00 m/s 2.95 N against STAWAVE-2's 1.39 N (2.13 times; window was 0.7 to 2.8 N); 2.74 times at 0.5 m/s, 2.28 at 1.5, 2.61 at 2.3. On the monohull itself the regular-wave peak is 5.6 to 6.6 times STAWAVE-2's (sigma 16.9 at Fr 0.16, lambda/L 0.85, against 2.97 at 1.05), area 2.2 to 2.4 times. **S4 held:** 30 W monohull range, share 0.25, 823.7 -> 788.2 km (-4.31%; STAWAVE-2 -2.52%); share 1 -15.06% (STAWAVE-2 -9.37%); T01 from DPD -5.80% and -19.00%; per buoy-month -1.66% (44013 July) to -7.74% (42036 January). **S5 held:** monohull leads the central foil to 200 W and 0 of 972 box cases overtake at or below 200 W at every H_lim and both shares; central break-even 641 to 656 W at share 0.25 (STAWAVE-2: 651 W unlimited), 591 W at share 1 unlimited; box minimum 293 W (248 W share 1). **S6 held:** best speed stays 1.00 m/s at share 0.25 and drops 0.10 m/s, to 0.90, with every wave head-on (the edge of the window).
- Reading: STAWAVE-2 is not conservative for this hull. Strip theory gives about twice its wave term in the measured seas, but strip theory also sits 1.6 times above STAWAVE-2 on a hull inside the fit range, so roughly 1.4 times (2.2 / 1.6 in area) is what the slender, deep 4 m sections add and the rest is the method gap that exists at ship scale too (the Gerritsma-Beukelman method is a radiated-energy estimate with no viscous damping, and STAWAVE-2 is an empirical fit; which side the truth lies on needs model-test data, and the Wigley tank data were not reachable here). The programme's 30 W wave loss is therefore 2.5% to 4.3% (bow sector) and 9.4% to 15.1% (all head seas) across the two methods, and the paper's claim that waves do not change the ranking or the 200 W scope holds under both. The foil crossover moves down by at most 10 W from 7y at share 0.25.
- Paper: one sentence pair after the STAWAVE-2 paragraph in section III-A citing `gerritsma1972added`, `frank1967oscillation` and `john1950floating` (added to `refs.bib`, DOIs checked); `check_numbers.py` gains 8 claims. Web figure `experiments/strip_added_resistance.svg` (`figure_strip_added_resistance.py`, shared `linefig`). Correction to the plan text above: the relative-motion strip theory and the added-resistance method are cited to Gerritsma and Beukelman 1972 (doi:10.3233/ISP-1972-1921701), which was opened as a record; the plan's "1967; Journee 1992" were from memory and are not cited.
- Limits: ordinary strip theory (no 3D or end effects beyond what the Wigley's zero end sections give; forward speed enters only through the U da/dx term and the encounter frequency); no viscous heave or pitch damping, which matters most at the lightly damped resonance that dominates this result; irregular frequencies patched by interpolation, not removed; head seas only and the same +/-45 deg share convention as 7y; the reflection term is still STAWAVE-2's; at 2.3 m/s and above (Fr over 0.35) strip theory is outside its usual range; no tank data for validation.
- Next: tank data for a Wigley at L/B 10 (Journee's Wigley I to IV tests are the obvious set) to say which method is right at this slenderness; a viscous roll-off of the resonance (an equivalent linear heave/pitch damping swept 0 to 20% of critical); the method figure (energy model); the slender survey-hull curve below Fn 0.3.

## 2026-11-19 · Milestone 7y: added resistance in measured waves on the monohull's 200 W lead and the foil crossover (`experiments/run_wave_added_resistance.py`; plan and expectations committed before the run)
- Why: 7x's "Next" and its named larger missing term. Every rung so far ran in calm water, and the 14,758 hourly NDBC sea states already in `experiments/data/ndbc_waves_2024.csv` (12 buoys, January and July 2024: Hs, dominant and average period) have only been used for the Wave Glider. A reviewer will ask whether the monohull's lead to 200 W and the 659 to 841 W foil crossover survive real seas, where a small displacement hull pays added resistance and a hydrofoil may not be able to fly at all.
- Method source: ITTC Recommended Procedure 7.5-04-01-01.1 (2021, rev. 06), Annex G.2, STAWAVE-2 (Boom et al. 2013): R_wave = R_AWML + R_AWRL per unit zeta_A^2, motion term 4 rho g B^2/L r_aw(omega) with r_aw = omega_bar^b1 exp(b1/d1 (1 - omega_bar^d1)) a1 Fr^1.5 exp(-3.5 Fr), omega_bar = sqrt(L/g) kyy^(1/3) Fr^0.143 omega / 1.17, a1 = 60.3 CB^1.34, b1 11 / -8.5, d1 14 / -566 (L/B)^-2.66; reflection term 0.5 rho g B alpha1, alpha1 = pi^2 I1^2(1.5kT) / (pi^2 I1^2 + K1^2) f1, f1 = 0.692 (V/sqrt(T g))^0.769 + 1.81 CB^6.95; irregular seas R_AWL = 2 int R_wave/zeta_A^2 S(omega) d omega with the ITTC 1978 spectrum of the same document (A = 173 Hs^2/T01^4, B = 691/T01^4). Bessel I1, K1 from Abramowitz and Stegun 9.8.3 to 9.8.8 (stdlib). kyy 0.25 (conventional), CB 4/9 (Wigley), B and T from each rung's hull (monohull 0.4 x 0.274 m; catamaran per demi-hull, summed, no interaction).
- Plan: 7w's central rungs, grids, common basis and hotel grid unchanged. T01 = 1.086 x APD (PM ratio T01/Tz; NDBC APD is a zero-crossing period); sensitivity T01 = 0.772 x DPD. Records binned on T01 to 0.1 s; R_AWL is exact in Hs^2 per bin. Headings: STAWAVE corrects only waves within +/-45 deg of the bow, so with survey lines on every heading the expected term is 0.25 of the head-sea value (primary); 1.0 (all head seas) as the upper bound. One survey speed (7x policy 1). The method's stated range (L 50 to 400 m, L/B 4 to 9, B/T 2.2 to 9, Fr 0.10 to 0.30) excludes these 4 m hulls (L/B 10 and 14, B/T about 1.5): an extrapolation, stated as such. SWATH and planing get no wave term (no applicable method here) and are dropped from the wave comparison. Hydrofoil: no wave model; foilborne operation is allowed only where Hs <= H_lim, H_lim swept 0.3, 0.5, 1.0, 1.5, 2.0 m and unlimited (no sourced limit for this craft; 0.3 m is its strut draft). Foil-favourable comparison: in the records where the foil can fly, the monohull pays its wave term averaged over those same records and the foil pays nothing; break-even hotel (central foil) and the 972-case box at k 0.25 against 200 W. Also: per buoy-month monohull 30 W loss and foil operable share; pooled waves plus 7x pooled wind at CdA 0.1.
- Expectations (before the run): (V1) Bessel values within 1e-6 relative of A&S tables (I1(1) 0.5651591, K1(1) 0.6019072, I1(5) 24.335642, K1(5) 0.0040446); the spectrum integrates to Hs^2/16 within 0.5%. (Y1) At 30 W with the 0.25 head share, pooled waves cut the monohull's peak range by 0.5% to 5% from calm; with all head seas by 2% to 20%. (Y2) The catamaran's 30 W ratio to the monohull moves by under 3% (share 0.25). (Y3) At the monohull's 30 W best speed the motion term is larger than the reflection term (draft 0.27 m needs waves under about 2 m long to reflect). (Y4) The monohull's 30 W best speed rises by 0 to 0.10 m/s (a near speed-independent added force acts like extra hotel load). (Y5) The 200 W scope survives the foil-favourable comparison: the central foil break-even stays above 200 W, and no box case overtakes at or below 200 W, at every H_lim with share 0.25. (Y6) At unlimited H_lim and share 0.25 the central foil break-even falls below 7w's calm 659 W, into 400 to 659 W. (Y7) Pooled, under 20% of hourly records have Hs <= 0.5 m and 20% to 50% have Hs <= 1.0 m.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_wave_added_resistance.txt`, 58 s, stdlib, deterministic; 8 new tests in `tests/test_wave_added_resistance.py`): all eight held (V1, Y1 to Y7). Data: 14,758 hourly records, 11 buoys and 20 buoy-months (the plan said 12 buoys: a miscount, same file), Hs mean 1.44 m, median 1.27, 90th 2.62, max 5.88. V1: I1, K1 within 1.5e-8 relative of Abramowitz and Stegun; spectrum m0 = Hs^2/16 +0.14% at T01 4, 7, 10 s. Y1: monohull 30 W range 823.7 -> 802.9 km (-2.52%) at the 0.25 bow-sector share, 746.5 km (-9.37%) with every wave head-on. Y2: catamaran ratio 0.866 -> 0.871 (+0.65%; +2.69% at share 1). Y3: at 1.00 m/s the pooled head-sea term is motion 1.39 N, reflection 0.72 N against 6.41 N calm water. Y4: best speed stays 1.00 m/s at both shares. Y5: the monohull leads the central foil to 200 W, and 0 of 972 box cases overtake at or below 200 W, at every H_lim and both shares (12 of 12 rows). Y6: unlimited H_lim, share 0.25: 659 -> 651 W (only -1.2%, in range but at its edge); share 1: 627 W; box minimum 302 W (279 W at share 1). Y7: pooled share of hours with Hs <= 0.3, 0.5, 1.0, 1.5, 2.0 m: 2.4, 11.9, 37.0, 60.7, 78.1%.
- Descriptive: the monohull's wave loss shrinks as hotel load grows (share 0.25: -5.1% at 1 W, -2.4% at 30 W, -1.9% at 100 W, -0.5% at 1 kW), the same direction as 7x's wind, because a near speed-independent added force matters most when propulsion is a small share of a slow hull's energy. Sensitivity T01 = 0.772 DPD (longer periods weigh swell, but the integral moves to the high-frequency tail less): -3.41% and -12.36%. Per buoy-month at share 0.25 the 30 W loss runs -0.96% (44013 July) to -4.60% (42036 January). Foilborne share at Hs <= 1 m: Gulf of Mexico and Gulf of Maine summers 92 to 100%, the four Pacific buoys 0 to 22.8% in either month. Waves and 7x's pooled wind together at CdA 0.1: monohull 30 W -4.90% from calm; foil break-even 706 -> 698 W.
- Reading: on energy alone waves do not threaten the ranking: the monohull still leads to 200 W even when the foil pays nothing in waves, and the crossover moves by 1 to 5%. The binding question for the foil is operability, not range: a 0.3 m strut flies in 2.4% of open-ocean hours and 0% of Pacific January hours, so outside sheltered summer water a fast-rung crossover is a statement about a craft that would mostly be hullborne. The paper now says so in section III-A.
- Paper: one paragraph after the wind paragraph in section III-A, `ittc2021trials` added to `refs.bib` (opened this session, Annex G.2); `check_numbers.py` gains 9 claims. Web figure `experiments/wave_added_resistance.svg` (`figure_wave_added_resistance.py`, shared `linefig`). Not compiled here (no pdflatex).
- Limits: STAWAVE-2 is fitted to ships of 50 to 400 m with B/T 2.2 to 9; these hulls are 4 m with B/T about 1.5, so every number is an extrapolation of a dimensionless fit (a strip-theory or panel check at this scale is the next step); head seas only, with the ITTC +/-45 deg convention for headings; one-peaked ITTC spectrum from total Hs, though many of these records mix swell and wind sea; no wave term at all for SWATH, planing or the foil (the comparison is foil-favourable by construction); the foil's Hs limit is swept, not sourced; kyy 0.25 conventional, not measured; calm-water speed loss from slamming or green water not modelled.
- Next: a seakeeping check of STAWAVE-2 at 4 m (strip theory, e.g. a Salvesen-Tuck-Faltinsen or Gerritsma-Beukelman added-resistance integral) on the monohull; a sourced foilborne sea-state limit for small fully submerged foils; the method figure (energy model); the slender survey-hull curve below Fn 0.3.

## 2026-11-19 · Milestone 7x: measured ocean wind instead of still air on the foil crossover (`experiments/run_wind_air_drag.py`; plan and expectations committed before the run)
- Why: 7w's "Next" and its first limit. 7w added air drag in still air only; at sea the craft meets a real wind from every heading. A wind of w at relative heading theta gives an along-track air force 0.5 rho CdA |V_a| (u + w cos theta), whose heading average for w >> u grows as about 1.5 u w, linear in the craft's own speed, not as u^2. So a slow hull (the monohull at 1 to 3 m/s) may pay proportionally more for real wind than the fast foil, the opposite of still air, and the sign of the change to the 659 to 818 W crossover is not obvious.
- Plan: 7w's script and cases unchanged (central rungs, speed grids, common basis, hotel 1 W to 10 kW at 12 per decade, CdA 0, 0.02, 0.05, 0.1, 0.2, 0.4 m2, rho_air 1.225), with the still-air term replaced by its expectation over (a) wind speed, the 84,776 ten-minute NDBC records already in `experiments/data/ndbc_wind_*.csv` (19 buoy-months, January and July 2024, pooled mean 5.7 m/s, as measured at the buoy anemometer, about 4 m), and (b) heading, uniform over the circle (survey lines cover all headings; 72 points). Drag along the apparent wind with one CdA for every direction; side force, leeway and windage on sea state omitted. Speed policy 1 (primary): one survey speed for all headings and winds, chosen to maximise range. Policy 2: the speed re-chosen per (wind, heading) cell on equal-length legs. Per CdA: 30 W ratios and the monohull's 30 W range change against still air; break-even per rung; the 200 W scope (central and the 972 foil cases at k 0.25). Sensitivity: winds scaled by 0.86 (log profile from 4 m to 1 m, z0 0.0002 m) and by 1.5; break-even per buoy-month (19 values) at CdA 0.1.
- Expectations (before the run): (W1) zero wind reproduces 7w's break-evens at every CdA (foil 659, 667, 681, 700, 739, 818 W). (W2) The monohull leads every rung at every hotel up to 200 W at every CdA under pooled wind, central and all 972 foil cases. (W3) At every CdA > 0 the pooled-wind foil break-even lies below the still-air one at the same CdA, by less than 10% (wind costs the slow monohull more, in proportion, than the foil). (W4) At CdA 0.1 and 30 W, wind lowers the monohull's peak range by 2% to 6% from still air, and the hydrofoil-to-monohull ratio rises over its still-air value. (W5) Catamaran and SWATH 30 W ratios move by under 1% from still air at every CdA. (W6) Policy 2 recovers less than half of the monohull's 30 W wind loss at CdA 0.1. (W7) Across the 19 buoy-months at CdA 0.1, every foil break-even lies in 500 W to 900 W.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_wind_air_drag.txt`, 15 s, stdlib, deterministic; 5 new tests in `tests/test_wind_air_drag.py`): five of seven held; W3 and W5 refuted. Wind: 84,776 records, mean 5.68 m/s, median 5.2, 90th 10.2, max 18.9. Force per unit CdA at 1 m/s: still 0.61, wind 5.25 N/m2 (8.6x); at 2.5 m/s 3.83 against 13.58; at 15 m/s 137.81 against 156.81. W1 held (zero wind = still air at every CdA; test against 7w's own function to 1e-9). W2 held: monohull leads to 200 W in all 24 scenario-CdA rows; 0 of 972 foil cases overtake at or below 200 W at any CdA under pooled wind. **W3 refuted (sign):** pooled-wind foil break-even is above still air at every CdA > 0: 669, 683, 706, 751, 841 W at CdA 0.02 to 0.4 (+0.2% to +2.8%; magnitude under 10% held); box median 893 to 1,086 W, minimum 316 to 420 W. W4 held: CdA 0.1, 30 W, monohull 821.2 -> 803.0 km (-2.21%), foil ratio 0.136 -> 0.137 (+1.04%); at 0.4 m2 the monohull loses 8.23%. **W5 refuted:** catamaran under 1% up to 0.2 m2 (+0.92%) but +1.87% at 0.4; SWATH +1.16% at 0.1, +2.29% at 0.2, +4.43% at 0.4 (both rise against the monohull, which pays most). W6 held, trivially: policy 2 recovers 0.000 of the loss (every cell picks 1.00 m/s). W7 held: buoy-month break-evens 701 to 711 W (mean winds 2.60 to 7.89 m/s). Sensitivity: winds x0.86 (log profile to 1 m) give 704 W at 0.1 and 834 W at 0.4; winds x1.5 give 714 and 874 W.
- Post hoc (added after the first run, marked in the script): at the still-air break-even (700 W, CdA 0.1) the monohull runs at 2.30 m/s with 38.5 N of water drag and the foil at 10.50 m/s with 143.3 N; wind adds 0.92 N to the monohull (0.32 -> 1.24 N) and 1.84 N to the foil (6.75 -> 8.59 N), +0.51% against +1.01% in energy per metre. The 1.5 u w penalty dominates only where the monohull is slow (30 W, 1 m/s), not at the crossover. Policy 2 recovering nothing holds on a 0.005 m/s grid too (804.07 km both ways): the monohull's best speed sits in a hollow of the Michell wave-resistance curve, and the wind's slope change is too small to move it to the next hollow (about 1.08 m/s). Thin-ship hollows are known to be sharper than measured ones, so the "no gain from speed adaptation" result is partly a property of the model.
- Reading: the paper's crossover becomes "659 to 841 W for CdA 0 to 0.4 m2 in measured wind" (874 W with winds 1.5x); the 200 W scope statement survives every case. Real wind hurts the slow monohull most at low hotel load (up to 8% of range at 0.4 m2), which narrows Table I's margins at 30 W but reverses none.
- Paper: three sentences after the still-air paragraph in section III-A, `ndbc2024` added to `refs.bib`; `check_numbers.py` gains 9 claims. Web figure `experiments/wind_air_drag.svg` (`figure_wind_air_drag.py`, shared `linefig`); no TikZ version. Not compiled here (no pdflatex in this container).
- Limits: drag along the apparent wind with one CdA for every direction (beam-on area is larger, but its force is mostly across track); side force, leeway and the induced drag of resisting it omitted; calm water even in 19 m/s wind (wind and waves co-vary, and added resistance in waves is the larger unmodelled term); NDBC anemometer height of about 4 m is from memory for the 3 m discus buoys, not opened this session (covered by the x0.86 and x1.5 scalings); two months of one year; uniform headings.
- Next: added resistance in waves with the NDBC wave records already in `experiments/data/ndbc_waves_2024.csv` (the larger missing term, and it co-varies with this wind); the method figure (energy model); the slender survey-hull curve below Fn 0.3.

## 2026-11-19 · Milestone 7w: air drag as a swept term, so the fast-rung crossover is more than indicative (`experiments/run_air_drag.py`; plan and expectations committed before the run)
- Why: 7v's last "Next". The paper scopes its headline to payloads up to 200 W and quotes the hydrofoil overtaking the monohull near 659 W, but every rung was run without aerodynamic drag, which 7v estimated at about 10% of foil drag at 11 m/s and more for planing (whose wide-grid peak sits at 38.75 m/s). A reviewer will ask whether the crossover, and the 200 W scope, survive air drag.
- Plan: 7v's script unchanged in every other respect (central cases, speed grids, common basis, hotel 1 W to 10 kW at 12 per decade), with a still-air drag term 0.5 rho_air CdA u^2 added to every rung's resistance at every speed. rho_air 1.225 kg/m3 (ISO 2533 standard sea-level atmosphere). No measured drag area exists for these notional 200 kg craft, so CdA is swept: 0, 0.02, 0.05, 0.1, 0.2, 0.4 m2 (Hoerner 1965: bluff above-water bodies Cd about 0.8 to 1.2, faired about 0.3; frontal areas of 0.06 to 0.4 m2 for a 4 m, 0.4 m-beam hull with sensor mast, the hydrofoil's whole hull being in air). The same CdA is applied to every rung (displacement rungs have less in air, so this is conservative for the monohull). Per CdA: order and ratios at 30 W; break-even hotel per rung; whether the monohull leads every rung up to 200 W; the foil box (972 cases) against the k 0.25 monohull; the planing central case on the 1 to 40 m/s grid (its best speed at 10 kW).
- Expectations (before the run): (A1) CdA 0 reproduces 7v exactly (foil 659 W, planing 1,068 W; 46 of 49). (A2) The monohull leads every rung at every hotel load up to 200 W at every CdA, central and all 972 foil cases. (A3) The central foil break-even rises monotonically with CdA and stays finite (the foil still overtakes by 10 kW) at 0.4 m2, between 659 W and 1.5 kW. (A4) With CdA >= 0.1 m2 the wide-grid planing best speed at 10 kW falls below 25 m/s, and the foil has at least the planing range at all 49 hotel loads (E4 of 7v restored once air drag is in). (A5) The displacement rungs' 30 W ratios move by less than 1% at every CdA up to 0.4 m2 (air drag is negligible at 1 to 1.5 m/s).
- Outcome (`experiments/results_air_drag.txt`, 2 s, stdlib, deterministic; 5 new tests in `tests/test_air_drag.py`): A1, A2, A3, A5 held; A4 refuted. A1: CdA 0 gives foil 659 W, planing 1,068 W, 46 of 49, box median 881 W, exactly 7v. A2: at every CdA the monohull leads every rung up to 200 W in the central case, and none of the 972 foil cases overtakes at or below 200 W (k 0.25). A3: central foil break-even 659, 667, 681, 700, 739, 818 W at CdA 0, 0.02, 0.05, 0.1, 0.2, 0.4 m2 (monotone, finite, inside 659 W to 1.5 kW); box median 881 to 1,063 W, minimum 310 to 398 W, 90th 1,425 to 1,906 W; all 972 still overtake by 10 kW. Planing central break-even 1,068 to 1,744 W. **A4 refuted:** at CdA 0.1 the wide-grid planing best speed at 10 kW is still 38.25 m/s (it drops to 29.25 at 0.2 and 23.50 at 0.4), and the foil is at or above planing at 47 of 49 loads (44 of 49 on the 40 m/s grid); all 49 only at 0.4 m2. A5: at 30 W the catamaran ratio moves +0.36% and SWATH +0.81% at 0.4 m2 (held); the hydrofoil's 30 W ratio falls 13% (0.141 to 0.123), as its whole hull is in air at 3 to 4 times the speed.
- Reading: air drag moves the fast-rung crossover up by at most a quarter over the swept range and leaves the 200 W scope statement untouched; the paper's "659 W" becomes "659 to 818 W for CdA 0 to 0.4 m2". The planing number stays a grid-ceiling artefact below about CdA 0.2 m2, so the foil crossover is still the one to quote.
- Paper: a paragraph in section III-A after the hotel-ranking paragraph; `check_numbers.py` gains 8 claims. Web figure `experiments/air_drag.svg` (`figure_air_drag.py`, shared `linefig`); no TikZ version yet.
- Limits: still air (no head or tail wind; a headwind at the foil's 15 m/s raises the dynamic pressure, a sweep of wind speed is the obvious next term); one CdA for every rung; spray drag and waves omitted; at 10 kW the monohull's best speed sits on its 6.00 m/s grid ceiling (Fn 0.96), far outside the validated regime, so the high-load ratios are model statements only.
- Next: the method figure (energy model); the slender survey-hull curve below Fn 0.3; a wind-speed sweep (apparent wind) on the foil crossover; a TikZ version of the air-drag figure if the paper keeps it.

## 2026-11-18 · Milestone 7v: where Table I's ranking breaks as the hotel load grows (`experiments/run_hotel_ranking.py`; plan and expectations committed before the run)
- Why: the paper's headline (section 1, Table I) is that the hull ranking is robust to every unsourced input, but every rung was compared at one hotel load, 30 W. Hotel load is the input that sets the best speed (7t panel b, slope 0.318), and the sourced survey payloads in this programme run from 8 W (Tethys) to 104 to 175 W (REMUS implied). A reviewer will ask over what range of payload power "the monohull leads" holds, and at what load a fast rung overtakes it.
- Plan: each rung's central case exactly as in 7t (same resistance functions, same speed grids: displacement 0.30 to 6.00 m/s, fast 1 to 20 m/s), on the common basis (200 kg, 10 kWh, eta 0.55 x 0.85). Hotel load on a log grid 1 W to 10 kW, 12 points per decade. Per hotel: each rung's peak range and best speed, ratio to the monohull, the full order, and the monohull's best-speed Froude number. Break-even hotel per rung (first log-interpolated crossing of ratio 1). Robustness: the hydrofoil's whole milestone-5 box without the battery axis (972 cases; battery cancels in a ratio) and the planing box (16 cases) against the monohull at k 0.10, 0.25 and 0.40: distribution of break-even hotel loads, and the share of foil cases that overtake below 200 W.
- Expectations (before the run): (E1) at 30 W the order and ratios reproduce 7t (monohull > catamaran > SWATH > hydrofoil > planing; ratios 0.866, 0.663, 0.141, 0.097). (E2) The monohull leads every rung at every hotel load up to 200 W, the whole sourced payload band, in the central case and in every box case. (E3) The central hydrofoil overtakes the monohull somewhere in 300 W to 3 kW (back of envelope: foil about 250 N at 10 m/s, monohull capped by its hump near 3 m/s, so range ratio tends to about v_foil / v_mono once hotel dominates). (E4) The hydrofoil has at least the planing hull's range at every hotel load (higher lift-to-drag at the same weight). (E5) At every crossover the monohull's best speed is above Froude number 0.3, i.e. every break in the ranking lies where milestone 3q found the displacement and Michell models unvalidated, so the paper can state the ranking for the validated regime and flag the rest.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_hotel_ranking.txt`, 2 s, stdlib, deterministic; 7 new tests in `tests/test_hotel_ranking.py`): E1, E2, E3, E5 held; E4 refuted at the top of the range. E1: at 30 W the ratios are 0.866, 0.663, 0.097, 0.141, as 7t. E2: the monohull leads every rung at every hotel load up to 200 W in the central case, and none of the 972 foil cases nor the 16 planing cases overtakes at or below 200 W against the monohull at k 0.10, 0.25 or 0.40 (held). The catamaran never overtakes (max ratio 0.969, approached at high load where its slender demi-hulls pass the hump more cheaply) and SWATH never does (max 0.733). E3: the central hydrofoil breaks even at 659 W (held); planing at 1,068 W. Box break-even for the foil: min 329 / 310 / 291 W and median 903 / 881 / 861 W at k 0.10 / 0.25 / 0.40 (10th to 90th at k 0.25: 539 to 1,425 W); planing min 675 / 656 / 638 W, median 1,338 / 1,298 / 1,272 W. **E4: the foil is at or above planing at 46 of 49 hotel loads; planing leads at the top three (10 kW: 2.230 against 2.104), refuted as worded.** E5: the monohull's best speed first exceeds Fn 0.3 at 215 W, and both central crossovers sit at Fn 0.37 to 0.39 on the next grid point; every box crossover (291 W and up) is past 215 W (held).
- Post hoc (not pre-registered; added after the first run): the planing peak sits at the 20 m/s grid ceiling from 215 W up. On a 1 to 40 m/s grid its best speed is 38.75 m/s at every load shown, break-even falls to 798 W and the foil is at or above planing at only 39 of 49 loads. At 39 m/s (Cv 14.8, wetted length near the module's floor) air drag, spray drag and Savitsky's range all bite and none is modelled, so neither planing break-even is a design figure; the foil's best speed (10.25 to 16.5 m/s) stays inside its 20 m/s grid, so its crossover is the one to quote.
- Paper: a paragraph and Fig. (hotel-rank) in section III-A; `paper/make_figures.py` writes `fig_hotel_ranking.tex`; `check_numbers.py` gains 10 claims (83 in all). Web copy `experiments/hotel_ranking.svg` (shared `linefig`). The headline claim now carries its scope: the ranking is a statement about payloads up to 200 W.
- Limits: central cases for the displacement rungs (the 7t curves), only the foil and planing boxes swept; calm water; air drag omitted for every rung (about 10% of foil drag at 11 m/s by a rough estimate, more for planing); above Fn 0.3 the Michell model is not validated (3q), so the crossovers are indicative. Not compiled here (no pdflatex in this container); the figure is a TikZ input in the same style as the three that compile.
- Next: the method figure (energy model); the slender survey-hull curve below Fn 0.3; air drag as a swept term if the fast-rung crossover is to be quoted as more than indicative.

## 2026-11-18 · Milestone 7u: glider against AUV as a curve in the hotel-load ratio, the paper's third figure (`experiments/run_glider_hotel_curve.py`; plan and expectations committed before the run)
- Why: 7t's last "Next". Section III-B states the glider-against-AUV result as three points (AUV hotel x1, x5, x20) and a break-even median of 4.87; a reviewer reads the mechanism off a curve, not three rows, and the REMUS-100 vendor band belongs on the same axis.
- Plan: the 2,430 milestone-4b cases (`run_slocum.py`, gliderflight Slocum coefficients, code unchanged) with the AUV's hotel load set to x times the glider's, x on a log grid 0.1 to 1,000 (12 points per decade). Per x: share of cases where the glider has the longer range (equal battery, so range ratio = AUV J/m over glider J/m) and the 10th / 50th / 90th percentile of that ratio. The REMUS-100 band from `run_remus.py`'s implied hotel loads divided by the glider's own (0.7 to 5 W) is marked on the axis.
- Expectations (before the run): (E1) the share curve is the empirical CDF of the break-even multiple, so it passes 10%, 50% and 90% near x = 2.38, 4.87 and 10.67 (within one grid step) and reproduces 0.0%, 51.6% and 99.9% at x = 1, 5 and 20; (E2) the median ratio is monotone in x and crosses 1 between x = 4 and 6; (E3) at large x the AUV's best J/m grows as hotel^(2/3) (minimising a v^2 + h/v), so the median ratio's log-log slope over x = 20 to 200 lies in 0.60 to 0.70 (a little under 2/3 because CF falls with Re at the higher best speed); (E4) the REMUS band lies wholly where the glider wins every case.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_glider_hotel_curve.txt`, under 1 s, stdlib, deterministic; 6 new tests in `tests/test_glider_hotel.py`): E1, E2 held; E3 refuted on the planned grid, explained post hoc; E4 missed by a hair. Share of cases the glider wins passes 10%, 50%, 90% at x = 2.38, 4.88, 10.83 (log-interpolated; 4b's break-even quantiles 2.38, 4.87, 10.67, so the 90% point sits within one grid step) and reads 0.0%, 51.56%, 99.88% at x = 1, 5, 20 (4b: 0.0, 51.6, 99.9; held). Median range ratio monotone, reaches 1 at x = 4.87 (held). At x = 0.1 the glider's median range is 0.083 of the AUV's. **E3: the median ratio's log-log slope over x = 20 to 200 is 0.850, outside 0.60 to 0.70 (refuted)**; over x = 1 to 10 it is 0.645. E4: the REMUS-100 band (implied hotel 104.1 to 174.9 W over the glider's 0.7 to 5 W, x = 20.8 to 249.8) has a minimum share of 0.9992 at its floor, so 2 of 2,430 cases still favour the swept AUV hull there (refuted as worded, "every case"); `run_remus.py`'s own REMUS hull gives 100%.
- Post hoc (not pre-registered; added to the same script after E3 failed): the 4b AUV speed grid stops at 1.5 m/s, and the AUV's unconstrained best speed exceeds it in 0.0%, 11.1%, 55.6% and 100% of cases at x = 1, 5, 20, 200. With AUV speeds to 4.0 m/s the slope over 20 to 200 is 0.653 (inside E3's range) and over 1 to 10 0.641, the crossover is unmoved (median ratio 1 at 4.87; share 10/50/90% at 2.38, 4.89, 11.10) and the REMUS-band minimum share is unchanged (0.9996 on the grid). So the 1.5 m/s ceiling of milestone 4b flatters the glider only where it already wins at least 99.88% of cases; no earlier verdict moves.
- Paper: Fig. (glider-hotel), two panels (range-ratio band with the uncapped median dashed; share of wins), and a paragraph in section III-B; `paper/make_figures.py` writes `fig_glider_hotel.tex`; `check_numbers.py` gains 12 claims (73 in all), including a correction of the REMUS implied-hotel floor the draft did not yet quote (104.1 W, not the 105.7 W of the k 0.2 row). Web copy `experiments/glider_hotel.svg` (shared `linefig`).
- Limits: the 4b grid's AUV hulls, not a fielded vehicle; equal battery; steady flight, no surfacing or current; the glider's own hotel is the grid's 0.7 to 5 W, unsourced per vehicle.
- Next: the method figure (energy model) toward OCEANS's 6 pages; the slender survey-hull curve below Fn 0.3 (section 3, item 1); open Michell 1898 and Ju 1983 when a host serves them; widen the 4b AUV speed grid to 4 m/s in `run_slocum.py` itself if the paper is to quote x = 20 rows.

## 2026-11-18 · Milestone 7t: range against speed per rung, the paper's second figure (`experiments/run_range_speed.py`, expectations committed before the run in 3b2f1f741)
- Why: 7s's first "Next". The paper had a ranking table and a solar bar chart but nothing that shows *why* the ranking comes out as it does; a range-against-speed curve per rung is the standard figure for that, and every reviewer of a hull comparison expects one.
- Plan: each rung's own resistance model (run_michell, run_swath, run_planing, run_foil) at the centre of its swept box, on the common basis (200 kg, 25% battery at 200 Wh/kg = 10 kWh, 30 W, eta 0.55 x 0.85); displacement speeds 0.30 to 6.00 m/s, fast rungs 1 to 20 m/s. Panel (b): the monohull at hotel loads 5, 15, 30, 60, 120 W. Expectations (docstring): peaks in Table I's order; every displacement peak in 0.8 to 1.5 m/s; best speed rising roughly as P_hotel^(1/3).

Peaks: monohull 823.7 km at 1.00 m/s; catamaran 713.1 at 0.90 (0.866); SWATH 546.1 at 0.70 (0.663); hydrofoil 116.4 at 10.25 (0.141), foilborne from 9.00 m/s; planing 80.3 at 3.25 (0.097), feasible from 2.75 m/s. Order as Table I (held). **SWATH peaks at 0.70 m/s, below the expected 0.8 floor (refuted for SWATH)**: its torpedo hulls carry more wetted area, so friction dominates further down the speed range. Hotel sweep: best speed 0.60, 0.85, 1.00, 1.20, 1.65 m/s and peak range 2,669.6, 1,300.1, 823.7, 516.5, 323.4 km for 5 to 120 W; log-log slope 0.318 (held).

The displacement curves ripple with Michell's humps and hollows. - Next: the slender survey-hull curve below Fn 0.3 (section 3, item 1); open Michell 1898 and Ju 1983 when a host serves them; grow the paper toward OCEANS's 6 pages with a method figure (the energy model) and the glider-vs-AUV curve against hotel ratio.

## 2026-11-18 · Milestone 7s: the hull-ranking paper as an OCEANS-format LaTeX draft (`paper/main.tex`, `paper/refs.bib`), every number copied from `paper/DRAFT.md` and the results files it names (no experiment run)
- Why: pick_track's CHAIN notice (milestone 6 has 89 sub-steps, 20 since a LOG heading named a paper step). Milestone 7 (paper in the venue's format) is the programme's last, and the draft existed only as Markdown, so no reviewer could read it as a paper. Section 2.7 of `DRAFT.md` already carries the clamp chain to 6zzzk, so another clamp step or another Markdown fold would add less than the format step.
- What: `paper/main.tex` (IEEEtran conference class, as OCEANS asks): abstract, introduction with narrow contributions, model, ranking table (catamaran, tank-bound catamaran, SWATH, planing, hydrofoil, glider against AUV at equal and vendor hotel), absolute-range section with deck solar, tank-data section (Wigley, Holtrop-Mennen, Delft yachts), threats, conclusion. `paper/make_figures.py` draws Fig. 1 (km/day at five sites, 4 m and 5 m, 5% to 95% whiskers) as TikZ from `results_solar_mc.txt`. `paper/check_numbers.py` holds 53 (results file, quoted string) pairs and fails if a rerun changes any; `tests/test_paper.py` (4 tests) runs it and the figure parser. Compiles with pdflatex + bibtex to 3 pages, no warnings printed.
- Corrections found while checking numbers against the files (the paper uses the file's figures): DRAFT's Wigley line says the no-form-factor model is within +1% to +5% of the tank CT; over 10 to 25 C `results_wigley_tank.txt` gives -1% to +5% (k = 0.25: +18% to +25%, DRAFT "about 20%"). DRAFT's table says "0 of 729 equal-length" for the Michell catamaran; 729 is per wave scale, the file's total is 0 of 2,187.
- Scope decision: the paper carries the hull-ranking claim only. The wing-sail chain (2.5) and the learned solar correction chain (2.7, 6zz to 6zzzk) are separate questions with their own evidence and would each fill a paper; they are named as companion work in the conclusion.
- Citations: Crossref and doi.org were refused by the proxy this session, so nothing was newly machine-checked. DOIs in `refs.bib` are the ones `PROPOSAL.md` lists (checked in milestone 1). Michell (1898) is new and written from memory, so its DOI was left out and marked to look up; Liu et al. (2016) author list is from memory and marked.
- Limits: 3 pages against OCEANS's 6 to 10; no hull figure (range against speed per rung) yet; Wang et al. author list incomplete; the yacht-series data are a public mirror.
- Next: a range-against-speed figure per rung from `run.py`'s sweep (needs a small script writing the curve, not a new experiment); open Michell 1898 and Ju 1983 when a host serves them; the slender survey-hull curve below Fn 0.3 (section 3, item 1).

## 2026-11-18 · Milestone 6zzzk: does a forced brightening remove the wide clamp's win at the nine in 6zzzh's 2010 to 2014 rows? 6zzzj's forced level shift, moved into the one design where the wide clamp won (predictions in `experiments/run_offset_sign_out.py`'s docstring, committed in 696024bcb before the full run)
- Why: 6zzzj's first "Next". 6zzzj found at 6zzzi's design that darkening the learned month ratios always costs at the nine and brightening always pays, so the dark offset of 6zzzh's learning years cannot be why ±10% won there (hi84 at k = 3: C10 46.7, C02 34.0, AT_90 27.6). Two readings were left: (A) offset accuracy, the 2010 to 2014 scored years are themselves dark, so the learned dark offset is a correct forecast that a wide clamp passes (brightening then removes the win, and AT_90 fails on dark hours there); (B) scored decade, something about 2010 to 2014 at the nine favours a wide clamp whatever the offset (C10 then rises with d, as in 6zzzj, and its lead survives).
- Plan: 6zzzh's design (2015 to 2024 climatology, leave-one-out), its OUT years 2010 to 2014 only, the frozen nine, k = 3, 10 lag sets, 50 W, 4 packs, real plus 20 swept shapes, nasa world; 6zzzj's rules unchanged (AT_90 and the learned ratios times (1 + d), clamped to 1 ± h; h in {±2%, ±5%, ±10%}, d in {-3%, -1.5%, 0, +1.5%, +3%}); hi84 failure modes per rule; realised harvest over the climatology at the nine for each scored year and its learning years.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_offset_sign_out.txt`, 5 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 5 new tests in `tests/test_offset_sign_out.py`): **two of nine predictions held, and reading (A) is refuted.** Checks passed: d = 0 reproduces 6zzzh's hi84 at the nine for AT_90 (18, 0, 44, 0, 76) and C02, C05, C10 in every year. hi84 five-year means, d = -3% to +3%: C02 31.2, 32.5, 34.0, 34.9, 35.2; C05 34.3, 37.5, 40.7, 42.2, 42.0; C10 36.7, 41.4, 46.7, 47.3, 46.6 (AT_90 27.6). So darkening costs here too, most through the wide clamp (C10 loses 10.0 from d = 0 to -3%, C02 2.8), and brightening helps up to +1.5% and then flattens. K1 held only as a tie (C10 46.6 at +3% against 46.7 at 0). K8 held: C10 beats C02 at every d (+5.5, +8.9, +12.8, +12.4, +11.5), so the wide clamp's win survives every shift. K2 to K7 and K9 refuted: the C10 endpoint difference is +9.9 (6zzzj +16.5), C10 rises then flattens, C02's best shift is +3%, and AT_90's failing cells fail on dark hours in 1 of 282 (km alone in 281). K9: eq9 disagrees in sign (C10 -1.6 from -3% to +3%, hi84 +9.9).
- Descriptive: realised harvest over the climatology at the nine is 0.9925 to 0.9976 in the scored years and 0.9932 to 0.9976 in the mean of their learning years, so at the nine the learned offset is about -0.5% and about as dark as the year it forecasts; the 30-point figures 6zzzh read as "darker" (0.989 to 0.998) overstate it at the nine. Failures are on km against B for every rule (dark share 0.4% for C02 at -3% to 22.5% for C10 at +3%). Years differ: 2011 and 2013 (AT_90 0 of 84) rise with d for C10 (15.4 to 40.6, 4.5 to 10.6), 2014 falls (77.2 to 48.7, its km already above B's for AT_90, so boldness spends dark hours). eq9 favours the dark side for every width (C10 16.4 at -3% to 14.8 at +3%; C02 14.4 to 13.8), so the hi84 rise is carried by the high-km points in the sum. pp36 sits within 0.5 of AT_90's 28.8 for every rule. Real shape, 2.5 kWh, lags (1, 2, 3): km over the nine rise with d for C05 and C10 in every year; C02 flattens above +1.5% in 2011 and 2012.
- Reading: in both designs the binding constraint at the nine is distance against B, and the plain climatology fails on km even in dark years (281 of 282 failing cells), so reading (A) does not hold: the dark years are not ones where the climatology is too bold. The wide clamp's win in 2010 to 2014 is not carried by the level offset, which is small (-0.5%) and which the forced shift moves by up to six times its size without removing the lead. What a wide clamp passes there, and a narrow one cuts, is the month-to-month shape of the learned ratios. Read with 6zzzj, the same three widths and five shifts give C10 - C02 between +5.5 and +12.8 here and between -20.0 and -8.6 there, so the sign of the wide clamp's lead is set by the design (scored decade and climatology together), not by any level offset in the tested range. For a designer the rule is unchanged: ±2% is at or above no correction in all five years here at every d (worst +0.4, 2011 at d = -3% and 2014 at +3%), and on the mean it is the only width at or above no correction in both designs (here +6.4 at d = 0; 6zzzi a tie); ±10% is a bet on the decade.
- Limits: one design, five years sharing learning seasons, k = 3 only; the shift is uniform over months, so the shape reading is inferred from what the shift could not move, not tested; the nine are mostly parked, so hi84 is carried by P-30 and the North Sea and eq9 disagrees with it; one boat, 50 W payload, weights and calm-water daily model; nasa world only; the 20 swept shapes are synthetic. After the run the failure-mode header's cell count was corrected from 700 to 4200 (84 cells x 10 sets x 5 years) in the script and the results file; no number changed (6zzzj's header has the same slip, 840 for 5040).
- Next: split the learned correction into level (the annual mean of the month ratios) and shape (the ratios over that mean) and clamp each separately under both designs, to test the shape reading directly; 2026 once NASA POWER completes it, C02 pre-registered.

## 2026-11-18 · Milestone 6zzzj: does the sign of the learned offset decide whether a wide clamp wins at the nine high-amplitude points? A forced level shift d of the learned month ratios, before the clamp, inside 6zzzi's design (predictions in `experiments/run_offset_sign.py`'s docstring, committed in 282adea41 before the full run)
- Why: 6zzzi's first "Next". 6zzzi read post hoc that the sign of the offset the learning years carry against the climatology decided the width curve at the nine: dark learning years (6zzzh, 2010 to 2014 under the 2015 to 2024 climatology) made the wide clamp win by 19 cases, bright ones (6zzzi, realised 1.015) made it lose by 12. Proposed mechanism (S): a dark expectation makes the boat cautious, which at mostly parked high-latitude points rarely costs distance, so a wide clamp passing it is safe; a bright one makes it bold and spends dark hours. A forced shift tests (S) with the years, climatology and noise held fixed: every learned month ratio times (1 + d), then clamped to 1 ± h; a narrow clamp cuts most of d, a wide one passes it.
- Plan: 6zzzi's design unchanged (2005 to 2014 climatology, scored 2020 to 2025, the frozen nine, k = 3 only, 10 lag sets, 50 W, 4 packs, real plus 20 swept shapes, nasa world); h in {±2%, ±5%, ±10%}, d in {-3%, -1.5%, 0, +1.5%, +3%}; d = 0 reproduces 6zzzi; hi84 failure modes (km only, dark only, both) counted per rule.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_offset_sign.txt`, 6 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 5 new tests in `tests/test_offset_sign.py`): **three of nine predictions held, and the mechanism is refuted in its direction.** Checks passed: d = 0 reproduces 6zzzi's AT_90 and its C02, C05, C10 rows at k = 3 in every year. hi84 six-year means rise with d for every width: C02 45.5, 47.2, 48.5, 49.4, 50.5; C05 37.1, 41.6, 44.1, 45.9, 47.8; C10 25.4, 32.7, 36.6, 38.5, 41.9 (d = -3% to +3%; AT_90 48.5). So darkening hurts and brightening helps, and the wide clamp is the more sensitive one (C10 moves 16.5 cases over the range, C02 5.0): C10 - C02 is -20.0, -14.5, -11.9, -10.9, -8.6, narrowing as d *rises*. S1 to S6 refuted (S1 gap non-increasing in d; S2 C10 > C02 at d = -3%; S3 a flatter curve at d = -1.5%; S4 C10 worse at +3%; S5 C02 within 3 at every d, it moves 5.0; S6 C10 better at -1.5%). S7 held (C10's failing cells fail on dark hours in 11.7% of cases at d = +3% against 0.3% at -3%); S8 held (at -3% 99.7% of C10's failures are on km alone); S9 held (best C02 at d = +3%, 50.5 against AT_90 48.5, +2.0).
- Descriptive: failures at the nine are overwhelmingly on km: across all 15 rules 88% to 100% of failing hi84 cells fail on km alone (AT_90 192 of 213 over the six years). eq9 follows hi84 (C02 35.1 to 38.6 over d, AT_90 37.6; C10 21.6 to 31.9); pp36 is flat (29.7 to 31.0 for every rule, AT_90 31.0). C02 at d = +3% is above AT_90 in 3 of 6 years (2021, 2024, 2025) and below in 2020, 2022, 2023. Real shape, 2.5 kWh, 2025, lags (1, 2, 3), summed over the nine: B 4,103 km / 47 h, AT_90 4,399 / 13, C02 4,542 to 4,662 km at 14 to 15 h over d, C10 4,347 to 5,196 km at 17 to 35 h.
- Reading: the sign-of-offset reading of 6zzzi is withdrawn. At the nine, under this design, the binding constraint is distance against B, not dark hours, so a darker expectation always costs and a brighter one always pays, most of all through a wide clamp; the dark-hour cost of boldness that (S) relied on appears (S7) but stays small (at most 12% of failures). This cannot explain 6zzzh's 2010 to 2014 wide-clamp win, where the learned offset was dark: a dark offset passed by a wide clamp is the worst cell here. What differs in 6zzzh is the scored decade and the climatology together, not only the offset sign, so the cause of that win is open again. For a designer the clamp rule is unchanged (±2%, never wide at high latitude), and the step adds one fact: at mostly parked high-latitude points the correction errs costly on the cautious side. The +2.0 for a +3% bias at ±2% is in-sample and holds in only half the years, so it is not a rule.
- Limits: one design (2005 to 2014 climatology, 2020 to 2025) and only k = 3; the shift is uniform over months, not seasonal; the six years share learning seasons; the nine are mostly parked (6zzzd), so hi84 is carried by P-30 and the North Sea; one boat, 50 W payload, weights and calm-water daily model; nasa world only; the 20 swept shapes are synthetic. After the prediction commit, `configure()` gained one line that re-applies the 2005 to 2014 climatology (`oc.use_clim`) so tests run in any order; the full run used the earlier file, whose import sets the same climatology, so no number changes. The same order leak was fixed in `tests/test_width_hi9.py`.
- Next: the same shift under 6zzzh's 2010 to 2014 design, where the wide clamp won, to see whether a forced brightening there removes the win (an offset-accuracy reading) or not (a scored-decade effect); a seasonal (summer-only) shift at the nine, since 6zzn found summer carries the high-latitude failure; 2026 once NASA POWER completes it, C02 pre-registered.

## 2026-11-18 · Milestone 6zzzi: at the nine high-amplitude points, is the best clamp wide because the season is large there, or because the learning years carry an offset? 6zzzg's width sweep rescored at the nine points of 6zz, frozen as a list (predictions in `experiments/run_width_hi9.py`'s docstring, committed in 852e3037a before the full run)
- Why: 6zzzg's third "Next" (a climatology-independent high-amplitude pool). `ls.high_amp_points()` measures the bias amplitude against the climatology in use, so under 6zzzg's 2005 to 2014 climatology it picked five points and hi84 ranked nothing (AT_90 41.7, every width 40.4 to 42.0). Here the pool is the nine that 6zz picked under the 2015 to 2024 climatology (P-60, P-30, P+50, P+60, A-50, A+50, A+60, North Sea, Gulf of Alaska), frozen. 6zzzh found opposite curves at those nine: with learning years outside the climatology (2010 to 2014) the widest clamp won (k = 3 hi84 C10 46.7, C02 34.0, AT_90 27.6); with learning years inside it (2020 to 2025) the narrow ones did (C01 39.7, C02 39.6, AT_90 38.8). Reading (a): the high-latitude season (4.5 to 8.5%) needs a wide clamp. Reading (b): the wide clamp wins only by passing a decade offset. Scoring 2020 to 2025 under the 2005 to 2014 climatology puts the learning years outside it and pairs each year with 6zzzh's IN row. Plan: 6zzzg's code and rule set unchanged, only the nine points evaluated; hi84, eq9 and pp36 at the nine.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_width_hi9.txt`, 4 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 5 new tests in `tests/test_width_hi9.py`): **three of eight predictions held, and neither reading survives.** Checks passed: on 6zzzg's five-point pool these cells reproduce its hi84 for AT_90 (52, 63, 42, 0, 43, 50) and C03 at k = 3 (55.3, 63.0, 42.0, 0.0, 42.0, 45.5). **H1 to H5 refuted**: the best width at k = 3 is C02 (48.5), level with no correction (AT_90 48.5), not wide; C10 is far below (36.6); C10's lead over C02 is larger than under 6zzzh's climatology in only 1 of 6 years; C02 is at or above AT_90 in 3 of 6 years. H6 held (eq9 best C01 37.8 against AT_90 37.6). H7 held: realised harvest over the old climatology at the nine averages 1.0153 (1.0014 to 1.0226), so the learning years do carry a bright offset. H8 held (best k = 5 C02, k = 1 C01).
- Descriptive: hi84 six-year means at k = 3: AT_90 48.5, C01 48.2, C02 48.5, C03 48.0, C04 46.3, C05 44.1, C07 40.0, C10 36.6; at k = 1 the curve falls from C01 48.0 to C10 32.6, at k = 5 from C02 47.8 to C10 34.5. Mean gain over AT_90 at k = 3, this step | 6zzzh IN (same years, 2015 to 2024 climatology): C01 -0.3 | +0.9, C02 -0.0 | +0.7, C03 -0.5 | +0.1, C05 -4.4 | -0.8, C07 -8.5 | -1.6, C10 -11.9 | -2.8. Every width gains in 2025 (+3.3 to +8.9) and every width from ±4% up loses in the other five years; 2022 is the worst (C10 -26.5). eq9 falls monotonically from C01 37.8 to C10 29.3; pp36 sits within 0.5 of AT_90's 31.0 for every width. 73% of learned month ratios at the nine fall outside ±2% and 8.5% outside ±10%. On the real shape at 2.5 kWh in 2025, km and dark hours both rise with h (AT_90 4,399 km / 13 h, C02 4,607 / 15, C10 4,817 / 28; B 4,103 / 47).
- Post hoc (not predicted): the two designs in which the learning years lie outside the climatology give opposite curves at the nine. Where they were darker than it (6zzzh, 2005 to 2013 against 2015 to 2024) the wide clamp won by 19 cases; here, where they are brighter (2015 to 2024 against 2005 to 2014, realised 1.015), it loses by 12. A plausible mechanism, not tested: a dark learned offset makes the boat more cautious, which at mostly parked high-latitude points rarely costs distance against B, while a bright one makes it bolder and spends dark hours. On this reading 6zzzh's wide-clamp win at the nine came from the sign of the offset, not from the size of the high-latitude season.
- Reading: at the nine high-amplitude points the clamp width that is safe on ordinary water (±2%) is also the best one, but it gains nothing over the plain climatology on the pooled count under either climatology for 2020 to 2025 (-0.0 here, +0.7 under the current one). No width from ±4% up is safe there: in five of six years they fall below no correction, by up to 26 of 84. This withdraws 6zzzh's suggestion that high latitude wants a wide clamp. For a designer, one rule now covers both pools: clamp the learned month ratios to ±2%; at high latitude expect it to match, not beat, the climatology.
- Limits: the nine were picked with the 2015 to 2024 climatology, so the pool is fixed but not neutral; 6zzzd showed the eight band points are mostly parked (B 0 km at three), so hi84 is carried by P-30 and the North Sea; one draw of offset sign per design (two designs), so the sign mechanism is a post-hoc reading; the six scored years share learning seasons; k = 2 and 4 not run; one boat, 50 W payload, weights and calm-water daily model; nasa world only; the 20 swept shapes are synthetic.
- Next: test the offset-sign mechanism directly with a forced level shift of the learned ratios (±1.5%) at the nine, as 6zzn did for one learning year; 2026 once NASA POWER completes it, with C02 pre-registered in both pools; ERA5 2025 at the nine for the cross world.

## 2026-11-18 · Milestone 6zzzh: does the ±2% to ±4% plateau move with the climatology? 6zzzg's fixed widths rescored under the 2015 to 2024 climatology on 2010 to 2014 (learning outside it) and 2020 to 2025 (learning inside it) (predictions in `experiments/run_width_new_clim.py`'s docstring, committed in 20b6e43be before the full run)
- Why: 6zzzg's second "Next". 6zzzg's plateau was measured with every learning year in the decade after the climatology, so the learned month ratios carried a decade-level offset (realised 0.5 to 1.8% above it) that a wider clamp lets through. Under the 2015 to 2024 climatology (leave-one-out by year) the same scored years 2020 to 2025 learn from years inside it, so each pairs with 6zzzg's row and only the climatology differs; 2010 to 2014 (learning 2005 to 2013, outside and before it) are 6zzze's reference years. The question: is the plateau a property of the month-ratio noise (stays put) or of the offset (narrows with no offset to learn)? Plan: 6zzze's code with 6zzzg's rule set (`configure()` reused): C01 to C10, AT_90, k = 1, 3, 5; nasa world; counts as 6zzze.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_width_new_clim.txt`, 66 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 5 new tests in `tests/test_width_new_clim.py`): **nine of ten predictions held.** Checks passed: AT_90 all84 reproduces 6zzze in 2010 to 2014 and 2025 (66, 77, 80, 68, 67, 63), and C03 its k = 3 rows (81.3, 83.0, 80.9, 76.7, 73.0, 69.3) and 2025 at k = 1 and 5 (63.8, 69.0). N1 to N3 held: the best width at k = 3 is **C02 in both groups** (IN = 2020 to 2025 mean 66.6, OUT = 2010 to 2014 mean 80.5), and C02 is also best at k = 1 and 5 (on OUT at k = 5 C01 and C02 tie at 80.6, broken to the narrower). N4 held only at rounding: C02's IN gain over AT_90 is +7.07 against 6zzzg's best +7.10 (both from counts printed to 0.1), so read it as a tie. N5 held: C02 beats AT_90 in all 11 years (worst +0.9, 2022). N6, N7 held: C10's IN mean gain is -2.6 against 6zzzg's +0.2, below 6zzzg's in 4 of the 6 paired years. **N8 refuted**: the IN plateau is C02 66.6 and C03 66.1, but C01 (64.3) is 2.3 below. N9 held: 40.3% of IN's learned month ratios lie outside ±3% (OUT 48.7%; 6zzzg 45%). N10 held: per point no width beats AT_90 on IN (best C01 75.5 against 77.3).
- Descriptive: IN mean all84 at k = 3: AT_90 59.5, C01 64.3, C02 66.6, C03 66.1, C04 63.8, C05 62.2, C07 58.7, C10 56.9; OUT: AT_90 71.6, C01 79.7, C02 80.5, C03 79.0, C04 77.3, C05 75.9, C07 73.8, C10 71.9. Years above AT_90 out of 11: C01, C02, C03 all 11; C04 8; C05 6; C07 5; C10 4. Paired with 6zzzg (IN mean gain over AT_90, 6zzzg | here): C01 +3.7 | +4.8, C02 +6.9 | +7.1, C03 +7.1 | +6.6, C04 +6.3 | +4.3, C05 +5.1 | +2.7, C07 +2.2 | -0.8, C10 +0.3 | -2.6. 2021 is the year every width wins big under either climatology (+12 to +22). On eq84, IN, C01 and C02 are best (35.7 and 35.6 against AT_90 34.8), and widths from ±4% up fall below it. On the real shape at 2.5 kWh in 2025, km and excess dark both rise with h: AT_90 128,815 km / 156 h, C02 130,241 / 179, C03 130,829 / 191, C05 131,638 / 212, C10 131,926 / 231 (B 125,114 / 364).
- Post hoc (not predicted): across 6zzzg and this step (17 scored-year rows, the 2020 to 2025 years under two climatologies), C02 and C03 are above no correction in every row (worst +0.9 each), and C02 is the best width, or tied for it (OUT at k = 5, with C01), in all six group-by-k means here.
- Reading: the narrow end of the plateau is a property of the month-ratio noise, not of the climatology. ±2% to ±3% gains the same 7 cases of 84 whether the climatology is current or a decade stale, while the wide shoulder (±5% to ±10%) only earned its keep under the stale climatology, by passing the decade offset through: with no offset to learn it loses 2 to 3 cases and falls below no correction in most years. For a designer this makes the rule climatology-agnostic: clamp the learned month ratios to ±2% (±3% is as good on the mean); do not widen the clamp on the grounds that the climatology may be old, because a narrow clamp already captures most of the offset's value.
- Limits: OUT's years are among those ±3% was chosen on (6zzy), so OUT is in-sample for the width; the eleven years share learning seasons and the paired rows share scored years, so neither count is a set of independent draws; N4 is a tie at the precision of the printed counts; k = 2 and 4 not run; one boat, 50 W payload, weights and calm-water daily model; nasa world only; the 20 swept shapes are synthetic.
- Next: 2026 once NASA POWER completes it, with C02 as the single pre-registered rule under both climatologies; a ±1.5% / ±2.5% refinement only if 2026 separates C02 from C03; restate section 2.7's designer rules around C02; ERA5 2025 at the nine points for the cross world.

On the real shape at 2.5 kWh in 2025 (30 points, lags 1 to 3), km and excess dark hours both rise with h: AT_90 128,074 km / 147 h, C02 130,381 / 173, C03 131,136 / 186, C05 131,490 / 208, C10 131,382 / 219 (B 125,012 / 343).
- Post hoc (not predicted): C02 has the largest worst-year margin of any width (+4.0 against C03's +1.5) at nearly the same six-year mean, and the 2025 best; the 12 rows of 6zzze and 6zzzf that made C03 look unique were scored without C02.
- Reading: ±3% is not a lucky pick; it sits on a plateau from ±2% to ±4% that holds out of sample, with the year-by-year optimum wandering from ±2% (2022, 2025) to ±5% and wider (2021, 2023). The safe end of the plateau is the narrow one: ±2% and ±3% beat no correction in every year at every k scored, ±4% fails one, ±5% and wider fail half. More seasons of log do not justify a wider clamp. For a designer: correct the climatology by the learned month ratios clamped to ±2% to ±3% (±2% if the worst year matters more than the mean); per point, expect no width to beat planning on the plain climatology.
- Limits: the six scored years share learning years (2021's lags are 2020's shifted by one), so they are not six independent draws; k = 2 and 4 not run; one boat, 50 W payload, weights and calm-water daily model; nasa world only; the 20 swept shapes are synthetic; hi84 and eq9 use 6zzzf's five-point pool, which depends on the climatology, and saturate or sit near zero, so they rank nothing.
- Next: 2026 once NASA POWER completes it, scoring C02 and C03 as the two pre-registered candidates; the same width sweep under 6zzze's design (2015 to 2024 climatology, 2010 to 2014 and 2025) to see whether the plateau moves with the climatology; restate the high-amplitude pool with a climatology-independent definition; ERA5 2025 at the nine points.

## 2026-11-18 · Milestone 6zzzf: is 2025's out-of-sample margin a decade-offset artefact? 2025 rescored with the climatology built from 2005 to 2014, so its learning years lie outside it (predictions in `experiments/run_old_clim_2025.py`'s docstring, committed in 885d0484f before the full run)
- Why: 6zzze's first limit. Its 2025 corrections learned from 2020 to 2024, years inside the 2015 to 2024 climatology, while its in-sample corrections learned from 2005 to 2013, outside it, so only the in-sample ones could learn a decade-level offset. Plan: 6zzze's code unchanged except `fy.CLIM_YEARS` = 2005 to 2014 (set before any climatology is built, in the parent and the forked workers); scored years 2025 and, as the reference under the same design, 2020 to 2024, each learning from the k seasons before it (so every learning year, 2015 to 2024, lies in the decade after the climatology: the mirror of 6zzze's in-sample design); same rules, packs, shapes, counts. The climatology also sets B's and AT_90's plans, so rules are compared by their gain over AT_90 under the same climatology.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_old_clim_2025.txt`, 52 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests in `tests/test_old_clim_2025.py`): **seven of eight predictions held.** D1 held: AP's mean half-width at k = 3 on 2025 is 0.037 (6zzze 0.031; 0.035 to 0.036 in the 2020 to 2024 reference), so the learned ratios do now carry an offset. D2 held: realised 2025 harvest over the old climatology is 1.0143 (every year 2020 to 2025 is 1.005 to 1.018 above it). D3 held, barely: AP k = 3 on all84 61.4 against AT_90's 61. **D4 refuted: AP's gain over no correction falls from +5.2 to +0.4.** D5, D6 held: the narrow rules still lead the wide ones (C03 66.0, AP 61.4, HR 60.6 against AK 50.1, A1 47.9, C15 47.8), and the wide ones fall further below AT_90 (-10.9 to -13.2, against -6.3 to -9.3 in 6zzze). D7 held: 61.4 is inside the 2020 to 2024 range of AP's set means (56.2 to 72.8). D8 held: AP rises with the log (55.0, 58.9, 61.4 at k = 1 to 3).
- Descriptive: gain over AT_90 on 2025 all84 at k = 3, 6zzze's climatology | this step's: C03 +6.3 | +5.0, HR +6.3 | -0.4, AP +5.2 | +0.4, AK -6.3 | -10.9, A1 -9.2 | -13.1, C15 -9.3 | -13.2. Year by year in the reference (k = 3, gain over AT_90): AP +1.2, +19.5, -0.8, +15.8, +3.0; HR +2.5, +10.4, -2.3, +8.2, -5.2; C03 +4.5, +16.2, +1.5, +10.6, +4.7. Post hoc, across both designs (6zzze's 2010 to 2014 and 2025, this step's 2020 to 2025: twelve scored-year rows), **C03 is the only rule above no correction in all twelve** (smallest margin +0.9 in 2012, +1.5 in 2022); AP falls below it in 2012 and 2022, HR in 2022 and 2024 and on 2025 here. On the equal-weight footing (eq84) C03 is within 0.2 of AT_90 in 2025 (37.3 against 37.5) and every other rule is below it (31.0 to 34.9). Per point on the real shape (pp120), no correction is again the best rule in 2025 (79 of 120; best rule-and-k AP k = 5 76.0). On the real shape at 2.5 kWh, AP over 2022 to 2024 sails 131,414 km with 194 excess dark hours over the 30 points, against B's 125,012 km and 343 h and AT_90's 128,074 km and 147 h.
- Side effect, disclosed: the high-amplitude set (`ls.high_amp_points`) is the points whose measured climatology bias amplitude exceeds 6zz's line, and that measurement uses the climatology, so with the old decade it picks five points (P-60, P+60, A+60, North Sea, Gulf of Alaska), not the nine. hi84 and eq9 here are on that five-point pool and are not comparable with 6zzze; no prediction used them.
- Reading: most of 6zzze's out-of-sample lead for the spread clamp AP and the pooled cosine HR was the decade offset. Once the 2025 corrections learn from years outside the climatology, as the in-sample ones did, AP and HR sit level with no correction on the 30-point pool (+0.4 and -0.4 of 84), while the fixed ±3% clamp keeps nearly all of its lead (+5.0) and the wide clamps lose more. 2025 itself is not unusual under this design (AP inside the reference range, every year 2020 to 2025 0.5 to 1.8% brighter than the old decade), so the result reads as rule against rule, not as a strange year. For a designer: with a few seasons of log, correct the climatology by the learned month ratios clamped to about ±3%; letting the clamp follow the ratios' own spread (AP) or pooling a cosine (HR) leaves the boat at or below no correction in two (AP) or three (HR) of the six years under a stale climatology; never open the clamp wide.
- Limits: C03's "all twelve" is a post-hoc reading of rows that share years (2025 twice, and 2020 to 2024 here are the learning years of 6zzze's 2025), across two designs; ±3% was itself picked from a fixed-width sweep on the in-sample years (6zzy), so 2020 to 2025 under the old climatology is the first scoring it was not tuned on; one boat, 50 W payload, weights and calm-water daily model; nasa world only; the 20 swept shapes are synthetic.
- Next: 2026 once NASA POWER completes it, scored under both climatologies; a fixed-width sweep (±1% to ±5%) on 2020 to 2025 under the old climatology to see whether ±3% is an optimum or a plateau; restate the high-amplitude pool with a climatology-independent definition so hi84 is comparable across designs; ERA5 2025 at the nine points.

## 2026-11-17 · Milestone 6zzze: do the learned sun corrections hold on 2025, a year no milestone has read, when the boat learns from the seasons just before it? (predictions in `experiments/run_oos_2025.py`'s docstring, committed in 736327710 before the full run)
- Why: open in every "Next" since 6zzy ("out-of-sample scoring on 2025 sun once NASA POWER publishes the full year"). NASA POWER serves all of 2025 at the 30 points with no fill values (fetched 2026-11-17, `experiments/fetch_sun_2025.py`, `experiments/data/sun_30pts_2025.json`; a re-fetch of 1 to 5 June 2024 at P+50 matched the committed series to four decimals, so the product has not been reprocessed under us). Every rule from 6zzj to 6zzzd was fixed and ranked on 2005 to 2014. Plan: the operator's design, each scored year learning from the k seasons before it (every k-subset of lags 1..5), scored on 2025 and, as an in-sample reference under the same design, on each of 2010 to 2014; climatology 2015 to 2024 leave-one-out by year as always; rules AP, AK, A1 (6zzzc), C03, C15 and HR (k >= 2), against AT_90 and B; nasa world only; one year per cell, so 84 (shape, pack) cells per year (all84, eq84 with zero-km ties dropped, hi84, eq9, pp120).
- Outcome (`experiments/results_oos_2025.txt`, 35 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 5 new tests in `tests/test_oos_2025.py`): **five of nine predictions held.** O1 held: AP at three seasons beats no correction on the 30-point pool in 2025 (68.2 of 84 against AT_90's 63). O2 refuted: 68.2 is below every in-sample year (72.9 to 81.5), but so is AT_90 (63 against 66 to 80), so 2025 is a harder year against B for every rule, not a failure of the correction. O3, O4 held: AP 68.2 > AK 56.7 > A1 53.8, and AP > C15 53.7. O5 refuted: HR ties or leads AP out of sample (69.3 at k = 3, 71.0 at k = 4 and 5, against AP 68.2 to 69.0). O6 refuted narrowly: on the equal-weight footing AP is 1.1 cells below AT_90 (37.6 against 38.7); C03 39.1 and HR 40.5 are above it. O7 held: realised 2025 harvest / climatology 1.0082 over the 30 points (1.0171 at the nine; in-sample years 0.9889 to 0.9978). O8 held: AP rises with the log (59.6, 65.2, 68.2 at k = 1 to 3). O9 refuted: the in-sample best (C03 at k = 2, 79.2) scores 68.0 in 2025; the 2025 best is HR at k = 4 and 5 (71.0).
- Descriptive, gain over no correction on all84 at k = 3, 2025 (in-sample mean of 2010 to 2014 in brackets): C03 +6.3 (+7.4), HR +6.3 (+3.5), AP +5.2 (+6.1), AK -6.3 (+1.2), A1 -9.2 (-0.7), C15 -9.3 (-0.6). The three wide rules, at or just below AT_90 in-sample, fall 6 to 9 cells below it in 2025 at every k from 2 to 5. Per point on the real shape (pp120) no correction is the best rule in 2025 (83 of 120; HR k = 3 80.1, AP 77.9), as in-sample (AT_90 79.4, AP 76.6). AP's mean half-width at k = 3 is 0.031 in 2025 against 0.038 to 0.040 in-sample. On the real shape at 2.5 kWh, AP over 2022 to 2024 sails 131,028 km with 193 excess dark hours over the 30 points against B's 125,114 km and 364 h (AT_90 128,815 km, 156 h). The high-amplitude pools nearly saturate in 2025 (hi84 AT_90 73, most rules 78 to 84), so they rank nothing this year.
- Reading: out of sample the narrow corrections keep their lead and the wide ones lose theirs. A ±3% clamp, the spread clamp AP and the band-pooled cosine HR each beat no correction by 5 to 6 cells of 84 on the 30-point pool in 2025, about what they did in-sample; the k-scaled clamps AK and A1 and the fixed ±15% fall below no correction by 6 to 9. So 6zzzb's "AK is the most robust single rule" and 6zzzc's "AK if the planning sun product is suspect" do not survive a fresh year in the NASA world; AP, C03 or HR do. On the equal-weight footing every rule sits within 2 cells of no correction, so the pooled lead is again carried by the high-km points. For a designer: with a few seasons of log, correct the climatology by the learned month ratios only inside a narrow clamp (about ±3%, or the ratios' own spread) or through the pooled cosine; do not open the clamp.
- Limits: one out-of-sample year, and 84 cells that share it, so the 2025 column is one draw, not a distribution; the 2025 learning years (2020 to 2024) lie inside the climatology decade (each judged against the other nine years), while the in-sample learning years (2005 to 2013) lie outside it, so the in-sample corrections can learn a decade-level offset (realised 0.989 to 0.998) that the 2025 ones cannot, which is one reason AP's h is smaller in 2025; the in-sample years are among those the rules were chosen on; nasa world only (no ERA5 2025 here); the 20 swept shapes are synthetic, the same in learning and scored years; one boat, 50 W payload, weights and calm-water daily model.
- Next: 2026 once NASA POWER completes it, to make the out-of-sample column two years; restate the paper's designer rules (section 2.7 of `paper/DRAFT.md`) to drop the wide clamps; separate the decade-offset confound by scoring 2025 against a 2005 to 2014 climatology with 2015 to 2024 as learning years; an ERA5 2025 pull at the nine points for the cross world.

## 2026-11-17 · Milestone 6zzzd: why does no rule beat B at the eight band points pooled (hi8)? (predictions in `experiments/run_band_failure.py`'s docstring, committed in a257a7d11 before the full run)
- Why: 6zzzc's second "Next" item. On hi8 no rule, and not the uncorrected AT_90, dominates the 7-day trailing-mean rule B in one of 168 cells at k = 3 in the nasa and reverse worlds, while in cross AP wins 36.2 and AK 47.0. Dominance needs km >= B's and excess dark <= B's; this step asks which half fails, by how much, and whether it fails point by point or only once the eight are summed. Plan: 6zzzc's scorer unchanged at the nine points in all three worlds, k = 3 only (AP, AK, A1, C15 on the 10 sets) plus AT_90; failure modes, medians of km/B and of the dark difference, wins under a relaxed test (dark + 24 / 120 / 480 h, km - 1% / 2%), and per-point modes. One smoke run on a single shape was seen after the predictions were committed and before the full run.
