- Outcome (`experiments/results_band_failure.txt`, 9 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests in `tests/test_band_failure.py`): **one of six predictions held.** Checks passed (hi8 at k = 3 reproduces 6zzzc in all three worlds, AT_90 0 in each). P1 refuted: every one of the 168 pooled nasa hi8 failures, for every rule and set, is **km-short only**; none is on dark hours. P2 refuted: B's pooled excess dark is never 0 (median 23.0 h per pool). P3 refuted: no dark tolerance up to 480 h, and no km tolerance of 1% or 2%, wins a single nasa cell. P4 refuted: the median rule goes **0.22 to 0.27 of B's km** (AT_90 0.221, AP 0.249) with about 22 h less excess dark per pool. P5 held: per point AP wins all 168 cells at P+50, A+50 and the Gulf of Alaska. P6 refuted: in cross, AP's 131.8 failures are also all km-short.
- Post hoc (`--posthoc`, added after the run; real shape, nasa, ten years x four packs): the 50 W boat barely moves at the band points under any rule. B's ten-year km: P+50, A+50, Gulf of Alaska 0; P+60 37, A+60 48; P-60 1,367; A-50 1,786; North Sea 4,456 (P-30: 114,144). So the northern per-point "wins" are ties at zero km and zero dark, the two Southern Ocean points (P-60, A-50) lose every cell on km (AT_90 74 and 356 km against B's 1,367 and 1,786), and the North Sea, 58% of B's band km, is the only band point with a contest (AP wins 92.3 of 168; 62.6 km-short, 12.6 dark-over).
- Reading: the hi8 zero is not a near miss and not a dark-hour margin. Pooled over the eight points, B is the bolder rule: on the real shape it spends 62 to 78 h of excess dark at the three points where it moves, against 1 to 27 h for AT_90 and AP, to sail 2 to 18 times their km, so no more cautious rule can dominate it however the test is relaxed. In the nasa and reverse worlds the corrections at these points barely matter because the boat is mostly parked; where the climatology and the sea disagree (cross), B's km is 7 times larger (median 3,443 per pool) and the rules separate. For a designer: at 50 to 60 degrees with a 50 W payload the question is whether the boat can work at all, not which sun correction to use; the high-latitude dominance counts since 6zzq should not be used to rank rules there. This also explains 6zzzc's finding that hi168 is carried by P-30: P-30 holds 94% of B's hi168 km on the real shape.
- Limits: in-sample, k = 3 only; one boat, payload, weights and calm-water daily model; the post-hoc table is the real shape only (the 20 swept shapes are in the pooled counts); a tie at zero km counts as a win under the dominance test every milestone since 6zv used, which this step reports rather than changes.
- Next: rescore the high-latitude points with a payload the boat can carry there (the 6zzza Wave Glider sweep put the all-year payload at 0 to 6 W at 50 to 60 degrees), or drop hi8 / hi168 from rule selection and keep eq168 with zero-km ties excluded; restate the history-length table of 6zzq (section 2.7 of `paper/DRAFT.md`) on that footing; out-of-sample scoring on 2025 sun.

## 2026-11-17 · Milestone 6zzzc: how should the spread clamp scale with the length of the log (h = d * k^alpha, alpha 0, 0.5, 1, one to five seasons), and do the high-latitude rankings survive once P-30 stops carrying the pooled count? (predictions in `experiments/run_clamp_exponent.py`'s docstring, committed in 0885a3c88 before any run)
- Why: 6zzzb's first two "Next" items. AK's sqrt(k) scale was fixed by argument and scored only at k = 2 and 3; and 6zzzb found that the pooled high-amplitude count (hi168), which sums km and dark hours over the nine points, is carried by P-30, so every high-latitude claim since 6zzq needed restating. Plan: AP (alpha 0), AK (0.5), A1 (1, new) and C15 on every k-subset of lags {1..5} (5, 10, 10, 5, 1 sets), worlds as 6zzz; two new footings for the nine points in all three worlds: hi8 (the pooled count without P-30) and eq168 (per cell, the share of the nine points at which the rule beats B on that point's own totals: every point weighs one ninth), plus eqall (equal weight over all 30, nasa).
- Outcome (`experiments/results_clamp_exponent.txt`, 70 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests in `tests/test_clamp_exponent.py`): **seven of ten predictions held.** All checks passed (AP k = 1 reproduces 6zzy per lag in nasa all168 and in hi168 in all three worlds; AP, C15 and AK at k = 2, 3 reproduce 6zzzb's means and ranges; AT_90 139 / 126 / 25). F1, F2, F3 held: at k = 3 in the nasa world C15's lead over AP is +21.2 cells on hi168 but +2.5 on eq168 and 0.0 on hi8; in reverse it is +2.5 on eq168. F4 refuted: AK has the smallest worst gap on the hi168 footing at k = 3 (-7.3) but on eq168 AP does (-2.5). S1, S2 held: A1 at k = 3 costs on ordinary water (all168 148.3 against AK's 154.5) and wins reverse hi168 (100.6 against 93.3). S3 held: AP keeps rising on all168 (160.8 at k = 4, 161.0 at k = 5). S4 held: AK's worst all168 set at k = 4 is 148. S5 refuted narrowly: AP's mean h on all 30 at k = 5 is 0.042, not under 0.040. S6 refuted at k = 2 only: the smallest worst gap of the three exponents (hi168 footing) is A1 at k = 2 (-13.3, AK -16.4) and AK at k = 3, 4 and 5 (-7.3, -5.2, -1.0).
- Descriptive: on hi8 no rule, and not the uncorrected AT_90 either, beats B in a single pooled cell at k >= 3 in the nasa and reverse worlds (AT_90 0; AP k = 1 4.4, k = 2 0.3), so the 25 to 105 cells of hi168 every rule since 6zzq has been ranked on are cells where P-30's totals swing the pool. In cross, hi8 still separates the rules and C15 is the worst (7.9 at k = 3, against AP 36.2, A1 45.1, AK 47.0). On eq168 all four rules sit within 3 cells (nasa k = 3: AP 110.1, AK 111.9, A1 112.6, C15 112.6, against AT_90's 103.4). On eqall AP leads at every k (k = 3: 94.1, AK 92.2, A1 90.4, C15 90.5; AT_90 86.6). Mean h on all 30 at k = 1 to 5: AP 0.063 to 0.042, AK 0.063 to 0.094, A1 0.063 to 0.211; AP's h falls far more slowly than 1/sqrt(k), so the spread is mostly a persistent bias by k = 3, not noise.
- Reading: the ordinary-water against high-latitude trade-off that 6zzr, 6zzz and 6zzzb read from hi168 is mostly a P-30 effect. Weighting the nine points equally, the wide clamps gain only 1 to 3 cells of 168 at the high-amplitude points, while the narrow spread clamp AP keeps a 1 to 17 cell lead on ordinary water (k = 2 to 5); on that footing AP is the most robust rule at k = 2 to 4 (worst gap -2.5, -2.5, -3.3) and the exponent sweep matters mainly where the climatology and the sea disagree (cross), where the wider rules AK and A1 lead AP on hi8 by 8 to 16 cells and the fixed ±15% falls 26 to 29 cells below AP. Within the exponent family, scored on hi168 as before, sqrt(k) is the robust middle from three seasons (alpha = 1 wins the high-amplitude pools and loses 5 to 11 cells on ordinary water). For a designer: AP is the default once the high-latitude score is not dominated by one bright point; AK if the planning sun product is suspect. The design advice of 6zzz and 6zzzb ("a wider clamp up north") does not survive the restating; their counts stand, but their reading of them does not.
- Limits: in-sample on the 30 points and ten years every milestone since 6zzj scored; k = 4 sets share three of four years and k = 5 is one set, so their brackets are narrow by construction; eq168 counts each point's own five-year totals and so weighs noisy low-km points as heavily as P-30; the all168 pool is also km-weighted (eqall restates it); gaps are against the four rules scored here, not 6zzz's five; ERA5 stands in for the real sun; one boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model.
- Next: restate the history-length table of 6zzq (section 2.7 of `paper/DRAFT.md`) on eq168 and eqall, since its "above 50 degrees no rule is safe" row rests on hi168; per-point dominance at the eight band points against B to see why no rule wins there pooled (is B's margin in dark hours?); out-of-sample scoring on 2025 sun once NASA POWER publishes the full year; a sourced Wave Glider bus load (6zzza).

## 2026-11-17 · Milestone 6zzzb: does scaling the spread clamp with the length of the log (AK, h = d * sqrt(k)), or switching to ±15% at 50 degrees and above (AB), close AP's high-latitude gap at two and three seasons? (predictions in `experiments/run_k_aware_clamp.py`'s docstring, committed in e9abec1bb before any run)
- Why: 6zzz's first two "Next" items. At k = 3, AP tied the best fixed clamp on all 30 points (159.5 against C03's 159.6) but its worst gap to the best of 6zzz's five rules was -26.6 (reverse hi168), while C15 (±15%) had the smallest worst gap (-10.3). 6zzz read the cause as a scale error: the noise in d shrinks like 1/sqrt(k), so AP's clamp narrows with k even where a real bias is large. Plan: AK, h = d * sqrt(k) in [0.02, 0.40]; AB, AP below 50 degrees and C15 at |latitude| >= 50 (nine of the 30 points, eight of the nine high-amplitude points: all but P-30), assembled per cell from AP's or C15's scored cell. 6zzz's sets, worlds, counts and scorer, unchanged; AP and C15 re-run as checks; gaps against 6zzz's best means.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_k_aware_clamp.txt`, about 29 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests in `tests/test_k_aware_clamp.py`): **seven of ten predictions held.** All 21 checks passed (AP and C15 at k = 2 and 3 reproduce 6zzz's means and per-set ranges in every count; AT_90 139 / 126 / 25). X1 held: AK costs on ordinary water (all168 154.5 [136, 160] at k = 3, against AP's 159.5). X2 refuted: AK's worst triple on all168 is 136, below no correction's 139 (hi168 worst 47). X3, X4 held: AK lifts the high-amplitude points (nasa hi168 89.0, reverse 93.3, against AP's 74.5 and 73.8). X5, X6 held: AK's worst gap at k = 3 is -7.1, the smallest of any rule scored (AP -26.6, C15 -10.3); at k = 2 it is -16.4 (C15 -17.4, AP -27.1). AK is the best rule in cross at both k (59.4 and 57.5, against 6zzz's best AS 55.5 and 50.5). X7 refuted: AB nasa hi168 75.3, not >= 90. X8 held: AB all168 159.4. X9 refuted: AB's worst gap -26.9. X10 held: AB cross hi168 37.5, below AP's 49.2.
- Descriptive: AK's mean half-width at k = 3 is ±8.0% on all 30 points (AP ±4.6%), ±10.5% at the nine, ±17.0% in cross and ±18.0% in reverse, so the sqrt(k) scale restores the order of 6zzy's one-season clamp across worlds. AB is AP almost everywhere it is scored: all168 159.4 against 159.5, nasa hi168 75.3 against 74.5, reverse 73.5 against 73.8; only cross moves (37.5, toward C15's 41.5). Per point on the real climatology (pp72) the four rules sit within 1.2 of each other in every world (nasa 47.4 to 48.2 of 72 at k = 3).
- Reading: the sqrt(k) scale is the right fix and the latitude switch is the wrong one, and the reason the switch fails corrects 6zzz's reading. Switching the eight band points from AP's clamp to C15's moves the pooled high-amplitude count by under 1 cell, while the two rules differ there by 21: the pooled count is a sum of km and dark hours over the nine points, and the one point outside the band, P-30, has far more sun and km than the eight at 50 to 60 degrees, so (by this result: swapping the other eight changes almost nothing) it carries the pooled total. C15's lead at "the high-amplitude points" was therefore a P-30 lead, not a high-latitude one, and 6zzz's design advice ("±15% above about 50 degrees") does not follow from its numbers; per point, the clamp barely matters at the band points. AK widens the clamp where the learned ratios are far from 1 at every latitude, which is what helps at P-30 and in the mismatched worlds, and it gives up 5.0 cells on ordinary water to do it. For a designer with three seasons: AK is the most robust single rule tested (worst gap -7.1), AP or C03 the best on ordinary water if the climatology and the sea are known to agree; neither crosses both floors with AK's worst draw (136).
- Limits: in-sample on the 30 points and ten years every milestone since 6zzj scored; the sqrt(k) scale was fixed by argument, with no sweep of the exponent; pairs and triples share years, so brackets are observed ranges; the gaps compare against 6zzz's means, not a re-run of C03, AS and AF; the pooled hi168 count weighs points by their km and dark hours, which is what made P-30 decisive; ERA5 stands in for the real sun; one boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model.
- Next: a pooled count that weighs each point equally (or the hi168 pool without P-30) to restate every high-latitude result since 6zzq on that footing; AK at k = 1 to 5 with the exponent swept (0, 0.5, 1) as a declared sensitivity; out-of-sample scoring of C03, AP, AK and HR on 2025 sun once NASA POWER publishes the full year; a sourced Wave Glider bus load (6zzza).

## 2026-11-17 · Milestone 6zzza: on its own deck-solar budget, how many days a year is a Wave Glider's payload dark, and does its high-latitude distance lead survive counting only the kilometres sailed with the sensors on? (predictions in `experiments/run_waveglider_payload.py`'s docstring, committed in 8a3a6c0a9 before any run)
- Why: open since 6zj. From 6x to 6zj the solar boat paid for its 30 W payload from its own panel and pack, while the Wave Glider got its wave-powered distance with no electrical budget at all, so its high-latitude lead "says nothing yet about whether its sensors are on". Plan: the SV3 at its published maxima (225 W solar, 6.8 kWh storage; liquid-robotics.com comparison table, opened in 6zh), 225 W read as nameplate at 1,000 W/m2 with the boat's derate 0.85 (191 W, against the boat deck's 196 W); NASA POWER 2015 to 2024 at 6zk's 30 points (300 one-year missions, launch full on 1 January); a 30 W payload plus a bus load B (command and control, GPS, Iridium, rudder) that no opened source gives, so swept over 0, 3, 5 and 10 W; the bus served first, the payload greedy (G) or barred from the last 30 days of bus energy (K). Distance for 2024: 6z's best-state Wave Glider km/day and 6zk's reserve-curve boat (R), each counted only for the hours its payload was on.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_waveglider_payload.txt`, 8 s, stdlib, deterministic; 5 new tests in `tests/test_waveglider_payload.py`, one of which reproduces 6zj's idle vehicle exactly when the Wave Glider code is given the boat's deck and pack): **six of nine predictions held.** W1 held: at the boat's exact load (B = 0) the Wave Glider's payload is dark at least as often as the boat's idle vehicle in all 300 point-years (smaller pack, 2% smaller deck). W2 held: with B = 5 every point-year at 40 degrees or more has at least 123 dark days. W3 held: within 10 degrees 69 of 70 point-years have none. W4 held: at 40 degrees or more the band mean is 199.9 dark days against the boat's 146.6 (1.36 times). W5 held: the median largest dark-free payload with B = 5 is 18 to 47 W within 30 degrees and 0 to 6 W at 50 to 60, so Zhang et al.'s 10 W for payloads (abstract) holds to about 40 degrees (10 to 13 W there). W6 refuted: B from 3 to 10 W adds 54 dark days a year at 40 degrees or more, not under 25, so the unsourced bus is the largest open input. W7 refuted: a 30-day bus reserve does not keep the vehicle steering; 38 point-years still lose the bus (the winter deficit at 50 to 60 degrees outlasts 6.8 kWh), and greedy (G) loses it 708 hours a year on average at 46 to 60 degrees. W8 refuted outright: at all 14 points of 40 degrees or more where the Wave Glider out-sails the boat in 2024, it still does on payload-on km (0 of 14 flip; for example P+50 13,604 against 540, North Sea 12,278 against 7,860, Gulf of Alaska 9,492 against 5,939). W9 held: within 20 degrees the boat's payload-on km are at least 1.25 times the Wave Glider's.
- Descriptive: the Wave Glider gives up 24 to 57% of its 2024 km to payload-dark hours at 40 degrees or more and 0 to 1% within 20. At 30 degrees the two sides of 6z's tie split: at P+30 the Wave Glider led on total km (21,723 against 21,373) but the boat leads on payload-on km (19,866 against 21,373), the only point that flips. Band means of payload dark days (boat idle; Wave Glider B = 0, 3, 5, 10): 31 to 45 degrees 98; 117, 136, 147, 179; 46 to 60 degrees 173; 190, 212, 229, 272.
- Reading: giving the Wave Glider the boat's payload bill does not move 6z's map; it changes what the high-latitude side of it means. Poleward of about 40 degrees the Wave Glider keeps going through the dark months on wave power, so even counting only the hours its sensors are on it covers 1.02 to 25 times the boat's distance there (median 1.65; the thinnest margins are P+60, 7,210 against 7,057, and the Gulf of Maine, 14,102 against 13,594; the 25 is P+50, where the boat's 2024 is an outlier at 540 km, as 6zj's foresight plan's 0 km there already showed), but its payload is dark more days than the boat's (its deck is the same size and its pack a third smaller), and at 50 to 60 degrees neither vehicle has its sensors on for even half the year (Wave Glider 189 to 264 dark days at B = 5, boat 150 to 210). So at high latitude the hull choice decides how far the line runs and the power system decides whether anything is measured on it, for both vehicles; 6zj's dark-free budget (8 to 13 W at 50 to 60 degrees for the boat) is 0 to 6 W for an SV3 with a 5 W bus.
- Limits: the SV3 figures are the vendor's maxima, not a fielded configuration, which favours the Wave Glider; the bus load is unsourced and moves the answer by 54 days a year over 3 to 10 W; no panel wash-over, tilt or shading on either deck; daily energy steps; the Wave Glider's distance is 6z's ERA5 plan for its 2015 best state and 2024 only, the boat's is calm water; the boat carries no separate bus, so B = 0 is the only like-for-like row.
- Next: a sourced Wave Glider bus load (an operator manual, a published power budget or the 2015 vehicle's `total_power` telemetry in `experiments/data/waveglider_onboard_hourly.csv`, whose unit the dataset does not label (values near 650,000 in May 2015), once its meaning is found); a k-aware AP clamp (6zzz's first "Next"); out-of-sample scoring on 2025 sun once NASA POWER publishes the full year; added resistance in waves on the solar side at 30 to 40 degrees.

## 2026-11-17 · Milestone 6zzz: does the spread-set clamp (AP) sharpen with two and three seasons of log, and does it beat the pooled cosine HR there? (predictions in `experiments/run_multi_season_clamp.py`'s docstring, committed in c4fdd0e27 before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zzy's first two "Next" items. With one learning year AP had the smallest worst gap to the best fixed clamp (-13.8) but gave up 4.8 cells on all 30 points to C03; 6zzr's design table said three seasons of a fitted cosine (HR) is the first point where a learned rule never falls below no correction in either pool. Plan: every pair (10) and triple (10) of learning lags, r_m and L pooled over the set's years (6zzw's `raw`); rules C03, C15 (= LC), AS, AP and a new AF (AP's h raised to at least 0.10 when |L - 1| > 0.05, for the reverse world), plus HR in the nasa world; AP and AF also at k = 1. Worlds nasa (all 30 and the nine), cross and reverse (the nine); 6zzy's era5 world dropped for run time.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_multi_season_clamp.txt`, about 80 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests in `tests/test_multi_season_clamp.py`): **seven of ten predictions held.** All 13 checks passed: AP at k = 1 reproduces 6zzy per lag in all three worlds; C15 reproduces LC at k = 2 (140.8, hi 92.3) and k = 3 (149.3 [123, 160], hi 95.7 [50, 128]); HR reproduces 6zzk (151.9 [145, 159], hi 54.0) and 6zzr (155.5 [150, 161], hi 56.7 [31, 86]). Z1, Z2, Z3 held: AP all168 158.1 [151, 163] at k = 2 and 159.5 [155, 162] at k = 3, above HR's 151.9 and 155.5. Z4 held: AP crosses both floors at k = 3 (worst 155 / 33). Z5 held: nasa hi168 74.5 against HR's 56.7. Z6 refuted, narrowly: AP's mean h on all30 falls (0.063, 0.051, 0.046) but in cross it falls to 0.099 at k = 3, not above 0.10. Z7 held: C03 159.6 against C15 149.3 at k = 3. Z8 held: AF reverse 71.4 against AP 70.8 at k = 1, all168 cost 1.0. Z9 refuted: cross AP hi168 52.8, 53.0, 49.2. Z10 refuted: at k = 3 the worst gaps are C03 -53.5, C15 -10.3, AS -27.4, AP -26.6, AF -23.5.
- Descriptive: per point HR stays ahead (pp240 140.2 / 142.6 at k = 2 / 3 against AP 132.2 / 134.2, C03 129.9 / 131.6; AT_90 126), so AP's lead is a pooled-cell lead. At k = 2 no rule except C15 crosses both floors (AP hi168 worst 7, HR 18, C03 16); at k = 3 C03, AS, AP, AF and HR all do. On all30, C03 and AP are tied (158.2 / 158.1 at k = 2, 159.6 / 159.5 at k = 3) and C03's range is the tightest of any rule (k = 3 [156, 161]), but C03 is the worst rule in cross (8.7) and reverse (46.9). AS's wide share collapses on all30 (7.2%, 1.4%, 0.3%) and falls by a third in cross (56.9%, 37.8%, 33.7%). AF lifts reverse at every k (71.4, 71.4, 76.9 against AP 70.8, 68.4, 73.8) at a cost of 0.8 to 1.6 on all30.
- Reading: more log does what 6zzy hoped on ordinary water: the noise in the month ratios averages out, AP's clamp narrows toward C03's, and AP joins the best fixed clamp (within 0.1) while being the only one of the two that keeps 50 cells in cross. It also beats the pooled cosine on both pool means at two and three seasons. But the spread signal narrows in the mismatched worlds too (cross h 0.120 to 0.099), and at the high-amplitude points the right clamp with three seasons is wide (C15 best in nasa hi168 and reverse at k = 2 and 3), so AP's worst gap grows from 13.8 at k = 1 to 26.6 at k = 3 and the most robust single setting at k = 3 is the fixed ±15% of 6zzj (-10.3), whose cost is all on ordinary water (149.3 against 159.6). The trade-off between ordinary water and high latitude persists at every history length here, as 6zzr found for the cosine rules; with three seasons a designer picks AP (or C03 if the climatology and the sea are known to agree) for ordinary water and ±15% above about 50 degrees. Mean d is the wrong scale once the ratios average several years: it measures noise plus bias, and the noise part shrinks like 1/sqrt(k), so a k-aware mapping (h = d * sqrt(k), say) is the natural next rule, to be fixed before a run.
- Limits: in-sample on the 30 points and ten years every milestone since 6zzj scored; ten pairs and ten triples share years, so the brackets are observed ranges, not intervals; the era5 world was not run; HR only in the nasa world (its band pool spans points outside the nine swapped ones); one AF form, no sweep of 0.10 / 0.05; ERA5 stands in for the real sun; one boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model.
- Next: a k-aware AP (h = d * sqrt(k), in [0.02, 0.40]) fixed in advance, scored as here; AP combined with a latitude band (C15 above 50 degrees), stated before the run; out-of-sample scoring of C03, AP and HR on 2025 sun once NASA POWER publishes the full year; a pyranometer or buoy shortwave record at a high-latitude point; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-17 · Milestone 6zzy: can the boat set its own clamp from the spread of its learned month ratios, and where does the fixed-clamp curve turn below ±5%? (predictions in `experiments/run_adaptive_clamp.py`'s docstring, committed in 98e5b008e before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zzx's first two "Next" items. No fixed half-width was within 5 cells of the best in every world (smallest worst gap C10, 23.8), and the all168 curve had to turn somewhere below 0.05 (h = 0 is AT_90, 139). Plan: 6zzx's raw month ratios r_m and level L on one learning year; d = mean |r_m - 1|. Fixed C01 to C04 (new), C05 and C15 (checks). Adaptive, chosen per learning year, nothing tuned on scores: AS (h = 0.05 if d < 0.10, else 0.30), AP (h = d, in [0.02, 0.40]), AL (h = 0.05 if |L - 1| < 0.05, else 0.30). The thresholds were read from 6zzx's published share-outside table, not from any score. Worlds and counts as 6zzx.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_adaptive_clamp.txt`, about 30 minutes on 4 cores for four worlds, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests in `tests/test_adaptive_clamp.py`): **eight of ten predictions held.** Checks passed: C05 and C15 reproduce 6zzx's all168 and hi168 in all four worlds; AT_90 139 / 126. Q1, Q2 held: the all168 curve turns at 0.03 (C01 148.0, C02 152.4, C03 152.6, C04 152.0, C05 151.4), a flat top from 0.02 to 0.05. Q3, Q4 held: AS keeps 150.2 on all168 (worst lag 136) and lifts cross hi168 to 57.4 (C05 22.2, C15 45.2). Q5 refuted: AS's worst gap to the best fixed h is -25.4 (reverse, 59.2 against C20's 84.6), against C10's -23.8. Q6 refuted: no adaptive rule is within 5 everywhere; AP comes closest, worst gap -13.8 (reverse), every other gap -4.8 to -11.8. Q7 held: AS sends 7.2% of nasa all-30 learning years wide, 56.9% in cross. Q8 held: AP all168 147.8. Q9 held: AL cross 52.6 against AS's 57.4. Q10 held: C01 scores 130.4 of 240 per point (C02 130.0), above no correction's 126, the first rule in this line to do so.
- Descriptive: AS's spread signal separates the self-consistent worlds from the mismatched ones (wide 7.2% nasa all30, 20.5% nasa nine, 23.5% era5, 56.9% cross, 56.2% reverse), but in reverse it is not enough: going wide at ±30% in only some years leaves AS 25 below the best fixed h. AP's mean h follows the same order (0.063, 0.082, 0.084, 0.120, 0.118). The narrow fixed clamps trade failure modes: C02's all168 failures are split between km and dark hours (lag 1 8/6), while every adaptive rule's are almost all dark hours (AS lag 5 0/32), so the adaptive rules give back some of the shrinkage on ordinary water. In cross every rule still fails on km only, except a few dark-hour cells at lag 4 (AS 8, AL 6).
- Reading: a boat can read from its own learned ratios how wide to clamp, and doing so removes most of the fixed clamp's dilemma: AP's worst gap to the best fixed h in any of the five settings is 13.8 cells, against 23.8 for the best fixed half-width, while giving up 4.8 cells on all30 (147.8 against C03's 152.6, still above no correction's 139). The switch (AS) is the better rule on all30 and cross but too coarse for the reverse world, where a brighter plan than sea needs a moderately wide clamp in most years, not ±5% in most and ±30% in a few. On all30 the fixed curve is flat from ±2% to ±5% (152.0 to 152.6); the narrowest clamps help most per point (C01 130.4 of 240) because a ±1% correction is almost no correction plus a nudge in the right direction, which is the shrinkage reading of 6zzx taken to its limit. C03's 152.6 and C01's 130.4 are the best of eleven fixed values on the scored years (a selection); AS, AP and AL were fixed in advance.
- Limits: in-sample on the 30 points and ten years every milestone since 6zzj scored; one adaptive form per signal, no sweep of the 0.10 / 0.05 thresholds or the AP mapping; one learning year; ERA5 stands in for the real sun; one boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model.
- Next: AP at two and three seasons against 6zzr's HR (does the spread signal sharpen with more log?); an AP variant with a floor of 0.10 when the level is off by more than 5% (the reverse world's case), fixed in advance; out-of-sample scoring of C03 and AP on 2025 sun once NASA POWER publishes the full year; a pyranometer or buoy shortwave record at a high-latitude point; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-17 · Milestone 6zzx: how wide should the clamp on the one-season learned month ratios be? (predictions in `experiments/run_clamp_sweep.py`'s docstring, committed in 20fef7290 before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zzw's first "Next". The clamp [0.85, 1.15] on the learned month ratios was set in 6zzj and never swept; 6zzw's one alternative ([0.70, 1.30]) was chosen after 6zzv saw the clamp bind, and scored only at the nine high-amplitude points. Plan: 6zzw's raw month ratios r_m on one learning year, clamped to [1 - h, 1 + h] for h in 0.05, 0.10, 0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30, 0.40 (C05 to C40; C15 is LC, C30 is LW), at five lags; the NASA world scored on all 30 points (all168, pp240, and hi168 / pp72 from the same cells), and era5, cross and reverse at the nine points as 6zzw.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_clamp_sweep.txt`, about 20 minutes on 4 cores for four worlds, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests in `tests/test_clamp_sweep.py`; figure `experiments/clamp_sweep.svg`): **six of ten predictions held.** Checks passed: C15 reproduces 6zzj's all168 (97, 117, 132, 152, 111) and 6zzw's LC hi168 in all four worlds, C30 reproduces 6zzw's LW, AT_90 139 / 126. K1, K2 held: on the 30-point pool the best half-width is the narrowest, and the mean falls from 0.10 upward (C05 151.4 [138, 160], C10 134.4, C15 121.8, C20 117.8, then 117.6 flat). K3 refuted: C05 clears no correction's 139 on the five-lag mean (151.4; worst lag 5 at 138). K4, K5 held: in cross hi168 rises monotonically with h (22.2, 38.6, 45.2, 50.2, 57.2, 61.6, 62.4). K6 held: in the NASA world at the nine points the clamp barely matters above 0.15 (81.0 to 81.4). K7 refuted: C05 keeps 35 of C15's 56-cell high-latitude lead (60.0 against 25). K8 held, but only as a ranking: C05 is best per point too (124.2 of 240), still below no correction's 126. K9 refuted by 0.2 (reverse C40 83.6, C15 83.8). K10 refuted: no single h is within 5 cells of the best in every world (smallest worst gap: C10, 23.8 cells).
- Descriptive: above h = 0.20 the NASA and ERA5 worlds stop changing (at most 2.1% of NASA real-shape ratios at all 30 points lie outside ±20%, 0.1% outside ±40%), so a "wider clamp" there is an unclamped correction; only the mismatched worlds keep moving (cross 6.7% outside ±30%). On all168 the narrow clamp removes dark-hour failures (C05 lags 1 to 5: 14, 20, 9, 8, 30 dark-only, against C15's 56, 45, 19, 10, 55), the same mechanism as 6zzt's shrinkage. In cross every failure stays on km at every width; in reverse C30 and C40 start failing lag 2 on dark hours (23) where C15 failed 5. Against the one-season rules of 6zzq's table, C05 has a higher mean than the band-pooled cosine HR on both pools (151.4 against 147.0 on all168; 60.0 against 50.2 on hi168), and a worst all168 lag one cell lower (138 against 140).
- Reading: the clamp is a shrinkage knob, and on ordinary water the right setting is tight. ±5% turns the raw one-season correction from the worst all-points rule of the line (121.8) into the first one-season rule to clear no correction's 139 on the pooled 30-point mean, while keeping 60% of the high-latitude lead. The cross world wants the opposite (wide), because there the learned season-shaped error is real and large, so no fixed clamp suits a boat that does not know whether its climatology matches its sea. That is the deployable reading: if the climatology and the logged sun come from the same product, clamp to about ±5%; if the boat sails under a different product (in practice, the real sun), widen it, which a deployed boat could decide from how far its learned ratios sit from 1. C05 is the best of a fixed seven-value sweep, so its 151.4 is a selection on the scored years, not an out-of-sample result; the monotone shape (K2) makes a narrow optimum unlikely to be an accident of one value.
- Limits: in-sample on the 30 points and ten years every milestone since 6zzj scored; seven half-widths, no value below 0.05 (the limit at h = 0 is AT_90, 139, so the curve must turn somewhere below 0.05); one learning year; ERA5 stands in for the real sun; per point C05 is still below no correction (124.2 against 126); one boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model.
- Next: an adaptive clamp fixed in advance (h set from the spread of the boat's own learned ratios, narrow when they sit near 1, wide when they do not), scored on all 30 points and the cross world; h below 0.05 (0.01 to 0.04) to locate the turn; C05 at two and three seasons against 6zzr's HR; a pyranometer or buoy shortwave record at a high-latitude point; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-16 · Milestone 6zzw: is the one-season learned correction a level correction or a seasonal one? (predictions in `experiments/run_level_season.py`'s docstring, committed in e7d629b82 before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zzv's first two "Next" items. 6zzv read LC's larger lead in the cross world (NASA plan, ERA5 sea) as a level correction, without testing it. Plan: 6zzv's code, cells and counts; at each lag one learning year gives raw month ratios r_m and a raw annual level L; rules LC (r_m in [0.85, 1.15], the check), LM (L alone, flat, same clamp), LS (r_m / L, the level-free shape, same clamp), LW (r_m in [0.70, 1.30]) and MW (L in [0.70, 1.30]); worlds nasa, era5, cross and a new "reverse" (ERA5 climatology frozen, NASA sun restored for 2005 to 2024).
- Outcome (`experiments/results_level_season.txt`, about 25 minutes on 4 cores for four worlds, stdlib, deterministic; 3 new tests in `tests/test_level_season.py`): **five of ten predictions held, and 6zzv's reading is refuted.** Checks passed: AT_90 and LC reproduce 6zzv's hi168 in nasa, era5 and cross. W1 and W2 refuted: in cross the level alone leads no correction by +2.2 (LM 5.2 against AT_90 3; LC +42.2); widened, by +23.2 (MW 26.2). W3 refuted: the level-free shape LS keeps all of LC's lead (45.4 against 45.2). W4 held: widening the clamp lifts LC in cross from 45.2 to 61.6. W5 held: it changes little in the self-consistent worlds (nasa +0.4, era5 +2.4). W6 held: in the NASA world LC 81.0 against LM 21.8 (and LM is below AT_90's 25). W7 held: reverse LC 83.8 against AT_90 18. W8 refuted: in reverse, no correction's 150 failures are still all on distance, none on dark hours. W9 held: 27.0% of learned reverse month ratios sit at 0.85 (era5 world 8.0%). W10 refuted: cross LM pp72 33.4 against AT_90's 35.
- Descriptive: the level is the wrong half in every world. LM never leads no correction by more than 17 (reverse 34.6 against 18) and in the NASA world trails it; LS tracks LC within 12 everywhere except reverse (71.2 against 83.8, where the level still adds). 12.2% (cross) and 11.1% (reverse) of learned real-shape levels lie outside [0.85, 1.15], none in the self-consistent worlds. In reverse the learned rules reach their highest counts of any world (LC_2 158 of 168), and the lag-2 learning year fails for the first time on dark hours (LW_2: 0 km, 23 dark). Per point the four worlds mostly move together; Gulf of Alaska and P+60 rise from 4 and 2 of 8 (cross) to 8 and 7 (reverse) for every rule.
- Reading: AT_90's 90-day running ratio already learns a level within the season; what it cannot follow is the season-shaped part of the error, and that part is what one logged year supplies. A product mismatch at these points is not a flat offset: ERA5's excess over NASA has a seasonal shape, which is why the learned correction gains in cross and why the level-free shape keeps the gain. The 6zzv mechanism ("any biased climatology gains from learning its own level") is wrong as stated; the clamp still matters, since it is the monthly swing (not the level) that hits it, and widening it is the first change in this line that lifts the cross world without costing the others. That widening was chosen after 6zzv saw the clamp bind, so it is a fixed-in-advance test of one alternative, not a tuned optimum. In reverse a too-bright plan still fails on distance, not dark hours: B plans on the same bright climatology, so "dominates B" compares two equally bold plans and the difference is in how fast each backs off.
- Limits: ERA5 stands in for the real sun; the nine high-amplitude points only; one learning year; [0.70, 1.30] is one alternative clamp, not a sweep; one boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model.
- Next: a clamp sweep (0.10 to 0.40 half-width) fixed in advance and scored on all 30 points, not only the nine; a pyranometer or buoy shortwave record at a high-latitude point as a third source; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-16 · Milestone 6zzv: when the boat plans with NASA POWER's climatology but sails, logs and is scored under ERA5 sunlight, does the learned seasonal correction's high-latitude lead grow or shrink? (predictions in `experiments/run_cross_source.py`'s docstring, committed in 90ef5731c before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zzu's first "Next". 6zzu tested a second self-consistent world (ERA5 everywhere); a deployed boat plans with a product and sails under the real sun, so the climatology and the sea come from different sources. ERA5 runs 2% to 22% above NASA at the nine points (6zzm), so this adds a level error the one-season month ratios can absorb up to the clamp [0.85, 1.15]. Plan: 6zzu's code, cells, rules (LC, DG, BG, OG, OM, OA, OL, AT_90), lags and counts (hi168, hi8, pp72); in the "cross" world fy._clim (plans, B and the 21 bias shapes) is frozen from NASA, then ERA5 replaces the sun for 2005 to 2024 at the nine points. 6zzu's two worlds are rerun as checks.
- Implementation fix after the first launch: a syntax error in the check printout stopped the script before any world ran (no number was produced); fixed, design and predictions unchanged.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_cross_source.txt`, under 15 minutes on 4 cores for three worlds, stdlib, deterministic; 3 new tests in `tests/test_cross_source.py`): **seven of eight predictions held.** Checks passed: NASA and ERA5 reproduce 6zzu's hi168 for AT_90, LC and DG at every lag. X1 held: LC mean hi168 45.2 against AT_90's 3. X2 held: LC's lead over no correction is +42.2, against +28.4 in the ERA5 world (NASA +56.0). X3 held: DG 67.4 > LC 45.2. X4 held: 23.7% of learned real-shape month ratios sit at 1.15 (ERA5 world 6.7%, NASA 4.2%); their mean is 1.044 (0.995, 0.977). X5 held: Spearman 0.90 between ERA5-world and cross LC_g (lag 2 best at 104, lag 3 worst at 0). X6 refuted: the replay says "short" more often in cross (0.337 against 0.304). X7 held, barely: LC mean pp72 35.4 against AT_90's 35. X8 held: OA 50.6 against OG 53.2.
- Descriptive: no correction collapses (hi168 25 NASA, 29 ERA5, 3 cross), and all 165 of its failures, like every failing cross cell of LC, DG, OG and OA, are on km alone: a plan built on a darker climatology than the sea makes the boat too cautious, and its 90-day running ratio does not catch up within the season. Every learned rule loses ground against the ERA5 world too (LC 57.4 -> 45.2, DG 75.6 -> 67.4, OG 68.2 -> 53.2), but less than no correction does. The orderings of 6zzu hold (DG > OG, OM, OA > LC > OL > AT_90 on the five-lag means). Per point the changes are small and mixed: P+60 falls to 2 of 8 for every rule; North Sea LC rises (lag 2 5 -> 6).
- Reading: a product mismatch acts as a level error, and the one-season correction is, among other things, a level correction. That is why its lead over no correction grows from +28 to +42 when the boat plans on one product and sails on another, while both lose against B. So part of the "high-latitude tool" reading of 6zzq to 6zzu is not seasonal: a deployed boat with any biased climatology would gain from learning its own level, and the clamp, binding on a quarter of months, is the first thing that limits it. X6's refutation fits the same mechanism: the replay compares the uncorrected plan with B, and a too-cautious plan falls short of B on km more often. The per-point real-shape count barely separates the rules (35 to 37 of 72), so this is a pooled-shape result.
- Limits: ERA5 stands in for the real sun, so "cross" measures a product-to-product mismatch, not a product-to-truth one; only the nine high-amplitude points; the climatology level and the sea level differ by a known, fairly stable offset here, which favours learned ratios; the clamp was not widened (a selection the cross world now invites); one boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model.
- Next: split level from season in the cross world (a learned annual-mean ratio alone, LM, against LC; and LC with the clamp widened to 1.30, both fixed before the run); the reverse mismatch (plan on ERA5, sail on NASA: a brighter plan than sea, the dark-hours side); a pyranometer or buoy shortwave record at a high-latitude point as a third source; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-16 · Milestone 6zzu: does the high-latitude case for a one-season seasonal correction survive a second irradiance source? (predictions in `experiments/run_era5_rules.py`'s docstring, committed in a16840487 before any configuration was run on ERA5)
- Why: 6zzs's and 6zzt's "Next" (open since 6zzq). Every count of the seasonal-correction line came from NASA POWER, and the design table's reading "one season of log is a high-latitude tool" rests on the nine high-amplitude points alone. 6zzm checked ERA5 against NASA's lag-3 irradiance but never ran a boat on it. Plan: ERA5 daily shortwave (6zzm's file, MJ/m2 / 3.6) replaces NASA at the nine points for every day 2005 to 2024, so climatology, learning year and scored year all come from ERA5; everything else as 6zzs and 6zzt; rules LC, DG, BG, OG, OM, OA, OL and AT_90 at all five lags; counts hi168, hi8 and pp72 (per point, real shape). The same code on NASA is the check.
- Implementation fix after the first launch: `ls.high_amp_points()` filled `fy._sun`'s cache with NASA values before the swap, so the first "ERA5" column repeated NASA cell for cell (no ERA5 number was produced). `use_era5` now clears every cache and the run checks the swapped series reached the boat; `tests/test_era5_rules.py` pins it.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_era5_rules.txt`, under 10 minutes on 4 cores for both sources, stdlib, deterministic; 5 new tests): **six of nine predictions held.** Check passed: NASA reproduces 6zzs/6zzt's hi168 exactly. E1 held: LC's mean hi168 on ERA5 is 57.4 against AT_90's 29 (NASA 81.0 against 25). E2 held: DG 75.6 > LC. E4 held: DG's worst lag 46. E5 held: LC 57.4 <= OG 68.2 <= DG 75.6. E6 held: OA 66.8 against OG 68.2. E7 held: the five lags rank identically for LC (Spearman 1.00; lag 2 best, 100, lag 3 worst, 16). E3 refuted: LC_3 is 16, not >= 30 (NASA 1). E8 refuted: the source moves the means by -23.6 (LC), -33.6 (DG), -17.6 (OG), -18.2 (OA). E9 refuted: on the per-point real-shape count LC averages 36.4 of 72 against AT_90's 38 (NASA 46.6 against 43).
- Descriptive: ERA5 lowers every learned rule's hi168 mean and lifts AT_90 (25 -> 29), so each rule's lead over no correction shrinks: LC from +56 to +28, DG from +84 to +47, OG from +61 to +39, OA from +60 to +38. Every LC cell lost on ERA5 is lost on km; DG loses a few on dark hours (lag 2 20, lag 4 12). On the per-point real-shape count only the fallback rules stay at or above no correction (OA 38.8, OL 38.2 against 38); LC, DG, OG and OM fall below it. The change is concentrated at three points: Gulf of Alaska (8 -> 4 of 8 for AT_90 and LC), P+60 (LC 7 -> 2 to 4) and P-30 (LC lags 2, 4, 5 from 6, 6, 4 to 2, 2, 2). The replay says "short" more often on ERA5 (0.304 against 0.196).
- Reading: the direction of the high-latitude result is not a NASA artefact. On the pooled count over the 21 bias shapes, a second irradiance source with a different radiation model keeps every ordering the paper uses (DG > the gated rules > LC > no correction, the same best and worst learning year), and lag 3 is the worst year in both. The size is source-dependent: about a third to a half of each rule's lead over no correction goes away, and on the real shape scored per point the learned corrections no longer beat no correction at all. So the design-table row "above about 50 degrees the learned correction wins more cases" should be read as a pooled-shape statement whose magnitude has an irradiance-source uncertainty of about 18 to 34 cells of 168, and the per-point claim does not survive. ERA5 is not ground truth either; a pyranometer record at one of the points would decide which magnitude is nearer.
- Limits: only the nine high-amplitude points (the other 21 stay NASA-only); ERA5 replaces NASA in the climatology too, so this tests "a different world", not a boat planning on one source and sailing on another; the pool was chosen with NASA amplitudes; one boat, payload, weights and calm-water daily model.
- Next: a cross-source run (plan on NASA's climatology, sail on ERA5's sun) at the nine points, the deployment case where the boat's climatology and the weather come from different products; a pyranometer or buoy shortwave record at a high-latitude point (e.g. an OOI or NDBC station with SW radiation) as a third source; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-16 · Milestone 6zzt: when the replay of the learning year says the uncorrected plan did fine, should the boat drop the learned seasonal correction altogether, and does a continuous gate on the replay's km margin beat the binary one? (predictions in `experiments/run_replay_fallback.py`'s docstring, committed in 6fe5f076f before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zzs's "Next" (a continuous gate on all 30 points). 6zzs's best deployable all-points rule, OM, scored 131.6 of 168, still below no correction (AT_90, 139), and 61% of all-30 replays said "ok" yet kept the raw ratios LC (121.8 alone). Plan: 6zzs's data, cells, counts, lags and replay side unchanged. OA = DG where the replay is short, no correction (ratios 1, the AT_90 result) otherwise; OL = LC where short, no correction otherwise (control); CS^s = ratios 1 + w (c_DG - 1) with w = min(1, max(0, -m / s)), m the replay's relative km margin against B, s in 1%, 3%, 10% (a fixed sweep; the best s is a selection, not a test).
- Implementation fix after the first launch (no result was read): m divides by B's km, which is 0 in some cells (B never leaves port); there AT_90 cannot be short, so m is set to 0 (w = 0), matching the replay side. Tested in `tests/test_replay_fallback.py`.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_replay_fallback.txt`, about 8 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): **three of seven predictions held.** Checks passed: LC, DG and AT_90 as 6zzp, OG 127.2 / 85.8 and OM 131.6 as 6zzs. R1, R2, R7 refuted: OA's mean all168 is 128.8 (worst lag 106), below both no correction's 139 and OM's 131.6. R3 held: OA's mean hi168 85.0, below OG's 85.8. R4 refuted: the control OL beats OA on all168 (135.0 against 128.8). R5, R6 held: CS rises monotonically with s, CS01 130.0, CS03 132.0, CS10 137.2 (worst lag 122).
- Descriptive: every all168 cell the fallback rules lose, they lose on dark hours (OA lag 1: 61 of 62); every hi168 cell OA loses at lags 3 to 5 it loses on km. On the per-point real-shape count (pp240) all five new rules beat no correction on average (OA 129.2, CS10 130.2, against AT_90's 126; LC 114.0, OM 115.8). At high amplitude the fallback raises the worst lag: OA_3 41 (LC_3 1, OG_3 18, AT_90 25). With s = 10%, 31% of short replays get a partial weight.
- Reading: on the 30-point pool, more correction is worse in every direction tried. Dropping the correction where the replay is fine recovers most of LC's cost, but what remains comes from the short replays: there DG makes the boat too bold (dark-hour failures), the raw ratios do better than DG (OL > OA), and shrinking DG toward 1 does better still, monotonically in s, approaching no correction's 139 from below without reaching it. So no one-season rule tested in 6zzp to 6zzt beats no correction on the pooled 30-point count, while the fallback rules are the first one-season rules to beat it on the per-point count and keep a high-latitude mean near OG's with a better worst year. For the paper's design table: one season of log is a high-latitude tool; elsewhere, use it only to shrink toward the climatology, or wait for three seasons (6zzr).
- Limits: in-sample on the 30 points and ten years every milestone since 6zzj scored; the s sweep is three values, and its monotone shape suggests the limit is no correction, not an interior optimum; one boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model and NASA POWER only.
- Next: the ERA5-driven harvest at the nine high-amplitude points (the outside source for a high-latitude guard, open since 6zzs); a pp240 versus all168 reconciliation (why per-point and pooled counts disagree on the fallback rules); the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-16 · Milestone 6zzs: does a dark-summer guard gated on the operating side the boat reads by replaying its own learning year keep 6zzp's high-amplitude gain without its cost on the other points? (predictions in `experiments/run_side_guard.py`'s docstring, committed in 31b84d44a before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zzp's and 6zzr's "Next". 6zzp's dark guard DG lifted the high-amplitude five-lag mean from 81.0 to 109.2 (worst lag 1 -> 74) but cost the 30-point pool (106.0 against LC's 121.8), losing every all168 cell on dark hours, so 6zzp read that a guard has to know which side of the km / dark-hours trade the boat sits on. A boat cannot see the scored years but can replay the one it logged. Plan: 6zzp's data, cells, counts and lags unchanged. Side per (pack, point, shape, learning year): the uncorrected AT_90 against B on the learning year itself (short if km below B, else dark if more excess dark hours, else ok); every learning year is also a scored year, so the replay is read from the same cells, and AT_90 there uses only the 2015 to 2024 climatology. Rules: OG = DG where short, LC otherwise; OM = DG where short, BG (the mirror, summer months min(c_m, 1)) where dark, LC where ok; BG alone as a control.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_side_guard.txt`, 6 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): **five of ten predictions held.** Checks passed: LC and DG reproduce 6zzp on all168 and hi168, AT_90 139 / 8 / 25 / 126. S2, S3 held: OG's mean all168 127.2, above LC's 121.8 and DG's 106.0. S7 held: OM 131.6 (OM_4 156). S8, S9 held: BG alone is no high-amplitude rule (26.0) and no all-points rescue (127.4). S1 refuted: OG's mean hi168 85.8, not >= 100 (DG 109.2). S4, S5 refuted: OG_3 hi168 18 (DG_3 74, LC_3 1). S6 refuted: 127.2 < 139. S10 refuted: the replay side matches the scored side in 64.5% of per-point real-shape cells (lags 61.8% to 67.7%), 80.6% at the high-amplitude points.
- Descriptive: replay shares short / dark / ok are 0.246 / 0.144 / 0.611 over all 30 points and 0.196 / 0.006 / 0.798 at the high-amplitude points. On all168 the gate removes almost all of DG's dark-hour failures (lag 1 79 -> 60, lag 3 32 -> 19, lag 5 99 -> 57) and OG_3 149 beats both LC_3 132 and DG_3 136; at hi168 OG's failures stay on km (lag 3 150 of 150), like LC's.
- Reading: the replay is a good gate for the cost and a poor one for the gain. Where DG hurt (ordinary water, the boat already bold), the learning year usually replays as fine or dark-heavy, so the gate leaves LC in place and the 30-point pool recovers past LC; that confirms 6zzp's mechanism with a rule fixed before the run. Where DG helped (high latitude, lag 3), 80% of replays say "ok": the uncorrected plan does fine on the year the boat logged, so the replay has nothing to say about whether the summer learned from that year is too dark for the years ahead. OG takes DG's ratios only where the replay says short, and at lag 3 that recovers 17 of DG_3's 73-cell gain over LC_3 (pooled counts, so not an exact per-cell attribution). The high-latitude failure is a forecast error about other years, which the one year in hand cannot reveal; a guard there needs information from outside the log (a second irradiance source, a longer climatology, or a neighbour's log), and the band pooling of 6zzr is the version of that already in the table. OM is the best one-season all-points rule with a deployable side test (131.6), still below no correction's 139 and 6zzp's blind early-bright tilt (133.6).
- Limits: one replay per learning year, scored by the same dominance test, so "short" is a coarse binary of a continuous margin; the side is read with B, which a deployed boat would also have to simulate on its log; the agreement figure has no chance baseline (side shares differ by pool); one boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model and NASA POWER only.
- Next: the ERA5-driven harvest at the nine high-amplitude points (now also the obvious outside source for a high-latitude guard); a continuous gate (shrink toward 1 by the replay's km margin) on all30 only, where the binary gate already helps; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-16 · Milestone 6zzr: with three, four or five seasons of log, do the shrinkage rules (LHF, LAP, LB, HR, HX) scored over every subset of learning years cross both floors before the raw month ratios LC do at four? (predictions in `experiments/run_many_year_rules.py`'s docstring, committed in b2b5085d9 before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zzq's first "Next". Its design table said only LC with four or five seasons never falls below no correction (AT_90: 139 of 168 on all 30 points, 25 of 168 at the nine high-amplitude points) on both pools, but the shrinkage rules had been scored over every subset only at one and two seasons, so the claim was "only LC among the rules run there". Plan: 6zzi to 6zzk's data, shapes, cells and rules unchanged; every lag set with three (10 sets), four (5) and five (1) years, every pool on the same years.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_many_year_rules.txt`, 40 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests, plus 1 in `test_history_length_table.py`): **all ten predictions held.** Check passed: LC reproduces 6zzi at k = 3, 4, 5 (149.3 [123, 160], hi 95.7 [50, 128]; 152.2 [143, 160], hi 99.8 [81, 107]; 158, hi 105). R1: HR's three-season mean 155.5 > 151.9. R2: HR's worst four-season set 153. R3: HR crosses both floors at k = 3 (worst 150 / 31). R4: at k = 4 (153 / 59). R5: at the high-amplitude points LC beats HR at k = 3 (95.7 against 56.7) and k = 4 (99.8 against 69.8). R6: HR 156.6 > LC 152.2 at k = 4. R7: harmonic means at k = 3 within 1.1 cells (155.4 to 156.5). R8: k = 3 spans LC 37, LHF 17, LAP 14, LB 12, HR 11, HX 11. R9: at k = 3 the six sets with lag 3 average below the four without at the high-amplitude points for every rule (HR 48.7 against 68.8). R10: HR, HX and LHF cross both floors at k = 3.
- Descriptive: fewest seasons that never fall below no correction on either pool, over every draw: LHF, HR, HX 3; LC, LAP, LB 4. LAP and LB miss at k = 3 only at the high-amplitude points (worst 21 and 23 against 25), LC only on all 30 points (worst 123). At four seasons every rule crosses both. On all 30 points the shrinkage rules lead LC by 6 to 7 cells at k = 3 and k = 4 (HR 155.5 / 156.6, LC 149.3 / 152.2) and by 2 to 4 at k = 5; at the high-amplitude points LC leads every shrinkage rule at every k (105 against 73 to 92 at k = 5), so the ordinary-water / high-latitude trade-off of 6zzj and 6zzk persists at every history length, it only narrows. LHF has the best three-season pair (156.5 [147, 164] and 83.0 [34, 112]). Per-point real-shape means at k = 3: LC 130.0, LHF 139.0, LAP 138.9, LB 140.7, HR 142.6, HX 142.8 (AT_90 126); pooled real-shape km / B stays at 1.07 to 1.08, below AT_90's 1.080, as at one and two seasons.
- Reading: the answer to 6zzq's open question is yes. A pooled cosine (HR, HX) or an unshrunk cosine (LHF) never falls below the uncorrected climatology on either pool from three seasons of log, a season before the raw month ratios, and it does so with a higher mean on ordinary water (155 to 157 against 149). What the extra season of shrinkage buys is a high-latitude floor: lag 3 still costs every rule about 20 to 34 high-amplitude cells at k = 3, but two other years now dilute it above 25. The paper section's third design rule changes from "four seasons of raw ratios" to "three seasons of a fitted cosine, pooled or not; four of anything".
- Limits: five learning years per half, so k = 3 has ten overlapping sets and k = 4 five, and the brackets are observed ranges, not intervals; the margins at the floor are thin (HR and HX worst 31 against 25 at k = 3, LHF 34), so another year or another climatology could move the crossing; all ten predictions held, which again says the priors were cautious; lag and calendar year stay tied; the swept shapes share a calendar-phase cosine that favours the harmonic rules (6zzg's caution); 6zzp's guards were not run at k >= 2; same boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model and NASA POWER only.
- Next: the ERA5-driven harvest at the nine high-amplitude points (does LC's high-latitude lead survive a second irradiance source?); a guard conditioned on the boat's operating side, fixed before the run; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-16 · Milestone 6zzq: the history-length paper section (6zz to 6zzp) with a design table of mean, worst and best counts per rule and amount of log
- Why: the paper section on history length has been in every "Next" since 6zzj, and 6zzj asked for per-rule year ranges as design intervals. Sixteen milestones of results sat only in this log. Plan: no new simulation; a stdlib script parses the "mean [min, max]" rows already written by 6zzi, 6zzj, 6zzk and 6zzp into one table, and the section is written from that table and these log entries. No predictions were registered, because nothing new is measured.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_history_length_table.txt`, `experiments/history_length_table.py`, under a second; 5 new tests that pin the parsed values to the numbers logged in 6zzi to 6zzp): the table reproduces the logged means (HR one season 147.0 / 50.2, DG 106.0 / 109.2, HX two seasons 152.1 / 56.0, LC by k 121.8 to 158.0). Against no correction (AT_90 139 / 25), the rules never below it in every draw are: on all168, one-season HR and HX and two-season LAP, LB, HR and HX; on hi168, one-season DG, DH and SZ and two-season LC. On both pools only LC with four or five seasons qualifies.
- Reading: that last line is new as a statement, though not as data. Every one- and two-season rule tested trades the 30-point floor against the high-latitude floor, and the only way across both is more years of raw log. The section (`paper/DRAFT.md` 2.7) gives a designer three rules: a band-pooled cosine after one season; the raw correction or dark-summer guard as candidates only above 50 degrees; and four seasons before trusting raw month ratios everywhere.
- Limits: a compilation, so it inherits every limit of 6zz to 6zzp (one boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model, NASA POWER only); the brackets are observed ranges over five or ten overlapping draws, not intervals; the shrinkage rules were never scored over all subsets at three or more seasons, so "only LC at k >= 4" means only among the rules run there.
- Next: score HR and HX over every subset at k = 3 and 4, which decides whether a pooled rule also crosses both floors with fewer seasons; a guard conditioned on the boat's operating side with the rule fixed before the run (6zzp's Next); the ERA5-driven harvest at the nine high-amplitude points; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-16 · Milestone 6zzp: does a deployable, asymmetric guard on the learned local summer (never let a one-year summer ratio fall below the climatology's) repair the bad learning years at the high-amplitude points without costing the rest, over all 30 points and the five single-year lags? And does 6zzo's early-bright tilt hold out of sample? (predictions in `experiments/run_dark_summer_guard.py`'s docstring, committed in 264af1f77 before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zzo's "Next". 6zzo found lag 3's collapse (LC_3 1 of 168 high-amplitude cells) carried by the cells where it learned a darker summer than the scored years saw, a split a boat cannot make because it needs the in-sample OC. The one reference a boat with one learned year has is the climatology (c_m = 1). Plan: 6zzj's data, shapes, cells and counts (all168, real8, hi168, pp240) for lags 1 to 5. Rules per lag: LC (raw), DG (summer months max(c_m, 1)), DH (summer months below 1 moved halfway to 1), SZ (summer months set to 1, the symmetric control), DA (every month max(c_m, 1)), TE (6zzo's summer tilt -0.03, level and curvature held, on every lag).
- Outcome (`experiments/results_dark_summer_guard.txt`, about 6 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): **eight of eleven predictions held.** Checks passed: LC all168 97 / 117 / 132 / 152 / 111, hi168 77 / 153 / 1 / 124 / 50, TE_2 166, AT_90 139 / 25 / 8 / 126. G1 held: DG_3 hi168 74 (from 1). G2 held: DG_2 152 (LC_2 153). G3 held: DG's five-lag mean hi168 109.2 against LC's 81.0. G4, G5 refuted: DG's mean all168 106.0, below LC's 121.8 and AT_90's 139. G6 held: DG 109.2 against the symmetric SZ's 52.8 on hi168. G7 held: LC_3 1 <= DH_3 30 <= DG_3 74. G8 held: the all-year guard DA is worse again on all168 (82.0). G9 refuted: TE beats LC on hi168 for 3 of 5 lags (lag 1 70 against 77, lag 4 105 against 124). G10 held: TE's mean all168 133.6 against LC's 121.8 (all five lags at or above LC; lag 4 158, the highest single-year count in 6zzj or here). G11 held: DG's worst lag on hi168 is 74 (LC's 1). The guard acts often: 42 to 55% of learned local-summer months are below 1, by lag.
- Post hoc (`experiments/run_dark_summer_guard_posthoc.py`, written after the run, no predictions): every one of DG's failing all168 cells fails on dark hours (lags 1 to 5: 79 / 63 / 32 / 37 / 99, none on km), against LC's mix (for example lag 1 15 km-only, 56 dark-only). On the 21 points outside the high-amplitude set DG's mean is 98.6 against LC's 120.0. Gating DG to |latitude| >= 50 (DGX, a cut chosen after seeing DG) keeps the 30-point pool (mean 123.2) but loses the high-amplitude gain entirely (DGX_3 hi168 2, mean 81.0): at the high-amplitude points DGX differs from DG only at P-30, the one high-amplitude point below 50 degrees, so lag 3's 1 -> 74 runs through guarding P-30's summer, and guarding the other eight alone is worth 1 cell.
- Reading: the asymmetry is real and has the predicted direction at high amplitude: refusing a darker-than-climatology summer is worth twice as much as refusing every summer correction (109 against 53), and it lifts the worst learning year from 1 to 74 without costing the best (152 of 153). But it is not a free guard. Off the high-amplitude points the same rule makes the boat too bold: every cell it loses, it loses on dark hours, which is 6zzn's mechanism (a brighter expected summer raises the battery target the boat spends against) running the other way. And the pooled high-amplitude gain is a threshold effect carried by one point's summer: the nine-point pool flips when P-30's darker learned summer is refused, not because the high-latitude summers each need the guard. So 6zzo's "erring bright is the cheaper mistake" holds only where the boat sits below the boldness edge, which is most of the high-amplitude pool and few of the other 21 points. The early-bright tilt, applied blind to every lag, is the better all-points rule (133.6 against 121.8 on all168 and above LC at every lag, though only lags 2 and 4 reach no correction's 139) but not a high-amplitude fix (lag 3 stays at 2). For a designer: a one-year correction should not be guarded by a single sign rule; the cheap mistake depends on which side of the km / dark-hours trade the boat already sits, so a guard has to look at that (for example the excess dark hours the uncorrected plan already carries), not only at the sign of the learned error.
- Limits: the guard's reference is the 2015 to 2024 climatology, which is itself a NASA POWER estimate; TE can leave the clamp range and is not re-clamped; the latitude gate and the P-30 attribution are post hoc and rest on pooled threshold counts, not per-point effects; "local summer" at the near-equator points is a calendar label; one boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model and NASA POWER only.
- Next: a guard conditioned on the boat's operating side (apply DG only where the uncorrected AT_90 plan loses on km, or shrink toward 1 by an amount set by the plan's own dark-hours margin), scored on all 30 points and lags with the rule fixed before the run; per-point dominance of DG against LC to replace the pooled attribution to P-30; TE on two-year pairs (6zzk) and ERA5; the paper section on history length (6zzb to 6zzp).

## 2026-11-16 · Milestone 6zzo: inside lag 3's learned local summer, does the level, the tilt (early against late) or the curvature (mid-summer against its edges) carry the high-amplitude collapse, and does lag 3's summer shape break the best year? (predictions in `experiments/run_summer_tilt.py`'s docstring, committed in bccbf3e1a before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zzn's "Next". 6zzl's summer repair (lag 3's three local-summer months replaced by the in-sample correction OC's) recovers 0.87 of the gap (LC_3 1 to R3_S 90 of 168), and 6zzn's uniform shifts showed a summer level change of the size separating lags 2 and 3 (0.4% on average) is worth about one cell, so the remaining suspect was the summer's shape. Plan: 6zzl and 6zzn's data, cells, clamp and scoring at the nine high-amplitude points. Each summer (c1, c2, c3, in seasonal order) is split into level L = mean, tilt T = (c3 - c1) / 2 and curvature K = c2 - (c1 + c3) / 2 and rebuilt exactly from them; rules swap components per (point, half, shape) cell: lag 3 with OC's L, T, K, or T and K (R3_L, R3_T, R3_K, R3_TK); lag 2 with lag 3's T, K, or T and K (G2_T3, G2_K3, G2_TK3); lag 3 with lag 2's T and K (G3_TK2); lag 2 with its tilt forced by -0.03 to +0.03 (G2_t). Read before scoring (ratios only): lag 3's mean tilt error against OC is +0.009 (late summer learned brighter), lag 2's -0.008; by mean |error| lag 3's summer shape is not worse than lag 2's (curvature 0.064 against 0.091).
- Outcome (`experiments/results_summer_tilt.txt`, 57 s on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): **two of nine predictions held, and the shape reading is refuted.** Checks passed: AT_90 25, LC_2 153, LC_3 1, OC 103, R3_S 90. T1 refuted: the level repair alone wins 49 (recovery 0.54), not <= 10. T2 refuted: the shape repair (tilt and curvature) wins 4 (0.03). T3, T4 refuted: tilt 10, curvature 10 (0.10 each). T5, T6 refuted: lag 3's summer shape costs lag 2 only 18 cells (G2_TK3 135; tilt alone 130; curvature alone 157). T7 refuted: lag 2's shape lifts lag 3 to 35 (0.38), not >= 52. T8 held: forcing lag 2's summer early-bright helps and late-bright hurts, monotonically (-0.03 166, -0.01 156, 0 153, +0.01 150, +0.03 148). T9 held: all 33 of G2_TK3's failures are on km.
- Post hoc (`experiments/run_summer_tilt_posthoc.py`, written after the run, no predictions): lag 3's summer level is brighter than OC's in 219 of the 378 (point, half, shape) cells and darker in 159. Repairing the level only where it is too bright (darkening it) wins 1, nothing; only where it is too dark (brightening it) wins 49, all of R3_L; that plus OC's tilt and curvature wins 90, all of R3_S.
- Reading: lag 3's collapse is carried by the cells where it learned a darker summer than the scored years saw, a level error of a few percent per point and half that averages out to +0.0035 over the nine points because a larger number of cells err bright, where erring bright costs nothing at this edge (6zzn: brighter makes the boat bolder, and lag 3 sits below the boldness edge). So 6zzn's "level cannot be the cause" holds only for a uniform shift: a level error that differs in sign by point is half the damage, and the shape matters only on top of the right level (shape alone 0.03, level alone 0.54, together 0.87: the parts interact, they do not add). 6zzl's exploratory "bad years learned a brighter summer" was an average over cells of both signs and pointed the wrong way. Lag 2, given lag 3's shape, keeps 135: the best year is good because its level is right where it matters, not because its shape is. The one shape lever with a clear direction is tilt: an early-bright summer makes lag 2 bolder in early summer and wins 166 of 168, more than any rule scored on these cells in 6zzl, 6zzn or here, the in-sample OC (103) included. For a designer at high latitude: a correction learned from one year should be checked for summers it learned darker than recent ones, point by point; erring bright is the cheaper mistake, and if anything, lean the summer expectation toward its early months.
- Limits: the components are unweighted (each summer month counts equally, not by energy); OC is an in-sample five-year mean, not a truth; components are swapped after the clamp and not re-clamped; the early-bright gain of G2_t-0.03 is one forced direction on one year, not a learned or validated rule, and it is scored on the cells it was chosen on; the post-hoc split is by the sign of an in-sample comparison a deployed boat cannot make; one boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model and NASA POWER only.
- Next: a deployable guard for the dark-summer error (shrink a learned summer level toward the climatology only when it is darker, an asymmetric rule, scored on all 30 points and all lags); test the early-bright tilt as a rule out of sample (other lags, the all-points 168, ERA5); score 6zzi to 6zzk's rules as a km against dark-hours frontier; the paper section on history length (6zzb to 6zzo).

## 2026-11-16 · Milestone 6zzn: does a brighter learned local summer, on its own, make the seasonal correction fail at the high-amplitude points? (forced-shift test of 6zzl's summer-sign reading; predictions in `experiments/run_summer_shift.py`'s docstring, committed in bdd4ddc82 before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zzl and 6zzm's "Next". 6zzl read the lag-3 collapse (1 of 168 high-amplitude cells, all lost on km) as a local-summer shape that errs bright, making the AT ratio read an ordinary summer as a dark spell so the boat holds back; 6zzm found the between-year summer differences that would carry that reading (about 0.01) are as large as the NASA / ERA5 disagreement, so only a forced shift can test it. Plan: 6zzl's data, cells, clamp and scoring at the nine high-amplitude points; the learned monthly ratios of lags 2, 3 and 4 with their local-summer months multiplied by (1 + d), d = -3 to +3% (lag 2 and 3: nine steps; lag 4: five), and lag 2's local winter shifted +/-3% as a control. For scale, lag 3's local-summer mean error is +0.0023 and lag 2's -0.0021 (6zzl), a gap of about 0.4%.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_summer_shift.txt`, 94 s on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): **one of eight predictions held, and the reading under test is refuted in sign.** Check passed: d = 0 reproduces 6zzi's 153 / 1 / 124. F1 refuted in the strongest way: lag 2's real-shape pooled km/B rises with d in all 8 half x pack series (for example E 10 kWh 1.461 at -3% to 1.794 at +3%), with excess dark hours rising alongside (0.71 to 0.97 of B's). F2 held: lag 2's hi168 falls 153 / 152 / 152 / 148 / 142 from 0 to +3%, but every added loss is on dark hours (14 to 26 dark-only failures), not km. F3 refuted (+1%: 152, not <= 102). F4 refuted (+3%: 142, not <= 25). F5 refuted: darkening lag 3's summer makes it worse (-1%: 0), brightening helps (+1% 4, +2% 10, +3% 32). F6 refuted (winter +3% 155, summer +3% 142). F7 refuted: lag 2 darkened 3% fails 40 cells, all on km. F8 refuted: lag 4 gains as its summer brightens (-3% 63, -1% 105, 0 124, +1% 126, +3% 146).
- Post hoc (`experiments/run_summer_shift_posthoc.py`, written after the run, no predictions): lag 3 needs its local summer brightened about 10% to reach lag 4's count (+4% 57, +5% 70, +6% 80, +8% 100, +10% 124), and every remaining failure stays on km; brightening lag 3's whole year works less well than its summer alone (+10%: 91 against 124). Lag 2 brightened all year +3%: 140, all 28 failures on dark hours; darkened all year -3%: 125, all 43 on km.
- Reading: a brighter learned summer makes the boat bolder, not more cautious: in this model the corrected climatology sets the battery target, and a higher expected summer lets the boat spend more, so km and dark hours both rise with d. The high-amplitude count is then a window on boldness: lag 2 sits near its top edge (dark hours start failing above +1%), lag 4 and lag 3 sit below it and lose on km. 6zzl's exploratory summer-sign link (rho -0.60 over five lags, bad years learned a brighter summer) therefore cannot be causal through the summer level: by the forced shift, a brighter summer would have helped lag 3. And level alone cannot be the cause either: the 0.4% summer difference between lags 2 and 3 is worth about one cell, while closing lag 3's gap takes about 10%, some 25 times the observed difference. What 6zzl's summer repair (0.87 of the gap) fixed is the month-to-month shape inside and around local summer, not its mean. For a designer: if a learned correction leaves a high-latitude boat short on distance with dark hours to spare, nudging the summer expectation up is the cheap fix (a few percent buys cells at lag 4, at lag 2 it starts costing dark hours above +1%); a correction should be judged by where it lands between the two failure modes, not by how close its mean summer is to the truth.
- Limits: the shift is applied after the clamp and not re-clamped; local seasons are fixed three-month blocks, interpolated to days, so a summer shift also moves the edge half-months of spring and autumn; one boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model and NASA POWER only; the post-hoc extension and the whole-year rows are descriptive; the eight priors leaned on 6zzl's post-hoc reading, so seven refutations say that reading was wrong, not that the test was weak.
- Next: decompose lag 3's summer error into the month-to-month pattern inside summer (for example a tilt between early and late summer, with level held) and force-shift that pattern on lag 2, the test this result now points to; score 6zzi to 6zzk's rules as a km against dark-hours frontier rather than a dominance count; drive the rules with ERA5 harvest (6zzm's "Next"); the paper section on history length (6zzb to 6zzn).

## 2026-11-15 · Milestone 6zzm: is the lag-3 learning year (2007 / 2012) that wipes out every correction at the high-amplitude points real weather, or an artefact of NASA POWER? (predictions in `experiments/run_lag3_era5.py`'s docstring, committed in the PR's second commit before any comparison was computed)
- Why: 6zzj and 6zzk's "Next" asked to check lag 3 "against ERA5 or buoy cloud records". At the nine high-amplitude points every rule learned on lag 3 wins almost nothing (6zzj: LC_3 1, HR_3 9 of 168; AT_90 25; LC_2 153), and every number of the seasonal-correction line comes from one satellite product. The sibling milestone 6zzl (PR #16349) takes the failure apart by month, season, level and shape inside NASA POWER; this run first planned the same month repair, found the overlap at claim time, and narrowed to the independent-source question. Plan: ERA5 shortwave_radiation_sum (Open-Meteo, `experiments/fetch_era5_sun.py`, CC BY 4.0) at the same nine coordinates, 2005 to 2024; per source, month anomalies against its own 2015 to 2024 month means; the lag-g error e = anomaly of the learning year minus the scored half's mean anomaly, per point, half and month; energy weights from each source's own climatology.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_lag3_era5.txt`, stdlib, seconds; 4 new tests): **four of five predictions held.** M1 held: NASA and ERA5 month anomalies of 2007 and 2012 correlate r = +0.711 over 9 points x 12 months x 2 years. M2 held: the lag-3 errors themselves correlate r = +0.765 (216 point-half-months). M3 refuted: ERA5's energy-weighted mean |e| is largest at lag 5 (0.0822), not lag 3 (0.0789, second), and NASA's too is largest at lag 5 (0.0690; lag 3 0.0679, third). M4 held: the lag-3 years' anomaly r (mean +0.711) is within 0.03 of the other eight years' (+0.737); the per-year range is +0.604 (2009) to +0.796 (2006). M5 held: where NASA's lag-3 error is at least 0.05 in a month carrying at least 1/24 of the year's energy, ERA5 has the same sign in 69 of 74 cases.
- Descriptive: the five lags' energy-weighted mean |e| lie within 0.004 of each other in NASA (0.0651 to 0.0690) and within 0.013 in ERA5 (0.0697 to 0.0822), while their high-amplitude LC counts (6zzi) are 77 / 153 / 1 / 124 / 50: the size of a year's departure does not order the outcome (lag 5 departs most in both sources and wins 50; lag 4 departs more than lag 3 in NASA and wins 124). Post hoc (added after scoring, not a test): over the dark months alone (w_m < 1/24, 68 point-half-months per lag) the order is the same kind of mixed, NASA 0.089 / 0.068 / 0.099 / 0.107 / 0.086 and ERA5 0.093 / 0.077 / 0.095 / 0.111 / 0.095 for lags 1 to 5. Per point the lag-3 error correlation runs from +0.36 (P-60, the Southern Ocean point, where ERA5 departs about twice as much as NASA) to +0.92 (P+60). Pooled by month, both sources put the lag-3 year's largest shared departures in May (NASA -0.025, ERA5 -0.030) and November (-0.014, -0.027), with October, February and December disagreeing in size or sign. ERA5 is within -2% to +8% of NASA at eight points and 22% brighter at P+60 (2015 to 2024 annual level), which the anomaly method removes.
- Reading: the lag-3 year is real weather, recorded the same way by a satellite retrieval and an independent reanalysis, about as consistently as any of the ten years. It is not an outlier: by the size of its month departures from the scored half it sits in the middle of the five learning years in both sources. So the 1-of-168 collapse is not a data artefact that a second source would have fixed, and it cannot be screened out by asking "was the learning year unusually far from normal"; it comes from the particular pattern of an ordinary year (which months, with which sign) meeting the battery and the rule, which is what 6zzl's month and season repairs measure (it found a local-summer shape; see the post-hoc bullet). For the paper: the year-to-year spread of the learned corrections (6zzi to 6zzk) is a property of the weather and would carry over to ERA5-driven planning; for a designer, the guard against a bad learning year at high latitude has to be more history or shrinkage (6zzj, 6zzk), not a magnitude check on the year.
- Limits: irradiance only, not the boat: ERA5's anomalies were not run through the harvest model and the rules, so "ERA5 would have taught the same wrong lesson" rests on M2 and M5, not on a dominance count; ERA5 is a reanalysis that assimilates no surface radiation, so the agreement is between two models of cloud, not with a pyranometer (a buoy shortwave record at the North Sea or Gulf of Alaska points would be the next check); nine points and one 0.25 degree cell each; the magnitude comparison is one statistic (weighted mean |e|), and a pattern measure (for example the error's projection on the rule's response) was not tried.
- Post hoc, after 6zzl (PR #16349) landed during this run (not a test; appended to the same script): 6zzl's summer-sign reading says the two worst learning years (lags 3 and 5) are the only two that learned a brighter local summer than the scored years saw. On irradiance, the energy-weighted local-summer mean e by lag 1 to 5 is NASA -0.0031 / -0.0002 / +0.0090 / -0.0102 / +0.0044 and ERA5 -0.0245 / +0.0123 / +0.0056 / -0.0106 / +0.0172, against hi168 LC 77 / 153 / 1 / 124 / 50. NASA reproduces the reading (lag 3 the brightest summer, rho -0.70); ERA5 does not (the best year, lag 2, also learned a brighter summer, +0.012; rho -0.10). Two-sided permutation p for |rho| >= 0.7 with five lags is 0.23. So the shared month-by-month weather is real (M1, M2, M5), but the summer-brightness differences that would separate good from bad learning years (about 0.01) are the size of the disagreement between sources; the summer-sign mechanism needs 6zzl's planned forced-shift test, not a second data source, to stand.
