- Next: run 6zzl's pre-registered summer-shift test (shift a good year's local-summer ratios by +/- 1 to 3% and predict the km loss), which settles the mechanism without relying on a 1% inter-source difference; drive the rules with ERA5 harvest at the nine points (a full dominance count on the second source); a buoy shortwave check (NDBC or OceanSITES stations with radiation sensors near the high-latitude points); the paper section on history length (6zzb to 6zzm).

## 2026-11-15 · Milestone 6zzl: why does the lag-3 learning year (2007 for L, 2012 for E) wipe out the high-amplitude points, and do months, level or shape carry the damage? (predictions in `experiments/run_lag3_diagnosis.py`'s docstring, committed in c7e975186 before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zzi to 6zzk's "Next". Learned from lag 3 alone, the raw month ratios LC win 1 of 168 high-amplitude cells (lag 2: 153; no correction AT_90: 25), every shrinkage rule collapses there too (6zzj), and lag 3 halves the two-year counts (6zzk); over all 30 points it is a middling year (132). Plan: 6zzi's data, shapes, cells and rules at the nine high-amplitude points. Rules: LC_1 to LC_5; OC, the correction learned on the scored half itself (an in-sample ceiling for a five-year mean, not deployable); R3_m, lag 3 with month m replaced by OC's; R3_s, lag 3 with one local season replaced (seasons flipped south of the equator); LV3, lag 3's shape at OC's energy-weighted level; SH3, OC's shape at lag 3's level. Gap = OC - LC_3; recovery = (variant - LC_3) / gap.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_lag3_diagnosis.txt`, 72 s on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): **two of seven predictions held.** Check passed: LC_1 to LC_5 reproduce 6zzi's 77 / 153 / 1 / 124 / 50. D1 refuted: OC wins 103 of 168, below lag 2 (153) and lag 4 (124). D2 refuted: lag 3's energy-weighted mean |c_m - c*_m| is 0.0617, tied smallest with lag 1 (lags 2, 4, 5: 0.0633, 0.0636, 0.0659). D3 refuted: all 167 of LC_3's failing cells fail on km alone, none on dark hours. D4 held: repairing local summer recovers 0.87 of the gap (90). D5 refuted: summer, not winter (winter repair: 1). D6 held: the best single month (February) recovers 0.49 (51; January 44; every other month 0 to 2). D7 refuted: the shape repair SH3 wins 87, the level repair LV3 9.
- Descriptive: on the real shape pooled over the nine points LC_3 runs 0.64 to 0.93 of B's km with 0.05 to 0.36 of its excess dark hours, the most cautious of the rules shown (LC_2: 1.04 to 1.65 km/B at 0.58 to 1.09 ex/B; OC 0.90 to 1.22 at 0.33 to 0.62). Per point on the real shape the counts barely differ (LC_3 42, LC_2 48, OC 49, AT_90 43 of 72). The single-month repairs are not additive: the twelve sum to far less than the seasonal ones, because the pooled dominance test is a threshold. Exploratory, written after the run (`experiments/run_lag3_summer_sign.py`, no predictions): the local-summer signed error by lag is -0.0031 / -0.0021 / +0.0023 / -0.0106 / +0.0057; the two worst lags (3 and 5) are the only two that learned a brighter summer than the scored years saw, and rho(hi168, summer error) = -0.60 over five lags.
- Reading: the lag-3 year is not a bad estimate of the seasonal error; it is an estimate whose local-summer shape errs on the cautious side, and at high latitude, where summer carries most of the energy, that makes the adaptive rule hold back (a plausible path: an over-bright summer expectation makes the AT ratio read an ordinary summer as a dark spell). The dominance test then fails it on distance alone. Two things follow for the paper. First, the high-amplitude 168-cell count is not a measure of correction accuracy: the in-sample correction scores 103 and a year with no smaller error scores 153, so 6zzi to 6zzk's reading that at high latitude "the raw month ratios keep a real seasonal error the pooled rules shrink away" (92 against 54) should be restated as a km against dark-hours trade, which those milestones did not separate. Second, "check against the lag-3 kind of year" becomes concrete: a year whose learned local-summer ratios run brighter than recent summers. For a designer at high latitude: judge a learned correction on distance and dark hours separately, and distrust one learned from a year with an unusually bright summer.
- Limits: OC is an in-sample five-year mean, not a per-year truth, so "error" is distance from that mean; the summer-sign link is five lags, post hoc, not a test; months are interpolated, so a month repair also moves its neighbours' half-months; LV3 can leave the clamp range (by design, it rescales after clamping); only LC was diagnosed, not the shrinkage rules; same boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model and NASA POWER only.
- Next: score the high-amplitude cells on km and dark hours separately (km at matched dark hours, or the share of cells on B's Pareto side) for 6zzi to 6zzk's rules; pre-register the summer-sign mechanism as a test (shift a good year's local-summer ratios by +/- 1 to 3% and predict the km loss); write 6zzb to 6zzl as a paper section; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-15 · Milestone 6zzk: do the two-year rules of 6zzb to 6zzh beat no correction when the two learning years are drawn from all ten pairs, not only the pair next to the scored half? (predictions in `experiments/run_pair_year_rules.py`'s docstring, committed in fa45b3586 before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zzj's "Next" (a two-year band pool averaged over its ten pairs). 6zzi found the nearest pair (lags 1 and 2) the worst of ten for the raw ratios LC (116 of 168 against a mean of 140.8), and 6zzj found lag 1 the worst single year for every shrinkage rule, so the two-year counts of 6zzd to 6zzh (LHF_2 140, LAP_2 143, LB_2 143, HR_2 149, HX_2 149) were one draw that contained the worst year. Two seasons is the history a designer most often has before committing to a correction. Plan: 6zzj's data, shapes, cells and rules unchanged, each learned on the two years of a lag pair {a, b} (ten pairs; L learns from 2010 - a and 2010 - b, E from 2009 + a and 2009 + b), every pool on the same two years.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_pair_year_rules.txt`, about 25 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): **all ten predictions held.** Checks passed: LC on the ten pairs reproduces 6zzi (nearest 116, mean 140.8, max 155) and the nearest pair reproduces 6zzd to 6zzh (LHF 140, LAP 143, LB 143, HR 149, HX 149; AT_90 139). K1: every harmonic rule's ten-pair mean is above 139 (LHF 151.2, LAP 151.9, LB 152.0, HR 151.9, HX 152.1). K2: HR 151.9 > its one-year 147.0. K3: HR's worst pair 145. K4: 151.9 >= 149. K5: the nearest pair ranks 8 of 10 for HR. K6: spans LC 39, LHF 26, LAP 19, LB 17, HR 14, HX 14. K7: HX has the highest mean (152.1). K8: at the high-amplitude points LC 92.3 against HR 54.0. K9: for every rule the four pairs with lag 3 average below the six without at the high-amplitude points. K10: HR 140.2 of 240 per-point real-shape cells (AT_90 126).
- Descriptive: going from one to two years of log adds 19.0 cells to LC, 12.4 to LHF, 9.7 to LAP, 8.6 to LB, 4.9 to HR and 5.5 to HX, so the five harmonic rules converge to within 0.9 cells (151.2 to 152.1) and differ now only in spread: worst pair LHF 137 (the only harmonic rule with a pair below no correction, pair {1,5}), LB 142, LAP 143, HR and HX 145. Every rule wins all 8 pooled real-shape cells on every pair (LC 7 to 8). Per-point real-shape means LC 125.4, LHF 137.1, LAP 137.7, LB 139.3, HR 140.2, HX 140.5 (AT_90 126). At the nine high-amplitude points the raw ratios stay best (LC 92.3 [33, 149], LHF 76.6 [9, 132], pooled rules 54 to 59; AT_90 25), and the nearest pair, the worst over all cells, ranks in the top three of ten there for every harmonic rule (first for LHF at 132 and HR at 80): the year that hurts on ordinary water is the one that helps at high latitude. Lag 3 (2007 for L, 2012 for E) roughly halves every rule's high-amplitude count (LC 59.0 with it against 114.5 without; HR 40.2 against 63.2); a second year dilutes but does not cancel it. Pooled real-shape km / B ranges 1.053 to 1.087 over rules and pairs, mean about 1.07, below AT_90's 1.080: as at one year, the rules win by cutting dark hours.
- Reading: with two seasons of log the choice of shrinkage rule stops mattering for the average (all five harmonic rules at 151 to 152 of 168, 12 to 13 above no correction), and what is left to choose on is the worst case: the band pools (HR, HX) never fall below 145 over ten pairs, the unshrunk cosine can fall to 137. Since one season of band-pooled log already scores 147.0 (6zzj), the second season buys about 5 cells of the 13-cell gain over no correction, and lifts the worst case from 140 to 145. The high-latitude exception persists: there the raw month ratios keep a real seasonal error the pooled rules shrink away (92 against 54), at the price of a 33 to 149 swing that one year (lag 3) drives. For a designer: after two seasons any harmonic rule beats the plain climatology on average; prefer the band pool for its floor; above about 50 degrees the raw correction pays off on average but needs a third year, or a check against the lag-3 kind of year, before it is trusted.
- Limits: ten pairs share years (each year is in four pairs), so the spread is not ten independent draws and no interval is given; the same pair is used for both halves, so lag and calendar year are tied; all ten predictions held, which says the priors (50 to 80%) were cautious after 6zzi and 6zzj, not that the test was strong; the swept shapes favour calendar-phase pools (6zzg's caution); RR and RB of 6zzh were not rerun; same boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model and NASA POWER only.
- Next: write 6zzb to 6zzk as a paper section (rule x history-length table with year ranges as design intervals); diagnose lag 3 at high latitude (which months of 2007 and 2012 the ratios get wrong, against ERA5 or buoy cloud records); learn on the fly within a first deployment; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-15 · Milestone 6zzj: do the one-year shrinkage rules of 6zze to 6zzh still match or beat no correction when the one learning year is drawn from all five years, not only the year next to the scored half? (predictions in `experiments/run_single_year_rules.py`'s docstring, committed in b124a732a before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zzi's first "Next". 6zzi found lag 1 (the only year 6zzc to 6zzh learned from) was the worst single year for the raw correction LC (97 of 168, five-year mean 121.8), so every one-year verdict against no correction (AT_90 139) rested on one bad draw. Plan: 6zzi's data, shapes, cells and lags; LC, LHF (6zzd), LAP (6zze), LB (6zzf), HR (6zzg) and HX (6zzh) unchanged, each learned on one year at lag g = 1 to 5, with fleet and band pools on the same single year at every point.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_single_year_rules.txt`, about 11 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): **seven of nine predictions held.** Checks passed: LC by lag 97 / 117 / 132 / 152 / 111 reproduce 6zzi, and at lag 1 LHF 126, LAP 132, LB 133, HR 140, HX 140 reproduce 6zzd to 6zzh. J1 held: HX five-lag mean 146.6 of 168. J2 held: HR 147.0. J3 held: spans LC 55, LHF 29, LAP 21, LB 19, HR 13, HX 13. J4 held: lag 1 is the worst lag for HX and HR (rank 5 of 5). J5 held: HX's worst lag 140. J6 refuted: at the high-amplitude points HX averages 51.8 against LC's 81.0. J7 refuted: HR 147.0 edges HX 146.6. J8 held: rho(HX, LC) +0.50. J9 held: every harmonic rule's mean is above LC's 121.8 (LHF 138.8, LAP 142.2, LB 143.4).
- Descriptive: lag 1 is the worst of the five years over 168 cells for LC, LHF, LB, HR and HX and tied worst for LAP (132, with lag 5). The per-lag counts of the rules that shrink toward zero track LC's year order (rho +1.00 / +0.95 / +0.90 for LHF / LAP / LB); the band-mean pools less so (+0.50), and HR and HX never drop below 140. On the 8 real-shape pooled cells every harmonic rule wins 7 or 8 in every year (LC 4 to 8); on the 240 per-point real-shape cells the five-lag means are LC 114.0, LHF 128.8, LAP 131.2, LB 133.2, HR 133.6, HX 133.8 against AT_90's 126. At the nine high-amplitude points the order reverses: LC 81.0 [1, 153], LHF 74.2 [1, 140], LAP 54.4, LB 49.4, HR 50.2, HX 51.8 (AT_90 25), and lag 3 (2007 for L, 2012 for E) is a near-total loss there for every rule (1 to 9 of 168), while lag 2 is the best (LC 153, LHF 140). Pooled real-shape km / B ranges 1.040 to 1.080 over rules and lags, all below AT_90's 1.080: the rules dominate B by cutting dark hours, and the corrected targets travel slightly less than the uncorrected one.
- Reading: the earlier verdict that one season of log cannot beat no correction (6zze to 6zzh: 126 to 140 of 168 against 139) was an artefact of always learning from the worst year. Drawn fairly, one season pooled within latitude bands beats no correction on average by 8 cells of 168 and never loses to it in any of the five years; shrinking toward zero helps less (+3 to +4), and the unshrunk cosine only ties (138.8). The pooling buys stability, not luck: the year-to-year swing falls from 55 cells (LC) to 13 (HR, HX), and HR's year order decouples from LC's. The cost is at high latitude, where the raw ratios and the unshrunk cosine keep more of a real, large seasonal error on average (81.0 and 74.2 against about 50), but swing from 1 to 153 with the year. For a designer: after one season, pool the fitted cosine within latitude bands (HR); keep a high-latitude boat's raw correction only with a second season to confirm it, since a single unlucky year there can undo nearly every case. This closes the one-year shrinkage line with a result the paper can state: on ordinary water one season of band-pooled log is enough.
- Limits: five years per half, so five draws per rule and the means carry the year-to-year spread shown, not an interval; the same lag is used for both halves, so lag and calendar year are tied; HX's 50-degree threshold is the grid's band edge; the swept shapes share a calendar-phase cosine, which favours HR and HX (6zzg's caution); RR and RB of 6zzh were not rerun; same boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model and NASA POWER only.
- Next: write the one-year and history-length results (6zzb to 6zzj) as a paper section with per-rule year ranges as design intervals; why lag 3 fails at high latitude (which month the 2007 / 2012 ratios get wrong); a two-year band pool averaged over its ten pairs; learn on the fly within a first deployment; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-15 · Milestone 6zzi: averaged over every choice of k learning years, how does the learned seasonal correction depend on history length, and does recency matter? (predictions in `experiments/run_subset_years.py`'s docstring, committed in 047aecb77 before any configuration was run)
- Why: every milestone from 6zzb to 6zzh listed "the k and recency confound of 6zzb" in its limits. 6zzb learned c_m from the k years of the other half nearest the scored half, so k = 1 meant one particular year and more years also meant older years; its high-amplitude counts peaked at k = 2 (77 / 138 / 104 / 105). Plan: 6zzb's data, shapes, cells, clamp and rule LC unchanged; learning years named by a lag set S in {1..5} (scoring L learns from 2010 - lag, scoring E from 2009 + lag), every S with |S| = 1 to 5 scored (31 sets, the same S for both halves); per k the mean, min and max over its subsets; recency as the Spearman rho of count against lag over the five single years, and within k over the 30 sets with k = 1 to 4.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_subset_years.txt`, about 14 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): **five of eight predictions held.** Check passed: the nearest sets {1..k} reproduce 6zzb's LC_k 97 / 116 / 142 / 158 exactly. S1 held: the subset-mean 168-cell count rises with k, 121.8 / 140.8 / 149.3 / 152.2 / 158.0 for k = 1 to 5 (AT_90 139). S2 held (121.8 < 139). S3 held (149.3). S4 held: at the nine high-amplitude points 81.0 / 92.3 / 95.7 / 99.8 / 105.0, so 6zzb's k = 2 peak was the draw of years. S5 held: the five single years span 97 to 152 of 168. S6 refuted: rho +0.40 over the five single years (lag 1 to 5: 97 / 117 / 132 / 152 / 111). S7 refuted: within-k rho +0.33. S8 refuted: 6zzb's nearest set is the worst of its k at k = 1 (97, rank 5 of 5) and k = 2 (116, rank 10 of 10), 24.8 and 7.3 cells below the subset mean at k = 2 and 3.
- Descriptive: which year is learned matters more than how many. One year scores 97 to 152 of 168 and, at the high-amplitude points, 1 to 153 (lag 3 learns from 2007 for L and 2012 for E and wins 1 there; lag 2 wins 153); two years 116 to 155 and 33 to 149; four years 143 to 160 and 81 to 107. The per-point real-shape counts follow the same order (subset means 114.0 / 125.4 / 130.0 / 134.6 / 138.0, AT_90 126), so on average two years already beat no correction there too (6zzb's nearest pair: 121). On the 8 pooled real-shape cells the subset means are 6.8 / 7.7 / 7.9 / 8.0 / 8.0. High-amplitude rho is -0.30 (single years) and -0.28 (within k), the opposite sign to all 168 cells; with five single years neither sign is distinguishable from zero (|rho| = 0.4 has a permutation p of about 0.5).
- Reading: 6zzb's history-length curve was right in shape and wrong in level, because its fixed choice (the year next to the scored half) happened to be the worst single year to learn from. Averaged over the year drawn, the raw month-by-month correction beats no correction from two years of log (140.8 of 168), not three, and gains steadily with more (S1, S4). There is no sign that recent years teach more than older ones over a five-year window: what sets the outcome is the year-to-year weather of the learning years, with a 55-cell swing between single years. That also qualifies 6zzc to 6zzh: every one-year rule there learned only from lag 1, the worst year for LC, so their one-year counts against no correction (126 to 140 of 168) are one draw, not an average. For a designer: two seasons of log are enough on average, but the spread is large (one unlucky pair scores 116, below no correction), so a correction should carry its own uncertainty or be shrunk, which is what 6zzc to 6zzh built.
- Limits: five years per half, so each k has 1 to 10 subsets, not independent draws (subsets share years), and the recency test has little power; the same lag set is used for both halves, so lag and calendar year are tied (lag 1 is 2009 for L and 2010 for E); only LC was rerun, not the shrinkage rules of 6zzc to 6zzh; the swept shapes repeat their bias in learning and scored years (as in 6zza); same boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model and NASA POWER only.
- Next: rerun the best one-year rules (LHF_1, HR_1, HX_1) on all five single years, since their verdicts against no correction rest on lag 1, before closing the one-year shrinkage line as a paper result; report the correction's spread over learning years as a design interval; learn on the fly within a first deployment; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-15 · Milestone 6zzh: does a REML estimate of the band's between-point variance, or keeping a high-latitude boat's own one-year cosine, beat 6zzg's moment-estimated band pool HR_1? (predictions in `experiments/run_reml_season.py`'s docstring, committed in 9f80c6ef6 before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zzg's first two "Next" items. 6zzg read HB_1's loss as the moment estimate of tau_H over 9 to 11 points being too small at high latitude. The moment estimate weights every point equally although V differs between points; REML on z_q ~ N(mu, tau^2 + V_q) with a precision-weighted mean (Morris 1983, JASA 78:47-55, doi 10.1080/01621459.1983.10477920) is the textbook fix. The blunt alternative is not to shrink above 50 degrees at all. Plan: 6zzg's data, cells, learning years, fit, V, bands, alignment and leave-one-out unchanged. RR_k: band pool in calendar phase, REML tau^2 (log grid plus golden section), precision-weighted leave-one-out mean; RB_k: the same, hemisphere-aligned; HX_k: HR_k below 50 degrees, the unshrunk fit (w = 1) in the high band. HR_k rerun as a check. 6zzg's caution (the swept shapes favour calendar-phase pools) stands.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_reml_season.txt`, about 9 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 5 new tests, one of which checks REML against the equal-V closed form): **five of eight predictions held.** Check passed: HR_k 140 / 149 / 151 / 160 and AT_90 139 reproduce 6zzg exactly. R1 held: RR_1 140 of 168. R2 refuted: RR_1 137 of 240 per-point real-shape cells (HR_1 140). R3 refuted: RB_1 130 (HB_1 133). R4 held: HX_1 102 of 168 at the nine high-amplitude points (HR_1 97, LHF_1 89). R5 held: HX_1 140 of 168. R6 refuted: the aligned high band's REML tau is 0.0120 against the moment 0.0128 (E) and 0.0200 against 0.0171 (L). R7 held: RR_k 148 / 152 / 161 against HR_k 149 / 151 / 160. R8 held: 8 of 8.
- Descriptive: REML moves tau by little: over 24 band, half and k combinations of the real shape the REML tau is within 0.004 of the moment tau in 22 (calendar) and 22 (aligned), and the median w of RR stays within 0.02 of HR's in every high-band row. Where they differ, REML goes to the boundary: in half L at k = 1 the mid band's REML tau is 0 in both phases (moments 0.0038 and 0.0163), so RR and RB pull every mid point fully onto the precision-weighted mean; that is the likely reason RR_1 loses at the high-amplitude points (66 of 168 against HR_1's 97; P-30 is a mid-band point), a reading not tested here. HX_1 is the best one-year rule on every count, by a little: 140 of 168 (HR_1 140), 102 of 168 at the high-amplitude points, 5 of 8 high-amplitude real-shape pooled cells (HR_1 5) and 141 of 240 per-point real-shape cells (HR_1 140). At k >= 2 HX_k equals HR_k (149 / 151 / 160), because HR already keeps 70 to 89% of the high band's own fit there. RB_5 162 ties the programme's highest 168-cell count (LB_5, HB_5, HF_5). No rule dominates B in any of the high band's own 168 pooled cells (0 for all, as in 6zzf and 6zzg).
- Reading: the variance estimator was not the weak link. With 9 to 11 points per band, the moment and REML estimates agree within 0.004 in 44 of 48 cases, so 6zzg's aligned-pool loss comes from the band mean itself, not from a mis-estimated pull. Not shrinking above 50 degrees is the safest one-year rule, but its edge over HR_1 is 1 cell of 168 and 1 of 240, which is noise. Every one-year rule on the fitted cosine in four milestones (6zze to 6zzh, 11 distinct rules) lands at 126 to 140 of 168 against no correction's 139; the remaining one-year signal is at the high-amplitude points (25 for no correction against 89 to 102 for the rules that keep most of the own fit). For a designer: after one season, keep a high-latitude boat's own fitted cosine unshrunk and pool lower-latitude boats within bands; do not expect more from a better variance estimator.
- Limits: HX's margin over HR is within noise and HX's 50-degree threshold is the grid's band edge, not tuned; the swept shapes favour calendar-phase rules by construction, so RB's loss on them is partly built in (on the real shape it loses too: 130 against 140 per-point); REML is fitted per band and half with few degrees of freedom and goes to 0 at the boundary; V treats months as independent; the k and recency confound of 6zzb remains; same boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model and NASA POWER only.
- Next: close the one-year shrinkage line as a paper result (rules converge to no correction over all cells; the gain is at high latitude); average over all k-subsets to separate history length from recency; learn on the fly within a first deployment; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-15 · Milestone 6zzg: does pooling the fitted yearly cosine itself (a1, b1) within a latitude band, hemisphere-aligned, let one year of the boat's log help? (predictions in `experiments/run_hier_season.py`'s docstring, committed in e06cd5bd7 before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zzf's first "Next". 6zzf's banded prior LB_1 shrank one year's (a1, b1) toward zero (133 of 168; 65 of 168 at the nine high-amplitude points against unshrunk LHF_1's 89) and read the rest of the high-latitude loss as noise. If neighbours at the same |latitude| share the seasonal error once the southern hemisphere is shifted six months, the right target is the band's mean cosine, not zero. Plan: 6zzf's data, cells, learning years, fit, V and bands unchanged. HB_k shrinks each point's hemisphere-aligned (a1, b1) toward the leave-one-out band mean by w = tau_H^2 / (tau_H^2 + V), tau_H^2 the between-point variance of a component net of mean V; HF_k pools all 30 points; HR_k pools the band in calendar phase (no alignment). LB_k rerun as a check. Fixed in advance: the swept shapes inject a calendar-phase cosine shared by every point, which favours HR, so the real shape (8 pooled, 240 per-point cells) is the fair test.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_hier_season.txt`, about 8 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 6 new tests): **three of nine predictions held, and the hypothesis did not.** Check passed: LB_k 133 / 143 / 151 / 162 and AT_90 139 reproduce 6zzf exactly. G1 refuted: HB_1 127 of 168. G2 refuted. G3 refuted: HB_1 64 at the high-amplitude points (LB_1 65). G4 refuted: HB_1 133 of 240 per-point real-shape cells. G5 held: 8 of 8. G6 refuted: HB_1 133 against HR_1 140. G7 refuted: HB_1 133 against HF_1 136. G8 held: the aligned high-band mean amplitude is 0.042 / 0.059 against the low band's 0.020 / 0.014 (E / L). G9 held: HB_2 143.
- Descriptive: the unaligned rule HR_1 is the best one-year rule in the programme on every count: 140 of 168 pooled cells (the first one-year rule to reach no correction, AT_90 139, by one cell), 97 of 168 at the high-amplitude points (LHF_1 89, LB_1 65, AT_90 25), 5 of 8 high-amplitude real-shape pooled cells (LB_1 and HB_1 2, AT_90 1) and 140 of 240 per-point real-shape cells (LA_1 136 was the best). At k >= 2 the rules converge (HR_2 149, HB_2 143, HF_2 142; HB_5, HF_5, LB_5 162; HR_5 160). Post hoc (scratch script, not pre-registered): in the aligned frame the high band has a strong common swing (mean amplitude 0.04 to 0.06, peak near June in northern terms) and a small between-point spread (tau_H 0.013 to 0.017), so HB keeps w about 0.2 to 0.3 and replaces each high-latitude point's own cosine with the band's; in calendar phase the two hemispheres cancel in the mean, the spread rises to 0.030 to 0.035 and HR keeps w about 0.6 of the point's own fit, shrinking toward a smaller mean. No rule dominates B in any of the high band's own 168 pooled cells (0 for all, as in 6zzf).
- Reading: a common hemisphere-aligned seasonal error does exist at high latitude, but pooling toward it costs, because the nine high-latitude points (two oceans, the North Sea and the Gulf of Alaska) differ in their seasonal error by more than the moment estimate of tau_H allows, so the band mean overrides each point's own signal. HR wins for a mechanical reason, not because the error is shared in calendar phase: mixing hemispheres inflates the between-point variance, so it trusts each point's own fit more while still pulling it toward a modest shared offset. The lesson for the shrinkage family is that one season's tau is the weak link: rules that keep more of the point's own fit at high latitude (LHF_1, HR_1) beat rules that shrink harder (LAP_1, LB_1, HB_1). For a designer: after one season, pooling a fleet's fits is safe on ordinary water, but do not pull a high-latitude boat's correction toward its neighbours' mean; keep most of its own fitted cosine.
- Limits: HR's 140 against 139 is one cell, and the swept shapes favour HR by construction (fixed in advance), so the fair evidence is the real shape (140 against 133 to 136 of 240 per-point cells; 5 against 2 of 8 high-amplitude pooled cells); the post-hoc reading of w is descriptive, not tested; bands of 9 to 11 points give tau_H few degrees of freedom; V treats months as independent; the k and recency confound of 6zzb remains; same boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model and NASA POWER only.
- Next: a per-point tau with a floor at high latitude (or a w floored at the LHF end above 50 degrees), which the evidence of 6zze to 6zzg now points to; a profile-likelihood or REML tau_H instead of moments, since the moment estimate over 9 points sets the high-band pull; average over all k-subsets to separate history length from recency; learn on the fly within a first deployment; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-15 · Milestone 6zzf: does a latitude-dependent prior on the one-year cosine's amplitude keep 6zze's gain on ordinary water and stop its loss at high latitude? (predictions in `experiments/run_lat_prior_season.py`'s docstring, committed in 35f908afd before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zze's first "Next". 6zze's fleet prior LAP_1 (one tau_A^2 pooled over all 30 points) reached 132 of 168 pooled cells (AT_90 139) but cost at the nine high-amplitude points (54 against unshrunk LHF_1's 89), and 6zze read that as one ocean-wide variance shrinking hardest where the seasonal error is largest. Plan: 6zze's data, cells, learning years, harmonic fit and V unchanged. With d_p = (a1^2 + b1^2) / 2 - V per point, LB_k pools d_p within three |latitude| bands fixed by the grid (low <= 20, 11 points; mid 25 to 42, 10; high >= 50, 9), LL_k fits d_p = c0 + c1 |lat| by OLS over the 30 points (floored at 0). LAP_k rerun as a check.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_lat_prior_season.txt`, about 5 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): **five of nine predictions held.** Check passed: LAP_k 132 / 143 / 151 / 161 and AT_90 139 reproduce 6zze exactly. F1 held: LB_1 133 of 168. F2 refuted (133 against 139). F3 held: at the high-amplitude points LB_1 65 of 168 (LAP_1 54, LL_1 55). F4 refuted (65 against 75). F5 refuted: LB_1 135 of 240 per-point real-shape cells (LA_1 136, LAP_1 134). F6 refuted: LL_1 130. F7 held: 8 of 8. F8 held: LB_2 143 (LAP_2 143). F9 held: high-band prior sd above low-band at k = 1 in both halves (E 0.0307 against 0.0297, L 0.0434 against 0.0204).
- Descriptive: over 168 cells LB_k 133 / 143 / 151 / 162 and LL_k 130 / 141 / 151 / 159 against LAP_k 132 / 143 / 151 / 161; LB_5 162 is the highest 168-cell count in the programme by one cell. The latitude gradient of the prior is real from two years on (high-band sd 0.027 to 0.042 against low-band 0 to 0.017 at k >= 2) but at k = 1 it is absent in one scored half (E: low 0.030, mid 0.025, high 0.031; LL's slope -1.9e-6 per degree): one year's weather is as large as the seasonal error at every latitude. On the real shape the median w at the high band moves only from 0.53 (LAP) to 0.63 (LB) at k = 1, because the sampling variance V is also larger there; at the low band LB shrinks harder than LAP from k >= 2 (w 0.60 / 0.42 / 0.22 against 0.78 / 0.83 / 0.89), which costs 2 to 3 cells at k = 3 and 5 on the low points alone. LL_5 sets the low-latitude prior to zero (w = 0) and still scores 147 of 168 there. The nine high-band points pooled alone never dominate B in any cell for any rule, AT_90 included (0 of 168); the nine high-amplitude points are a different set (they include P-30 from the mid band and leave out P-50), so their counts above are not the high band's.
- Reading: the prior's structure was not the main cause of 6zze's high-latitude loss. A banded prior gives back 11 of the 35 lost cells there (54 to 65) at no cost elsewhere (133 of 168, one more than LAP), but it cannot give back the rest, because at high latitude the one-year fit's own noise V is large too, so even the right prior variance leaves w near 0.6; the continuous line is worse than bands because one year's d_p has no clean trend with latitude. One year of log still does not beat no correction over all cells (133 against 139); the gap that remains is noise, not prior shape. For a designer: if a fleet shares its fits, pool them within latitude bands rather than ocean-wide (never worse, +11 cells at high latitude); above about 50 degrees after one season, the unshrunk cosine (LHF_1, 89 of 168) is still the better choice.
- Limits: three bands fixed by the grid, with 9 to 11 points each, so each band prior has few degrees of freedom; the prior includes the point's own learning years (as 6zze's LAP did; no scored year); V treats months as independent; the swept shapes repeat between learning and scored years; the k and recency confound of 6zzb remains; same boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model and NASA POWER only.
- Next: a prior on the signal-to-noise ratio rather than the variance (shrink toward the band's typical w), or a hierarchical fit that pools (a1, b1) themselves within a band, since the high-latitude phase is shared; average over all k-subsets to separate history length from recency; learn on the fly within a first deployment; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-15 · Milestone 6zze: does shrinking the fitted yearly cosine's own amplitude toward zero let one year of the boat's log help? (predictions in `experiments/run_amp_shrink_season.py`'s docstring, committed in 86a94c8f7 before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zzd's first "Next". 6zzd's LHF_1 (AT_90 on the clamped first harmonic fitted to one year's month ratios) was the best one-year rule (126 of 168) but trailed no correction (AT_90 139), and its median fitted amplitude on the real shape was 4.4% at k = 1 against 2.3% at k = 5: one year's weather inflates the cosine. Plan: 6zzd's data, shapes, cells, learning years, r_m, se_m and fit unchanged. Sampling variance per component V = sum se_m^2 / 72 (months independent). LA_k shrinks (a1, b1) toward zero by w = tau_A^2 / (tau_A^2 + V), tau_A^2 = max(0, (a1^2 + b1^2) / 2 - V) from the fit itself; LAP_k pools tau_A^2 across the 30 points' fits on their own learning years (a fleet prior; no scored year enters). a0 is not shrunk. LHF_k rerun as a check.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_amp_shrink_season.txt`, about 5 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): **seven of nine predictions held.** Check passed: LHF_k 126 / 140 / 152 / 161 reproduce 6zzd exactly. A1 held: LA_1 130 of 168. A2 refuted (130 against 139). A3 held: LA_1 8 of 8 real-shape cells. A4 held: LA_1 136 of 240 per-point real-shape cells (LHF_1 134, AT_90 126). A5 held: LAP_1 132 against LA_1 130. A6 refuted (132). A7 held: at the high-amplitude points LA_1 61 of 168 (LHF_1 89, LAP_1 54, AT_90 25). A8 held: LA_1 km / B >= 1.0 in 158 of 168 (min 0.985). A9 held: LAP_1 134 of 240.
- Descriptive: on the real shape at k = 1 the median sd of a component is 0.0225 against a median fitted amplitude of 0.0435; LA's median w is 0.53 (w = 0 in 20 of 60 fits), LAP's 0.61 (never 0), so the shrunk amplitude is 2.3 to 2.7%, about what five years fit unshrunk (2.3%). The fleet prior sd is 0.028 per component at k = 1 and 0.020 to 0.026 at k = 2 to 5. Over all 168 cells LA_k 130 / 138 / 150 / 160 and LAP_k 132 / 143 / 151 / 161 against LHF_k 126 / 140 / 152 / 161: shrinkage helps at k = 1, LAP_2 (143) is the first two-year rule to beat no correction by more than one cell, and at k >= 3 it is neutral. At the nine high-amplitude points shrinkage costs at every k (LA_k 61 / 101 / 62 / 71, LAP_k 54 / 86 / 59 / 85 against LHF_k 89 / 132 / 85 / 92) and on their real shape wins 2 to 4 of 8 against LHF_1's 4 and LHF_2's 7: the failures are distance at E 2.5 and 20 kWh (LA_1 km / B 0.97, LAP_1 0.92 to 0.95, LHF_1 1.01 to 1.04).
- Reading: the one-year cosine does carry about a year's worth of extra weather, and shrinking its amplitude by its own noise removes some of it: 4 to 6 more of 168 cells and 2 more per-point real-shape cells (136, the best one-year count in the programme). It does not close the gap to no correction over 168 cells (130 to 132 against 139), and it is the wrong move at high latitude, where the real seasonal error (4.5 to 8.5%) is larger than the noise and the shrinkage throws signal away; the fleet prior, set mostly by low-latitude points, shrinks hardest there. The prior has the right structure but one variance for the whole ocean is wrong: the seasonal amplitude grows with latitude (6zz), so the prior should too. For a designer: on ordinary water after one season, halve the fitted cosine's amplitude (median w about 0.5); above about 50 degrees use the fitted cosine unshrunk, and after two seasons either works.
- Limits: V treats months as independent and ignores the AR(1) carry-over between adjacent months; tau_A^2 per fit has 2 degrees of freedom (w = 0 in a third of one-year fits); the fleet prior pools very different latitudes; the swept shapes are pure cosines that repeat in learning and scored years, which favour no shrinkage, so the real shape (8 pooled, 240 per-point cells) is the fair test and its pooled counts do not separate the rules (8 of 8 for all); the k and recency confound of 6zzb remains; same boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model and NASA POWER only.
- Next: a latitude-dependent prior for the amplitude (tau_A as a fitted function of |latitude|, or pooled within latitude bands), which should keep the shrinkage on ordinary water and drop it at high latitude; average over all k-subsets to separate history length from recency; learn on the fly within a first deployment; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-15 · Milestone 6zzd: does shrinking each learned month toward a fitted first harmonic, not the flat mean, let one year of the boat's log help? (predictions in `experiments/run_harmonic_season.py`'s docstring, committed in 083848c4e before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zzc's first "Next". 6zzc's flat-prior shrinkage LS_1 tied no correction on the real shape but lost on the swept shapes (110 against AT_90's 139 of 168) and cost cells at the nine high-amplitude points (61 against LC_1's 77), because a flat prior shrinks a smooth seasonal swing as hard as noise. Plan: 6zzc's data, shapes, cells, learning years, r_m and se_m unchanged. Fit g_m = a0 + a1 cos theta_m + b1 sin theta_m to the 12 learned month ratios by OLS (theta_m at month mid-points); tau^2 = max(0, sum (r_m - g_m)^2 / 9 - mean se_m^2); LH_k shrinks each month toward g_m with w_m = tau^2 / (tau^2 + se_m^2); LHF_k uses g_m alone (full shrinkage, three parameters). LC_k and LS_k rerun as checks.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_harmonic_season.txt`, about 25 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): **three of eight predictions held.** Checks passed: LC_k 97 / 116 / 142 / 158 and LS_k 110 / 128 / 144 / 154 reproduce 6zzb and 6zzc exactly. H1 refuted: LH_1 106 of 168 (LS_1 110). H2 refuted. H3 refuted: LH_1 6 of 8 real-shape cells (LS_1 7, AT_90 8). H4 held: at the high-amplitude points LH_1 94 of 168 (LC_1 77, LS_1 61, AT_90 25). H5 held: LHF_1 126 of 168. H6 refuted: LH_k against LS_k 106 / 110, 127 / 128, 148 / 144, 158 / 154. H7 held: LH_1 km / B >= 1.0 in 158 of 168 (min 0.993). H8 refuted: LH_1 122 of 240 per-point real-shape cells (AT_90 126).
- Descriptive: the unshrunk part matters less than the prior's shape. **LHF_1, the fitted harmonic alone, is the best one-year rule tested in this programme**: 126 of 168 (LC_1 97, LS_1 110, LH_1 106; AT_90 139), 8 of 8 on the real shape, 134 of 240 per-point real-shape cells (AT_90 126, LS_1 129). With two years LHF_2 reaches 140 of 168, the first correction to match no correction (139) from two years (LC_2 116, LS_2 128); LHF_5 161 is the highest 168-cell count of any rule so far (LC_5 158). At the high-amplitude points LH_2 wins 142 of 168 and 7 of 8 real-shape cells, the best there at any k. Median fitted amplitude on the real shape 4.4% at k = 1 against 2.3% at k = 5 (one year's weather inflates the fitted cosine by about 2 points); tau = 0 in 13 of 60 one-year fits.
- Reading: a three-parameter smooth correction is a better use of one season of log than twelve monthly corrections, shrunk or not. Shrinking the residual months back toward the harmonic adds back month-to-month weather (LH_1 106 against LHF_1 126) and is only worth it where the seasonal error is large (high latitudes, k >= 2). It still trails no correction over all 168 cells at k = 1 (126 against 139): one year's fitted cosine carries the year's weather too (4.4% against 2.3% amplitude), so the remaining step is to shrink the harmonic's own amplitude, not the months around it. For a designer: after one season fit a yearly cosine to the log's harvest / forecast ratio and use that; after two, it matches or beats no correction; keep the raw months only above about 50 degrees.
- Limits: OLS ignores the month-to-month differences in se_m; the tau^2 moment estimate has 9 degrees of freedom and hits zero in 22% of one-year fits; the swept shapes are pure first harmonics by construction, which favours a harmonic fit (the real shape, 8 pooled and 240 per-point cells, is the fair test and agrees: LHF_1 8 of 8 and 134 of 240); the k and recency confound of 6zzb remains; same boat, payload, weights, calm-water daily model and NASA POWER only.
- Next: shrink the harmonic's amplitude itself toward zero by its own standard error (empirical Bayes on (a1, b1)); average over all k-subsets to separate history length from recency; learn on the fly within a first deployment; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-14 · Milestone 6zzc: does shrinking each learned month toward the year's mean ratio let one year of the boat's log help? (predictions in `experiments/run_shrunk_season.py`'s docstring, committed in ad6a0a334 before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zzb's first "Next". 6zzb's one-year correction LC_1 lost to no correction (97 against AT_90's 139 of 168; 4 against 8 real-shape cells) because a one-year month carries about 4.4% of that year's weather. Plan: 6zzb's data, shapes, learning years and 168 pooled cells unchanged. LS_k shrinks each raw month ratio r_m toward the k-year mean ratio c_bar by w_m = tau^2 / (tau^2 + se_m^2) (James and Stein 1961; Efron and Morris 1973), with everything estimated from the k learning years only: se_m from the month's daily residuals with an AR(1) inflation (lag-1 rho clipped to [0, 0.9]), tau^2 by method of moments across the 12 months; then 6zza's clamp and interpolation. LSP_k is PS's fixed tau on the same climatology. LC_k rerun as a check.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_shrunk_season.txt`, about 20 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): **five of eight predictions held.** Check passed: LC_k reproduces 6zzb's 97 / 116 / 142 / 158 exactly. S1 held: LS_1 dominates B in 7 of 8 real-shape cells (LC_1 4, AT_90 8). S2 refuted: LS_1 110 of 168 (AT_90 139). S3 refuted: LS_k against LC_k 110 / 97, 128 / 116, 144 / 142, 154 / 158 (shrinkage helps at k <= 3, costs 4 cells at k = 5). S4 refuted: real shape at the nine high-amplitude points LS_1 3 against LC_1 4 (AT_90 1). S5 held (154 against 158). S6 held: the noise estimate is calibrated, median mean(se) / year-to-year sd 0.886 (quartiles 0.68, 1.22) over 720 point-half-months. S7 held: per-point real-shape cells LS_1 129 of 240 against AT_90 126 (LC_1 106). S8 held: LS_1 km / B >= 1.0 in 161 of 168 (min 0.990; LC_1 153).
- Descriptive: median weight w_m 0.44 at k = 1 (tau 0.041, se 0.053); tau = 0 (full shrinkage to the mean ratio) in 9 of 60 point-halves at k = 1. At the nine high-amplitude points shrinkage costs at every k: LS_k 61 / 119 / 72 / 83 of 168 against LC_k 77 / 138 / 104 / 105. LSP_k (fixed tau) goes at least as far as B in all 168 cells for every k, and at the high-amplitude points LSP_1 wins 114 of 168 and 6 of 8 real-shape cells, the best one-year rule there; on all 30 points it trades downtime for distance (79 of 168; ex / B 1.18 to 1.25 in half L at 2.5 to 10 kWh).
- Reading: shrinkage removes about a third of the one-year penalty over 168 cells (42 cells behind no correction to 29), and on the real shape a one-year shrunk correction now ties no correction (7 against 8 pooled cells, 129 against 126 per-point cells, against LC_1's 4 and 106), and its distance never falls more than 1% below B. On the swept shapes it still loses (110 against 139), because a flat prior across months treats the seasonal error as twelve independent draws and shrinks a real 3% cosine as hard as noise; the high-amplitude points show the same cost (61 against 77). The noise model itself is not the problem (S6). For a designer: with one season of log on ordinary water, a shrunk monthly correction is no worse than none and uses the log safely; at high latitudes keep the raw correction, or use the shrunk climatology with the fixed 30-to-60-day feedback (LSP_1).
- Limits: one prior family (exchangeable months around the mean ratio); tau^2 from 12 months has few degrees of freedom and hits zero in 15% of one-year fits; the k and recency confound of 6zzb remains; pooled real-shape counts are 8 cells and per-point high-amplitude counts moved only 45 to 50 of 72 across rules; the swept shapes repeat their bias in learning and scored years; same boat, payload, weights and calm-water daily model; NASA POWER only. The results header's column list was trimmed after the run to drop a statistic the script never printed (no number changed).
- Next: a structured prior, shrinking toward a fitted first harmonic c_bar (1 + A cos(2 pi (d - phi) / 365.25)) rather than toward the flat mean, which keeps a smooth seasonal error and drops month-to-month noise; average over all k-subsets to separate history length from recency; learn on the fly within a first deployment; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-14 · Milestone 6zzb: how many earlier years does the boat need to learn its climatology's seasonal error? (predictions in `experiments/run_learn_years.py`'s docstring, committed in 878c00abb before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zza's first "Next". 6zza's month-by-month correction, learned from five earlier years, matched the per-day oracle; a designer needs to know whether one season of the boat's own log does the same. With one year the ratio also carries that year's weather. Plan: 6zza's data, shapes and 168 pooled cells unchanged; a cell on half H learns from the k years of the other half nearest H, k = 1, 2, 3, 5 (scoring L: 2009 back; scoring E: 2010 forward); k = 5 is 6zza exactly (a test checks LC_5 = LC_90, LCP_5 = LCP). Rules B, AT_90, LC_k (AT_90 on the k-year corrected climatology), LCP_k (PS's fixed tau on it). Added per-point cells (point x half x pack, 240 on the real shape, 72 at the nine high-amplitude points) for resolution.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_learn_years.txt`, about 12 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): **four of eight predictions held.** K1 refuted: LC_1 dominates B in 4 of 8 real-shape cells (AT_90 8, LC_5 8). K2 held: at the high-amplitude points LC_1 4 against AT_90 1. K3 refuted: there the counts run 4 / 7 / 5 / 5 for k = 1 / 2 / 3 / 5, not monotone. K4 held: LC_3 5. K5 refuted: LC_1 76 of 128 combined (AT_90 104). K6 refuted: LCP_1 2 of 8 (LCP_5 7). K7 held: LC_1 km / B >= 1.0 in 153 of 168 (min 0.983). K8 held: per-point high-amplitude cells LC_1 47 of 72 against AT_90 43.
- Descriptive: over all 168 pooled cells LC_k wins 97 / 116 / 142 / 158 (AT_90 139), LCP_k 75 / 97 / 148 / 151. The one-year ratio is off the five-year ratio by a median 0.044 per month (two years 0.027, three 0.017), and 1,113 of 15,120 one-year months hit the clamp (five years: 194). LC_1's real-shape failures are on dark hours, not distance (ex / B 1.02 to 1.20 at E 10 kWh and L 5 to 10 kWh): it trusts a noisy month and runs the pack low. At the nine high-amplitude points every k beats the uncorrected rule on all 168 cells (LC_k 77 / 138 / 104 / 105 against 25); the 20 kWh pack in half L loses at every k (km / B 0.85 to 0.95). Per-point real-shape cells over all 30 points: AT_90 126 of 240, LC_k 106 / 121 / 135 / 138.
- Reading: one year of the boat's own log is too little at most points. A one-year monthly ratio carries about 4.4% of that year's weather, more than the climatology's real seasonal error at most points (6zz: 1.8% median), so the correction adds more error than it removes and the corrected rule falls below the uncorrected one (97 against 139 of 168; 4 against 8 real cells). Three years bring the noise to 1.7% and the corrected rule back above the uncorrected one (142, 8 of 8); five give 6zza's 158. Above 50 degrees, where the real error is 4.5 to 8.5%, even one year pays (77 against 25). The k = 2 peak there (138) sits above k = 3 and 5; with the nearest years also the most recent, more years may trade noise against staleness, but this design cannot separate the two. For a designer: apply a learned month-by-month correction once the boat has about three years on the same water, or after one year only where the climatology's seasonal error is several per cent; a shrinkage of each month's ratio toward 1 by its noise is the obvious next candidate.
- Limits: one choice of learning years per k (the nearest), so k and recency are confounded; pooled real-shape counts are 8 cells, and per-point cells at the high-amplitude points moved only 43 to 49 of 72 across all rules, so differences of one or two pooled cells are small; the swept shapes repeat their bias in learning and scored years (as in 6zza), so their counts test learning noise and the rest of the rule, not the learning of the bias; the nine points were picked on all ten years; same boat, payload, weights and calm-water daily model; NASA POWER only.
- Next: shrink each learned month toward 1 by its estimated noise (an empirical-Bayes weight from the year-to-year spread), which should let one year help everywhere; average over all k-subsets to separate history length from recency; learn on the fly within a first deployment; re-read the tau table on the corrected climatology; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-14 · Milestone 6zza: can the boat learn its climatology's seasonal error from its own earlier years and close the gap to the per-day oracle? (predictions in `experiments/run_learned_season.py`'s docstring, committed in e142f8dfa before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zz's first "Next". 6zz's per-day oracle OR beat the 90-day ratio rule AT_90 by 16 of 128 cells, read as the value of knowing the seasonal shape, and 6zz measured that shape's real amplitude at 4.5 to 8.5% above 50 degrees. Plan: 6zz's data and split (2005 to 2014 scored in halves E and L, climatology 2015 to 2024, 30 points, 50 W, packs 2.5 to 20 kWh) and its 20 shapes plus the real shape (s = 1: only the climatology's own error), 168 cells. A cell on half H learns from the other half only: per point and shape, c_m = month-m harvest / month-m scaled climatology over those five years, clamped to 0.85 to 1.15, interpolated between month mid-points. LC_90 runs AT_90 on c(d) x climatology (K term, rebuilt 45-day-lead target and ratio denominator); LM_90 on the five-year mean ratio only; LCP uses the corrected climatology with PS's fixed tau. B is not corrected. Weights, tau table and prior frozen.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_learned_season.txt`, about 10 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests, one checks that AT_90, OR, PS and B reproduce 6zz's cells): **seven of seven predictions held.** L1 and L2: LC_90 dominates B in 120 of 128 combined cells (AT_90 104, OR 120). L3: LC_90 120 against LM_90 109. L4: LC_90 8 of 8 on the real shape (so does every rule). L5: on the real shape over the 9 high-amplitude points (6zz A_hat > 0.03), LC_90 5 of 8 against AT_90 1. L6: LCP 115 against PS 65. L7: LC_90 km / B >= 1.0 in 168 of 168 (min 1.008).
- Descriptive: over all 168 cells LC_90 158, OR 152, LCP 151, LM_90 145, AT_90 139, PS 90. LC_90's 10 failures are all half E at 10 to 20 kWh with a pessimistic mean (s0 0.97 to 0.98), on dark hours (1.01 to 1.37 x B) while going 13 to 21% further: the corrected target lets the big pack run lower than B's pessimistic one. AT_90's small-pack failures (banking sun it does not use, km / B 0.96 to 0.99) are gone under LC_90 (km / B 1.04 to 1.06 there). LCP's 13 failures sit at the pessimistic end with 2.5 to 5 kWh packs, on dark hours only just over B (1.01 to 1.18 x). At the 9 high-amplitude points every uncorrected rule mostly loses on the real shape to B on distance (AT_90 km / B 0.80 to 1.00, dark hours 0.20 to 0.49 x B): over those points B's own week of harvest outruns a climatology that is wrong by month. LC_90 recovers distance there at 2.5 to 10 kWh (km / B 0.94 to 1.21) but still trails at 20 kWh (0.88, 0.93); LCP wins 7 of 8 (all but L / 20 kWh, 0.90) and 146 of 168 high-amplitude cells, LC_90 105, PS 72, OR 27, AT_90 25, LM_90 21. Learned ratios on the real shape run 0.850 to 1.139; 194 of 15,120 learned months hit the clamp: 138 at s0 1.02 to 1.03, 50 at 0.97 to 0.98, 6 on the real shape (all at P+50, P+60, North Sea and Gulf of Alaska, at the lower bound).
- Reading: a seasonal correction learned from five earlier years of the boat's own harvest matches the per-day oracle on 6zz's combined sweep (120 against 120) and beats it over all 168 cells (158 against 152), without ever going less far than the 7-day rule. The month-by-month shape matters: a single mean correction gains little (LM_90 109; 21 of 168 at high latitude, below the uncorrected rule). The real-data result is the stronger one, because the sweep's added bias repeats exactly in the learning years: at the nine points where the climatology's real seasonal error exceeds 3%, the uncorrected adaptive rule wins 1 of 8 real cells and the learned correction 5, or 7 with a fixed tau. Once the climatology has been corrected, the adaptive tau adds little and costs distance at high latitude; it still helps the large pack at mid latitudes under a pessimistic sweep. For a designer: on a repeat deployment, scale the climatology by last years' month-by-month harvest ratio first; then either rule works, and above 50 degrees the fixed 30-to-60-day feedback is the better pairing.
- Limits: in the 20 swept shapes the added bias is identical in the learning and scored years, so the correction removes it by construction (the swept counts test the rest of the rule, not the learning); the honest test is the real shape, 8 cells overall and 8 at the high-amplitude points, small counts. The high-amplitude points were picked with 6zz's A_hat, which used all ten years, including the scored half. Five learning years, one clamp (never binding on the real shape at most points), one interpolation; same boat, payload, weights and calm-water daily model as 6zx to 6zz; the climatology and both halves are NASA POWER, not cross-checked against a ground station.
- Next: learn from fewer years (1, 2, 3) to find how much history a boat needs; learn on the fly within a deployment (each month's ratio updated as the year runs) with no earlier years; re-read the tau table on the corrected climatology, since its eff steps were set for an uncorrected one; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-14 · Milestone 6zz: does 6zw's adaptive rule hold when the climatology is wrong on average and by season, and how large is the real seasonal error? (predictions in `experiments/run_mean_seasonal_bias.py`'s docstring, committed in d563c3dac before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zy's first two "Next" items. 6zy found a zero-mean seasonal bias up to 3% a weak stress, and read that the mean error is what hurts; a mean and a seasonal error together might compound, since a lagging ratio could misread the mean during the swing. 6zy also assumed its cosine without measuring a real one. Plan: part S, 6zx's data and split unchanged (2005 to 2014 scored in halves E and L, climatology 2015 to 2024, 30 points, 50 W, packs 2.5 to 20 kWh), climatology (K term and 45-day-lead target, B rerun) x s(d) = s0 (1 + 0.03 cos(2 pi (d - phi) / 365.25)), s0 0.97 / 0.98 / 1.02 / 1.03, phi 0 / 91 / 182 / 274; 128 combined cells plus 32 flat references (A = 0, which reproduce 6zx's cells at s = s0; a test checks this). Rules frozen: AT_30 / 60 / 90, PS, PSS, per-day oracle OR. Part M, no rule run: per point, the monthly ratio of 2005 to 2014 harvest to the unscaled 2015 to 2024 climatology, its first-harmonic amplitude A_hat and peak day.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_mean_seasonal_bias.txt`, about 8 minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): **nine of nine predictions held.** R1: AT_90 dominates B in 104 of 128. R2: the seasonal swing costs AT_90 4 cells against 4 x its flat count (108). R3: AT_90 104 against PS 65 and PSS 71. R4: AT_90 104 >= AT_60 101 >= AT_30 88. R5: at s0 1.03 AT_90's count over phases is 6, 7, 8, 6. R6: the per-day oracle 120 against AT_90's 104. R7: AT_60 km / B >= 1.0 in 115 of 128 (min 0.956). M1: median A_hat 0.018. M2: A_hat > 0.03 at 9 of 30 points.
- Descriptive: by s0, AT_90's combined counts are 23 / 26 / 28 / 27 of 32 against flat x 4 of 24 / 28 / 28 / 28, so the swing costs at most 2 cells at any mean bias. Its 24 failures are of two kinds, as in 6zx: 13 at the 2.5 kWh pack at s0 0.97 to 0.98 on distance (km / B 0.955 to 0.996, dark hours 0.44 to 0.54 x B: it banks sun it does not use), and 11 at 10 to 20 kWh packs in half E on dark hours (1.00 to 1.25 x B), 10 of them with the seasonal optimism peaking 1 October or 1 January. The fixed rules swing hard with the mean: PS wins 30 and 32 of 32 at s0 0.97 / 0.98 and 2 and 1 at 1.02 / 1.03; PSS wins 4 of 32 at 0.97. The per-day oracle gains most from the swing (flat x 4 96, combined 120): knowing the seasonal shape is worth about 16 cells over the 90-day ratio. Part M: the measured amplitude is small at low and mid latitudes (median 0.018; 0.003 to 0.025 at most points between 40 S and 40 N) and large at high latitudes: A+60 0.085, Gulf of Alaska 0.078, P+60 0.071, North Sea 0.061, A+50 0.049, P-60 0.045, with single months off by 7 to 14%; the high-latitude northern points peak (optimistic) in May to July. Mean ratios per point run 0.960 (P+40) to 1.030 (A+60).
- Reading: a mean and a seasonal error together do not compound for the adaptive rule. The 90-day ratio loses at most 2 of 32 cells to a 3% seasonal swing on top of a 2 to 3% mean bias, while both fixed rules stay hostage to the sign of the mean. The real seasonal error of a ten-year NASA POWER climatology is inside 6zy's swept range at 21 of 30 points; at high latitudes it is 1.5 to 3 times larger, and those points were already in 6zx's real-data cells. The gap to the per-day oracle (16 of 128 cells) is the value of knowing the seasonal shape, which a boat on a repeat deployment could learn from its own earlier years. For a designer: the 90-day ratio stays the rule to ship; a learned month-by-month correction is the next candidate, worth most above 50 degrees.
- Limits: one amplitude (3%) in the combined sweep and one cosine with the same calendar phase at every point; part M's amplitudes come from monthly ratios over ten years at 30 grid cells and were not checked against a ground station; same boat, payload, weights and calm-water daily model as 6zx; dominance counts near their ceiling, so differences of a few cells are small.
- Next: a learned seasonal correction (the boat's own monthly harvest / climatology ratio from earlier years scaling the climatology) tested against the per-day oracle, with a 5-year learn / 5-year score split; the measured per-point seasonal error applied at its own amplitude and phase on new years; a continuous tau(eff) against the four steps; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-14 · Milestone 6zy: does 6zw's adaptive feedback rule survive a climatology whose bias changes with the season? (predictions in `experiments/run_seasonal_bias.py`'s docstring, committed in 00fb5a587 before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zx's first "Next". 6zv to 6zx swept a uniform bias (climatology x s all year); real climatology errors vary by season. A trailing N-day ratio lags a seasonal bias by about N / 2 days, so the long window that won under a uniform bias might lose, and a fixed tau has nothing to adapt with. Plan: 6zx's data and split unchanged (2005 to 2014 scored in halves E and L, climatology 2015 to 2024, 30 points, 50 W, packs 2.5 to 20 kWh), with the climatology (K term and the 45-day-lead target, B rerun) multiplied by s(d) = 1 + A cos(2 pi (d - phi) / 365.25), A 0.01 / 0.02 / 0.03, phi 0 / 91 / 182 / 274 days; 96 seasonal cells plus 8 flat reference cells. Rules frozen: AT_30 / 60 / 90, PS, PSS, and a per-day oracle OR (tau from s(d) / realised).
- Outcome (`experiments/results_seasonal_bias.txt`, a few minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 3 new tests): **four of seven predictions held.** Q1 held: AT_90 dominates B in 93 of 96. Q2 refuted: the window ordering does not reverse, AT_30 82, AT_60 89, AT_90 93. Q3 held, barely: AT_90 32 / 31 / 30 of 32 at A 0.01 / 0.02 / 0.03. Q4 refuted: at A 0.03 AT_90's count over phases is 7, 8, 8, 7 (span 1). Q5 refuted: PSS dominates in all 96, PS in 87. Q6 held: OR 94 against AT_90 93. Q7 held: AT_60 km / B >= 1.0 in 96 of 96 (min 1.013).
- Descriptive: the flat reference (6zx's s = 1.00 cells) is 8 of 8 for every rule but AT_30 (7). AT_90's 3 failures are all 20 kWh or 10 kWh packs with the bias peaking around 1 January or 1 October (optimistic in northern winter), on dark hours (1.03 to 1.14 x B) while going 10 to 15% further. PS's 9 failures are all 2.5 to 10 kWh packs with the bias peaking 1 April to 2 July, on dark hours (1.00 to 1.15 x B). The AT_60 estimator lags the season: at A 0.03, phi 0, its mean eff_hat is 1.0009 in April to June when the true eff is 0.9875, and 1.0161 in October to December against 1.0249; it also keeps 6zx's upward offset of about +0.004 on the flat shape.
- Reading: a zero-mean seasonal bias of up to 3% is a much weaker stress than a uniform one of the same size. Every rule, fixed or adaptive, keeps winning in most cells, and the slow fixed rule PSS, which failed every cell of 6zx at a uniform 3% pessimism, wins all 96 here. What hurt the rules in 6zv to 6zx is the climatology's mean error over months, not its seasonal shape; with the mean near honest (realised / climatology 0.993 to 0.995 on these years), the battery smooths a seasonal swing that averages out over a quarter. The adaptive rule's value is therefore insurance against a mean bias it cannot know in advance (6zx), not against seasonal error, and it costs almost nothing here (AT_90 93 against PSS 96). For a designer: a 90-day ratio is still the rule to ship; there is no case in this sweep for a shorter window to chase seasonal error.
- Limits: the seasonal shape is one cosine with the same calendar phase at every point, so it is optimistic in the southern summer where it is optimistic in the northern winter; the amplitude is at most 3% and the mean bias is held at zero, so the combination of a mean and a seasonal bias is untested; same points, boat, payload, weights and calm-water daily model as 6zx; dominance counts are near their ceiling, so differences of one to three cells between rules are small.
- Next: a mean plus seasonal bias (s(d) = s0 (1 + A cos ...)), where a lagging estimator may misjudge the mean during the swing; a seasonal error read from data (2005 to 2014 harvest against the 2015 to 2024 climatology, month by month, per point) rather than an assumed cosine; a continuous tau(eff) against the four steps; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-14 · Milestone 6zx: do 6zv's feedback-time table and 6zw's adaptive rule hold on ten years the programme has never read? (predictions in `experiments/run_fresh_years.py`'s docstring, committed in a80116001 before the data were fetched or any configuration run)
- Why: 6zw's first "Next". 6zw's adaptive rule (AT_N: r(d) = trailing-N-day harvest / trailing-N-day climatology with a 14-day prior at ratio 1, eff_hat = 1 / r, tau from 6zv's table 60 / 30 / 20 / 10 at eff_hat < 0.985 / 1.015 / 1.025 / above, PS's weights) won 46 of 56 cells, but the table was read from 6zv's results on those same cells. Plan: fetch NASA POWER daily ALLSKY_SFC_SW_DWN for 2005 to 2014 at the same 30 points (`experiments/fetch_sun_2005_2014.py`, 30 x 3,652 days, no fill values), plan with a climatology from all ten of 2015 to 2024, score halves "E" 2005 to 2009 and "L" 2010 to 2014, and rerun 6zv's bias sweep (s 0.97 to 1.03, B rerun at each s; 50 W, 4 packs; 56 cells) with every rule, table, weight and prior frozen. Rules AT_30 / 60 / 90, OR (tau from the cell's true eff), PS, PSS, B.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_fresh_years.txt`, 46 s on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): **seven of seven predictions held.** P1: AT_60 dominates B in 48 of 56 (6zw: 46). P2: 6, 7, 8, 8, 7, 6, 6 of 8 for s 0.97 to 1.03. P3: AT_60 48 against PS 37 and PSS 44. P4: AT_90 51 >= AT_60 48 >= AT_30 42, 6zw's ordering. P5: the oracle 47, within 1. P6: AT_60 km / B >= 1.0 in 53 of 56 (min 0.973). P7: realised / climatology 0.9954 (E) and 0.9926 (L), so at s 1.00 the later climatology overstates the earlier sun by under 1% (by region 0.990 to 0.996).
- Descriptive: AT_90 dominates at every s in at least 6 of 8 and in 7 or 8 at s 1.01 to 1.03, where PS falls to 4, 1, 1 (it overspends: dark hours 1.03 to 1.61 x B). PSS fails at s 0.97 in all 8 cells, on distance (km / B 0.973 to 0.999). AT_60's 8 failures: 3 at 2.5 kWh and s 0.97 to 0.98 on distance (km / B 0.973 to 0.996, dark hours 0.44 to 0.48 x B: it banks too much), and 5 in half E at 10 to 20 kWh and s >= 1.01 on dark hours (1.02 to 1.07 x B, distance 1.10 to 1.14 x); AT_90 removes 3 of the second kind. Of the oracle's 9 failures, 6 overshoot B's dark hours (1.02 to 1.36 x; 5 of them at s 0.97 to 0.98, where it holds tau 60 all year) and 3 fall short on distance (km / B 0.989 to 0.999). The estimator is again shrunk toward 1: mean eff_hat - eff runs from +0.011 at s 0.97 to -0.005 at s 1.03.
- Reading: the table and rule were read on 2015 to 2024 and hold, unchanged, on 2005 to 2014 with a climatology from the other decade: 48 of 56 (51 with a 90-day window) against 46 (48) in sample, and the window ordering replicates. So 6zw's result is not an artefact of reading the table off its own cells. On these years the ratio rule matches the oracle, as in 6zw, and beats both fixed rules; its remaining failures are the small pack under a pessimistic climatology (it banks sun it then does not use) and the large pack under an optimistic one. For a designer: a 90-day harvest-over-climatology ratio feeding the four-step table is the rule to ship; it does not need the climatology to be honest within the 3% swept here.
- Limits: the years are new but the points, boat, payload and weights are not; the climatology's realised bias on these years is small (under 1%), so the sweep, not the data, supplies most of the bias range; the bias is uniform, not seasonal; calm water, daily steps, NASA POWER 0.5 by 0.625 degrees, launched full each 1 January, one 4 m boat; NASA POWER's 2005 to 2014 record is the same product family as 2015 to 2024 but was not cross-checked against a ground station here.
- Next: a seasonal rather than uniform bias (scale by 1 + A cos of the day of year), where a trailing ratio should help more and a single tau less; new points (other meridians) with the table frozen; whether a continuous tau(eff) beats the four steps; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-14 · Milestone 6zw: can the boat measure its climatology's bias on the water and choose its battery feedback from it? (predictions in `experiments/run_self_bias.py`'s docstring, committed in 28e0c833c before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zv's "Next". 6zv read a feedback-time table off its bias sweep (tau 60 / 30 / 20 / 10 days at effective optimism below 0.985 / 1.015 / 1.025 / above) but a boat does not know its effective optimism. Plan: on 6zv's 56 cells (cross climatology, scale s 0.97 to 1.03 on climatology and target, 50 W, 4 packs, 30 points, B rerun at each s), the boat computes r(d) = trailing-N-day harvest / trailing-N-day climatology with a 14-day prior at ratio 1 and sets eff_hat = 1 / r. Rules, all at PS's weights: AT_N (tau from the table at eff_hat, N 30 / 60 / 90, 60 primary), DC (climatology term K multiplied by r, PS's tau), AD (both), OR (oracle: tau from the cell's true eff, fixed). PS and PSS for reference.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_self_bias.txt`, a few minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): **four of seven predictions held.** P1 held: AT_60 dominates B in 46 of 56 cells (PS 30, PSS 36). P2 held: 7, 7, 7, 7, 7, 6, 5 of 8 for s 0.97 to 1.03, no s below 5, where PS ranges 0 to 8 and PSS 3 to 8. P3 refuted: DC dominates in only 24 (it goes 7 to 21% further than B but overspends the battery, dark hours up to 1.31 x B on R). P4 held: AT_60 46 against the oracle's 44. P5 held: AT_60 km / B >= 1.0 in 52 of 56 (the four misses are T / 2.5 kWh at s 0.97 to 1.00, km / B 0.927 to 0.994, where it banks too much: dark hours 0.36 to 0.51 x B). P6 refuted: AT_90 48 >= AT_60 46 >= AT_30 42; a longer window is better, not worse. P7 refuted: mean eff_hat is within 0.01 of the true eff in 12 of 14 (s, half) pairs; it is shrunk toward 1 (+0.014 at T s 0.97, -0.007 at R s 1.03), the 14-day prior and the 7-day restart each January.
- Descriptive: AT_60 never settles on one tau. At s 0.97 on T it spends 64% of days at tau 60 but 12% at tau 10; at s 1.03 on R 55% at tau 10 and 12% at 60. AD (debiased K plus adaptive tau) is the worst rule here (12 of 56): by its numbers it goes 3 to 25% further than B and overspends the battery (dark hours up to 2.03 x B); our unchecked guess is that the two corrections compound, since both push speed up once the ratio says the sun is better than planned. Of the oracle's 12 failures, 10 overshoot B's dark hours (1.06 to 1.43 x: T / 10 kWh at s 0.99 and 1.02, T / 20 kWh at 0.98 and 0.99, R / 5 and 10 kWh at 0.97 and 1.00, R / 20 kWh at 0.97 and 1.03) and 2 fall short on distance (T / 2.5 kWh at 0.97 and 0.98, km / B 0.947 and 0.982); one tau per cell does not suit every point in it.
- Reading: the boat can do the climatology-checking itself. A trailing harvest-over-climatology ratio chosen once (60 to 90 days) and a four-step tau table give a rule that beats the 7-day trailing-mean rule in 46 to 48 of 56 cells across a 6-point bias sweep, where any fixed rule wins in at most 36. It matches or beats the oracle that knows the true bias, which suggests the local, time-varying ratio (one point's anomaly, this season) carries information a single bias number does not. Using the ratio to correct the climatology term instead is the wrong move: it removes the caution the blend relies on.
- Limits: the tau table was read from 6zv's results on these same cells, so this tests whether an online estimate can realise the table, not the table out of sample (the oracle shares that advantage); the bias is a uniform scale; weights are PS's, not re-tuned; calm water, daily steps, NASA POWER 0.5 by 0.625 degrees, launched full each 1 January, one 4 m boat, five-year halves; P6's window ordering is on one grid of three windows.
- Next: a fresh-data test of the table and AT (other years from NASA POWER, or other points, with the table frozen); a seasonal rather than uniform bias, where a trailing ratio should help more; whether AT_90 or a continuous tau(eff) beats the four-step table; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-14 · Milestone 6zv: which battery feedback keeps the blend rule's payload margin when the climatology is wrong by 1 to 3%? (predictions in `experiments/run_bias_sweep.py`'s docstring, committed in fa2d37bde before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zu's "Next". 6zu found PS (W(0.5) tau 60 / W(0.5) tau 30 / W(0.375) tau 30 for 2.5 / 5 / 10-20 kWh at 50 W) lost its dark-hour margin over B where the test years were about 1% dimmer than the climatology, and read post hoc that one step shorter feedback (PSS: tau 30 / 20 / 20 / 20, same weights) kept it. Plan: cross climatology (2020-2024 plan with all of 2015-2019, half "T"; 2015-2019 with all of 2020-2024, half "R"), climatology multiplied by s in {0.97, ..., 1.03} before anything uses it (the estimate's K term and the 45-day-lead target every rule steers to, B included; B rerun at each s); 50 W, 4 packs, 30 points; PS, PSS, the default W(0.5) tau 30 and 6zt's 36-variant grid; 56 (s x half x pack) cells; "longest safe tau" = the longest tau at which some weight dominates B.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_bias_sweep.txt`, 3 min 14 s on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 6 new tests): **three of seven predictions held.** P1 held: PS dominates in 7 of 8 cells at s 0.98. P2 refuted: PS's count is not monotone in s but peaked, 4, 7, 8, 5, 4, 2, 0 for s 0.97 to 1.03: at the pessimistic end PS falls behind B on distance (km / B 0.947 to 0.989 on T at s 0.97). P3 refuted: PSS dominates in 36 of 56 cells against PS's 30, but in only 3, 4, 5 of 8 at s 0.97 to 0.99 (it gives up distance: km / B down to 0.929). P4 held: PSS dominates in 4 of 8 at s 1.03. P5 refuted: PSS km / B >= 1.03 in 33 of 56 cells (min 0.929). **P6 held: the longest safe tau is non-increasing in s in 8 of 8 (half x pack) series**, from 60 days at the pessimistic end to 30, 20, 10 or none (R / 20 kWh at s 1.03). P7 refuted: B's own km moves 0.7 to 7.3% across s, through its climatology-built target.
- Post hoc (`experiments/run_bias_sweep_posthoc.py`, `results_bias_sweep_posthoc.txt`, reads the results file, reruns nothing): the halves start from opposite realised biases (realised / climatology 1.0108 on T, 0.9893 on R), so T at s and R at s - 0.02 have nearly equal effective plan optimism (eff = s / realised). In those 16 matched (pack x bias) pairs PS's dominance agrees 15 times, PSS's 15, the default's 13, and the longest safe tau is equal in 11 and within one grid step in 16. Pooled by eff, PS dominates B between 0.969 and 1.001 to 1.011 (by pack), PSS between 0.979 and 1.019 to 1.031; longest safe tau is mostly 60 below eff 0.985, 30 at 0.985 to 1.015, 20 to 30 at 1.015 to 1.025, and 0 to 20 above.
- Reading: the blend rule's dominance over the trailing-mean rule is a window in the climatology's bias, 3 to 4 points of bias wide, not a one-sided safety margin. A climatology that promises too little makes the blend too cautious and B goes further; one that promises too much makes it overspend. A shorter feedback slides the window toward optimism by about one point rather than widening it much, which is why 6zu's shorter-tau advice was right on split R (eff 1.011) and would be wrong on a pessimistic climatology. Once the two halves are matched for effective bias they agree, so the years matter through their bias, not otherwise. For a designer: the feedback time should be chosen from how far the climatology may overstate the sun: about 60 days if it is known to understate by 2%, 30 if it is honest, 20 if it may overstate by 1 to 2%; past about 2.5% optimism nothing at 20 days or longer keeps the payload as lit as the plain 7-day rule in most cells.
- Limits: as 6zu (calm water, daily steps, NASA POWER 0.5 by 0.625 degrees, launched full each 1 January, one 4 m boat, five-year halves); the bias is a uniform multiplicative scale, while real climatology errors vary by season and region (Pacific + corridor 0.986 against Atlantic + corridor 0.998 on R in 6zu); eff uses each half's all-points ratio; window edges are read at a 0.01 step in s; the safe-tau weight is chosen on the scored cells (descriptive, in sample); the post hoc pairing was chosen after seeing the results.
- Next: let the boat estimate the bias itself (a trailing ratio of its harvest to the climatology, e.g. 30 to 90 days) and choose tau from the window above, tested pre-registered on the cross split with the bias sweep as a stress test; a seasonal rather than uniform bias; a payload-to-harvest-ratio formula for a; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-13 · Milestone 6zu: does 6zt's pack-scaled blend rule hold when time runs the other way? (predictions in `experiments/run_reverse_split.py`'s docstring, committed in a810a8568 before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zt's "Next". 6zt read a pack-scaled rule for a 50 W payload off its 2020 to 2024 test tables (PS: W(0.5) at tau 60 for 2.5 kWh, W(0.5) at tau 30 for 5 kWh, W(0.375) at tau 30 for 10 and 20 kWh, W(a) = a L + (1 - a) K), where it dominated the trailing-mean rule B in 12 of 12 (split x pack) cells, in sample. Plan: split R, the reverse of 6zt's time split: climatology from 2020 to 2024 only (strict history within those years for selection), test years 2015 to 2019 (climatology from all five of 2020 to 2024, so no test-year data enters any choice); PS scored unchanged on three test regions (all 30 points; 6zt's S1 test set, Atlantic + corridor; its S2 test set, Pacific + corridor); "R pick" = rule S over 6zt's 36-variant grid selected on 2020 to 2024. Everything else is 6zt's. 2015 to 2019 were 6zt's selection years (with their own climatology), so the pairing is new but the weather is not unseen by the programme.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_reverse_split.txt`, 5 min 3 s on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): **four of seven predictions held.** P1 refuted: PS dominates B on all 30 points at 1 of 4 50 W packs (20 kWh: 1.154 x B km at 0.90 x B's excess dark hours); at 2.5, 5 and 10 kWh it goes 12.6, 13.1 and 15.1% further but spends 1.16, 1.10 and 1.12 x B's dark hours. P2 refuted: 6 of 12 (region x pack) cells; all 4 Atlantic + corridor cells dominate (dark hours 0.77 to 0.98 x), only 20 kWh among Pacific + corridor (the others 1.14 to 1.22 x). P3 held: PS's km / B is 1.111 to 1.164 in 12 of 12 cells. **P4 held, and more than predicted: the R pick is PS exactly at all 4 packs** (a* 0.5, 0.5, 0.375, 0.375 and tau 60, 30, 30, 30), so the rule read off 2020 to 2024 test tables is what rule S picks when it selects on 2020 to 2024. P5 held trivially for the same reason (gaps 0.0000). P6 refuted: the fixed default W(0.5) at tau 30 dominates in 7 of 12 cells against PS's 6 (it wins at 2.5 kWh in all three regions with 0.68 to 0.89 x dark hours, and loses at 20 kWh / Pacific + corridor with 1.04 x). P7 held: mean a* is 0.094, 0.125, 0.438 at 10, 30, 50 W (6zt: 0.083, 0.167, 0.469).
- Post hoc (written after the predictions were scored; `experiments/run_reverse_split_bias.py`, `results_reverse_split_bias.txt`): on split R the test years' harvest is 0.989 x the climatology the boat plans with over all points, 0.986 x on Pacific + corridor and 0.998 x on Atlantic + corridor; on 6zt's split T it was 1.011, 1.014 and 1.002 x. The dominance failures sit exactly where the climatology is optimistic by more than 1%: the rule spends against sun that does not come. B (the 7-day trailing mean) does not use the climatology, so it is unaffected. In the all-points tables the knob that buys the margin back is tau, not a: one step shorter feedback (tau 30 for 2.5 kWh, 20 for the others, same weights) dominates B at all 4 packs (km / B 1.081, 1.072, 1.076, 1.074 at 0.86, 0.79, 0.77, 0.74 x dark hours), while no weight at PS's tau dominates at 2.5 or 10 kWh (at 5 kWh a = 0.25 at tau 30 does, 1.110 x km at 0.97 x); at tau 30 the dark hours are U-shaped in a with the minimum at 0.25 to 0.375, so less weight does not always mean more margin. 6zt's S1 failure at 20 kWh was also a tau failure.
