- Reading: the blend's weight and feedback are stable when time is reversed, to the grid step: tuning on 2020 to 2024 picks the same (a, tau) at every 50 W pack as the rule read from 6zt's tests, and the payload ordering of the weight replicates. Distance transfers too (11 to 16% over B on 2015 to 2019, more than on 2020 to 2024). What does not transfer is the payload-downtime margin, and it fails in a predictable direction: when the years flown are about 1% dimmer than the climatology, a 30- or 60-day battery feedback lets the boat overspend. A designer who cannot vouch for the climatology should shorten the feedback one step (20 days, 30 for a 2.5 kWh pack), which gave up 4 to 8 points of distance and kept the payload as lit as B on every pack here. This revises 6zt's advice ("take one step less weight"): on this split one step less weight at PS's tau dominates B only at 5 kWh (and at 20 kWh, where PS already does).
- Limits: as 6zt (calm water, daily steps, NASA POWER 0.5 by 0.625 degrees, launched full each 1 January, one 4 m boat, five-year windows); the climatology for 2015 to 2019 comes from later years, a stationarity check rather than a forecast; the shorter-tau rule and the 1% bias reading are post hoc and on two five-year windows only, so they need a split of their own (e.g. a climatology deliberately biased by plus or minus 1 to 3%); packs and payloads a sweep, not vehicles.
- Next: a bias-robustness sweep (scale the climatology by 0.97 to 1.03 and see which tau keeps dominance), which would turn the shorter-tau advice into a tested rule; a payload-to-harvest-ratio formula for a; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-13 · Milestone 6zt: does 6zs's half-and-half power estimate transfer across oceans, and is a = 0.5 a grid answer? (predictions in `experiments/run_blend_transfer.py`'s docstring, committed in db92a706c before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zs's "Next". 6zs's blend a L + (1 - a) K (L: 7-day trailing mean harvest, K: strict-history climatology) with a = 0.5 dominated the trailing-mean rule B at every 50 W pack, but only on split T and a three-step weight grid. Plan: 6zp's 12 configurations, gate dropped, W(a) for a in {0, 0.125, ..., 1} x tau {10, 20, 30, 60} (36 variants; W(0), W(0.25), W(0.5), W(0.75), W(1) are 6zs's K, B25, B50, B75, L, which a test checks), B = (L, 10, theta 1); three splits, all selecting on 2015 to 2019 and testing on 2020 to 2024: T (all 30 points), S1 (select on the 13 Pacific points, test on the 12 Atlantic points + 5 corridor sites), S2 (select Atlantic, test Pacific + corridor), so S1 and S2 are new in both place and year; rule S; G4 = the 6zs weights.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_blend_transfer.txt`, 6 min 22 s on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): **three of six predictions held.** P1 refuted: at 50 W the pick dominates B on test at 4 of 4 packs in S2 but 2 of 4 in S1; at 10 and 20 kWh in S1 it goes further (1.139 and 1.348 x B km) but spends 1.05 and 1.98 x B's excess dark hours. P2 held: a* is in [0.25, 0.75] in **12 of 12** 50 W cells (0.375 to 0.625; 0.5 in 7). P3 held: a* spans at most 0.125 across the three splits at every 50 W pack (0 at 5 kWh). P4 refuted: the finer grid adds nothing at 2.5, 5 and 20 kWh on split T but **+0.026 x B km at 10 kWh** (a* 0.375 against 0.25: 1.0718 against 1.0459, 0.66 x B's dark hours). P5 held: mean a* rises with the payload, 0.083 (10 W), 0.167 (30 W), 0.469 (50 W). P6 refuted: the fixed default W(0.5) at tau 30 dominates B at 2 of 4 50 W packs in S1 (fails at 10 and 20 kWh, dark hours 1.05 and 1.13 x B) and 3 of 4 in S2 (fails at 2.5 kWh, 0.994 x B km).
- Post hoc (read from the test tables after the predictions were scored; in sample, not a held-out result): the weight transfers and the dark-hour margin is what breaks. In both S1 failures the best-on-test variant has a 0.375 at tau 30, one step from the pick, and dominates B (10 kWh: 1.114 x km at 0.84 x dark hours; 20 kWh: 1.151 x at 0.98 x); the S1 20 kWh pick fails on tau (60), not on a. A pack-scaled rule, W(0.5) at tau 60 for 2.5 kWh, W(0.5) at tau 30 for 5 kWh, W(0.375) at tau 30 for 10 and 20 kWh, dominates B in **12 of 12** (split x pack) 50 W cells (km / B 1.020 to 1.151, dark hours 0.54 to 0.98 x B). At 10 W, rule S's pick misses dominance on 7 of the 7 S1 / S2 cells with B's excess above zero by small overshoots of B's few dark hours (1.02 to 1.18 x, and 2.46 x at 20 kWh in S2) while gaining 0.7 to 2.2% km; the light-payload picks are W(0) or W(0.125) in 17 of 24 (split x pack) cells at 10 and 30 W. Every weight's km and dark hours rise together down each column of the 50 W tables, so a is a dial along one frontier, and the Atlantic-and-corridor test set (S1, which includes the North Sea and Gulf of Alaska) sits further out on it than the Pacific one.
- Reading: the blend weight is a property of the payload-to-harvest ratio, not of the ocean it was tuned on: picked on the Pacific it lands within one eighth of where it lands picked on the Atlantic or on all points, near 0.5 for a payload above the mean harvest and near 0 for one well below it. What does not transfer cleanly is rule S's spending of B's dark-hour budget on a larger pack, where a step too much weight or too long a feedback overshoots that budget on a sunnier-than-selected test ocean. A designer who wants a margin should take one step less weight than the tuned value on packs of 10 kWh or more (0.375 here), which costs 2 to 3 points of distance and kept the payload as lit as B on every test set we have.
- Limits: as 6zs (calm water, daily steps, NASA POWER 0.5 by 0.625 degrees, launched full each 1 January, one 4 m boat, one five-year strict-history window); the space splits share the corridor sites as test points and both use the same years; weights are eight steps and tau four; the pack-scaled rule above was read off the test tables and needs its own held-out check; packs and payloads a sweep, not vehicles.
- Next: score the pack-scaled rule on a fresh split (e.g. select 2020 to 2024, test 2015 to 2019, or a latitude-band split); a payload-to-harvest-ratio formula for a so a designer need not tune it; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-13 · Milestone 6zs: for a small pack under a heavy payload, does a power estimate that keeps the climatology's season and the trailing mean's week cover the trailing-mean rule's distance? (predictions in `experiments/run_season_week.py`'s docstring, committed in 9b7907088 before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zr's "Next". With the gate dropped, the climatology rule (K) still covered only 0.928 and 0.962 x B's km at 50 W with 2.5 and 5 kWh packs. 6zr's untested reading: the trailing mean (L) moves on the sunny weeks inside a dull season, which climatology averages away. Plan: 6zp's 12 configurations, target lead 45, gate dropped (theta 0) for every grid variant, grid {K, L, M = max(K, L), B25, B50, B75 (a L + (1 - a) K), KA7 (K scaled by the last week's actual / climatological harvest, clipped to [0.5, 1.5])} x tau {10, 20, 30, 60} (28 variants), B = (L, 10, theta 1) unchanged, split T, rule S; "K pick" = rule S over the (K, tau, theta 0) column (a test checks that column and B against 6zr's `score`).
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_season_week.txt`, 3 min 27 s on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 3 new tests): **four of five predictions held.** P1 held: at 50 W the pick dominates B on test at **all 4 packs** (6zr: 2 of 4), test km / B 1.0385, 1.0523, 1.0459, 1.1214 for 2.5 to 20 kWh at 0.72, 0.69, 0.53, 0.94 x B's excess dark hours. P2 held: at 2.5 and 5 kWh the pick covers 1.0385 and 1.0523 x B (6zr: 0.928, 0.962), +0.111 and +0.090 x B over the K pick. P3 held: both picks are B50 (tau 60 and 30); every 50 W pick is a blend (B50 at three packs, B25 at 10 kWh). P4 held: at 10 and 30 W the pick is K itself in 5 of 8 cells and a blend (B25 or B50, tau 20) in 3, gaining +0.0010 to +0.0131 x B km; its excess dark hours exceed 1.2 x the K pick's in 2 of 8 (2.5 kWh / 10 W: 2.11 x; 5 kWh / 30 W: 1.98 x, both still below B's). P5 refuted narrowly: at 2.5 kWh / 50 W, M at tau 60 moves on 0.241 of test point-days against the K pick's 0.200 (+0.041, predicted >= 0.05).
- Post hoc (not predicted): the blend's distance does not come from moving more often. At 2.5 kWh / 50 W the pick (B50, 60) moves on 0.210 of test point-days against (K, 60)'s 0.200, a 5% rise in moving days for 12% more km, so most of the gain is speed on days it moves. Both pure estimators sit on a worse frontier than the blend: on the selection years at 2.5 kWh / 50 W, (K, 60) uses 0.99 x B's excess dark hours for 1.004 x B's km, (B50, 60) 0.94 x for 1.095 x; on test (L, 10) at theta 0 gives 1.001 x km at 1.00 x dark hours, and longer-tau (L) and every (M) and (KA7) above tau 10 buy km only with 1.3 to 5.4 x B's dark hours. M = max(K, L) is the worst spender: the max of two estimates is biased high, so it spends the pack before a dull spell. KA7 (a 7-day anomaly on the season) tracks (L) closely (within 0.021 x B km at every tau at 2.5 and 20 kWh / 50 W), so the multiplicative anomaly lets the week dominate; the additive half-and-half blend is what keeps the season.
- Reading: for a heavy payload on a small pack, plan speed from the average of the climatological and the last week's harvest, not either alone, and close the pack error over 30 to 60 days. That rule beats the 7-day trailing-mean rule on distance (by 4 to 12%) and payload downtime (0.53 to 0.94 x) at every 50 W pack, closing the gap 6zp to 6zr left open. Where the deck carries the payload (10 and 30 W), plain climatology stays the pick at 5 of 8 cells and a blend adds at most 1.3%, so one rule can serve every payload only with care: (B25, 20) is within -0.0014 to +0.0131 x B km of the K pick in all 8 light-payload cells but reaches 1.03 x B's excess dark hours in 2 of them (read from the grids, not a pre-registered test); the safer design keeps climatology below the mean harvest and switches to (B50) above it. This revises the 6zo reading that "letting recent weather back in does not help": it does not help as a multiplicative 30-day anomaly (6zo's KA) or as a 7-day one (KA7 here), but an additive equal-weight blend does, once the payload exceeds the mean harvest.
- Limits: as 6zr (calm water, daily steps, NASA POWER 0.5 by 0.625 degrees, launched full each 1 January, one 4 m boat, one five-year strict-history window, split T only); blend weights are three steps (0.25, 0.5, 0.75) and tau four, so a = 0.5 is a grid answer; best-on-test differs from the pick in 6 of 12 cells (by one grid step in weight or tau in 3, two in the other 3); packs and payloads a sweep, not vehicles; at 2.5 kWh / 50 W the picked tau (60) is the grid's edge.
- Next: a space split (6zo's S1 / S2) for the blend at 50 W, to see whether a = 0.5 transfers across oceans; a finer weight grid with a in the selection; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-13 · Milestone 6zr: is the heavy-payload loss a gate problem? If the climatology speed rule may move when its power estimate clears a threshold below the hotel load, does it cover the trailing-mean rule's distance at 50 W? (predictions in `experiments/run_hotel_gate.py`'s docstring, committed in bba7c8e58 before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zq's "Next". 6zp and 6zq found the climatology estimate (K) short of the 7-day trailing mean (L) on distance at 50 W with 2.5 to 10 kWh packs, and no lead or tau fixed it. 6zp's post hoc reading: the speed rule holds station whenever the estimate f(d) is below the hotel load, and smooth climatology sits below 50 W for whole seasons. Plan: 6zp's 12 configurations, target lead 45 unchanged, gate p = hotel if f < theta x hotel else f + (SOC - T) / (tau 24), grid (K) x tau {10, 20, 30, 60} x theta {1, 0.75, 0.5, 0.25, 0} (20 variants; theta 1 is 6zp's rule, a test checks it against 6zp's `score`), B = (L, 10, theta 1), split T, rule S.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_hotel_gate.txt`, 2 min 58 s on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 3 new tests): **two of five predictions held.** P1 refuted: at 50 W the pick dominates B on test at 2 of 4 packs (10 and 20 kWh: 1.0036 and 1.0513 x B km at 0.49 and 0.61 x B's excess dark hours; 6zp and 6zq: 1 of 4), but at 2.5 and 5 kWh it still covers 0.928 and 0.962 x B. P2 held: theta* = 0.75 at all four 50 W packs. P3 refuted: at 10 W the lower gate adds +0.0000, +0.0002, +0.0027 and +0.0077 x B km for 2.5 to 20 kWh, so the gate costs distance even on a light payload once the pack is big. P4 held: theta 0 at the 6zp pick's tau gains +0.0049, +0.0113, +0.0153 and +0.0204 x B km at 50 W. P5 refuted: the 6zp pick's gate binds on 0.778 of test point-days at 2.5 kWh / 50 W (climatology below 50 W), the pick's (below 37.5 W) on 0.469, more than half of it.
- Post hoc (not predicted): at 50 W, every bit of the gain is between theta 1 and 0.75; theta 0.5, 0.25 and 0 give the same km as 0.75 to four decimals in all 16 (pack, tau) cells. That follows from the rule: with the gate gone the boat moves only when f + (SOC - T) / (24 tau) exceeds the hotel load, and that boost is at most (C - T) / (24 tau) <= 0.5 C / (24 tau), 0.9 to 42 W across the grid, so a day whose climatology is far below the payload stays at station gate or no gate. Where the reach is large (20 kWh, tau 10 to 30 at 10 W) theta below 0.75 does add km (up to +0.0048). Gate or no gate, (K) holds station about as often as (L) (B's gate binds on 0.755 of 50 W test point-days), so (L)'s distance edge at small packs is not about how often it moves.
- Reading: the gate is a real but small cost: a designer should drop it (theta 0 is never worse than 0.75 by more than 0.0011 x B km in any of the 48 cells and is the rule with one fewer parameter) and keep the pack feedback. That repairs the 10 kWh / 50 W boat and leaves 2.5 and 5 kWh 7% and 4% short of the trailing mean. On a small pack the feedback cannot carry the boat through a below-payload season, because its reach is a fraction of a watt to a few watts, so what is left is when the boat spends its energy; the likely reason (not measured here) is that (L) moves on the sunny weeks inside a dull season, which climatology averages away.
- Limits: as 6zq (calm water, daily steps, NASA POWER 0.5 by 0.625 degrees, launched full each 1 January, one 4 m boat, one five-year strict-history window, split T only); lead 45 and the 0.5 C floor kept; five thetas and four taus; packs and payloads a sweep, not vehicles. The reach bound (C - T) / (24 tau) is the rule's algebra; that T sits near C at 50 W, which would make the realised reach smaller still, was not measured.
- Next: for small packs under heavy payloads, an estimate that keeps (K)'s season and (L)'s week, e.g. max(K, L) or a blend, scored on the same split; a space split for the pack-scaled tau; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-13 · Milestone 6zq: once the target's lead is re-tuned per pack and hotel load, does the lead carry 6zp's configuration dependence and settle tau, and does it repair the heavy-payload regime? (predictions in `experiments/run_lead_retune.py`'s docstring, committed in 32fbc4a55 before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zp's "Next". 6zp kept the target T = lead(max(0.5 C, Rc), 45 days) whose 45-day lead was chosen at 10 kWh / 30 W, and found tau_K moving with the configuration. A full refill takes time proportional to C / surplus, so the lead, not tau, might be what should rescale, and a mis-set lead could have been absorbed by tau. Plan: 6zp's 12 configurations, grid (K) x tau {10, 20, 30, 60} x lead {0, 15, 30, 45, 60, 90} days (24 variants), B = (L, 10, lead 45) unchanged, split T (select 2015 to 2019, score 2020 to 2024, strict-history climatology), rule S over the grid; "6zp pick" = rule S over the lead-45 column, which reproduces 6zp's tau_K in all 12 (a test checks the lead-45 column against 6zp's `score`).
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_lead_retune.txt`, 2 min 51 s on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): **three of six predictions held.** P1 held: lead* moves with the pack at every hotel load (10 W: 30, 45, 60, 60 days for 2.5, 5, 10, 20 kWh; 30 W: 30, 30, 30, 60; 50 W: 0, 30, 45, 30). P2 refuted, by the 50 W row only: lead* is non-decreasing in C at 10 and 30 W, but at 50 W it peaks at 10 kWh and is 0 at 2.5 kWh. **P3 refuted: tau does not settle.** tau* = 20 in 3 of 12 (6zp: 5 of 12); with the lead free, tau* *also* rises with the pack (10 W: 10, 20, 20, 30; 30 W: 10, 10, 20, 30; 50 W: 30 at every pack), so lead and tau do not trade off; a bigger pack wants both a longer lead and a slower feedback. **P4 refuted: re-tuning does not repair 50 W.** It adds 1.8 to 2.5 points of km / B at 2.5, 5 and 20 kWh (0.923 to 0.947, 0.951 to 0.969, 1.031 to 1.056) but the pick still covers less than B at 2.5, 5 and 10 kWh (0.947, 0.969, 0.988), so it dominates B at 1 of 4 packs, as in 6zp. P5 held: at 10 and 30 W the re-tuned pick gains less than 0.01 x B km over the 6zp pick in 8 of 8 (largest +0.0081 at 20 kWh / 30 W, where its dark hours rise from 0.66 to 0.995 x B). P6 held: at 10 kWh / 30 W, lead* is 30 (pick (K, 20, 30): 1.0368 x B km at 0.69 x dark hours against lead 45's 1.0349 at 0.56).
- Post hoc (not predicted): at 10 and 30 W, tau* grows roughly as the square root of the pack (2.5 to 20 kWh is 8x; tau* 10 to 30 is 3x, sqrt 8 = 2.8), not linearly as storage D would have it; four packs and a three-step tau grid cannot tell a square root from a log, so this is a description, not a law. Rule S spends any freed slack on km up to B's dark-hour budget: in 9 of 12 cells the re-tuned pick's test dark hours are higher than the 6zp pick's (equal in 2, lower in 1), so the gain is a trade, not free. The best-on-test variant differs from the pick in 8 of 12 cells, by one grid step in lead or tau in 4 of them and two steps in the other 4.
- Reading: the 45-day lead is close enough for a payload the deck can carry: freeing it buys at most 0.8% more distance, usually 0.2%, and that by moving along the km / dark-hour frontier rather than up it. So 6zo's rule stays as designed, with one adjustment a designer can apply: scale the feedback with the pack (about 10 days at 2.5 kWh, 20 at 5 to 10 kWh, 30 at 20 kWh) and lead the target 30 days, 60 for a 20 kWh pack. The heavy-payload regime is not a tuning problem: no lead or tau in the grid makes the climatology rule cover B's distance at 2.5 to 10 kWh with 50 W, which points back to 6zp's post hoc reading (the rule holds station whenever average sun is below the payload draw) and to the estimator, not the target, as the next thing to change.
- Limits: as 6zp (calm water, daily steps, NASA POWER 0.5 by 0.625 degrees, launched full each 1 January, one 4 m boat, one five-year strict-history window, split T only); the 0.5 C floor was not re-tuned; leads are six steps and tau four, so lead* and tau* are grid-resolution answers; pack and hotel values are a sweep, not vehicles.
- Next: for the heavy-payload regime, a power estimate that moves on climatology above a lower threshold than the hotel load (or a de-trended climatology); a space split for the pack-scaled tau; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-12 · Milestone 6zp: is 6zo's 20-day pack feedback a property of the pack's days of storage, or does it hold for other packs and payloads? (predictions in `experiments/run_pack_hotel_tau.py`'s docstring, committed in 68aaf22d2 before any configuration was run)
- Why: 6zo's "Next". Every step from 6zj to 6zo used one 10 kWh pack and a 30 W hotel load; a designer with a different pack or payload needs to know whether to keep tau = 20 days or rescale it. Plan: pack C in {2.5, 5, 10, 20} kWh x hotel {10, 30, 50} W (12 configurations, storage D = C / 24 hotel from 2.1 to 83 days; swept ranges, not sourced figures), grid {L, K} x tau {5, 10, 20, 30, 45, 60, 90}, 6zo's split T (select 2015 to 2019, score 2020 to 2024, strict-history climatology) and rule S, the lead-45 target rebuilt for each C and hotel, (Fh) on a C / 100 pack grid.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_pack_hotel_tau.txt`, about 7 min on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): **one of six predictions held** (P6: the 10 kWh / 30 W cell picks (K, 20) again, reproducing 6zo). The storage reading is refuted (P1, P2): tau_K is **20 days in 5 of the 8 configurations at 10 and 30 W** and 10 in the other three (2.5 kWh at 10 and 30 W, 5 kWh at 30 W), across a 24-fold range of D, so tau_K / D runs from 0.24 to 2.9; at 50 W it is 30 at three packs and 60 at 2.5 kWh. P3 refuted narrowly (9 of 12 dominate B on the test years, km >= B and excess dark h <= B): every configuration at 10 and 30 W and 20 kWh / 50 W do; 2.5, 5 and 10 kWh at 50 W lose 1 to 8% of km while cutting dark hours to 0.45 to 0.66 x. P4 refuted the other way: the climatology rule's gain *grows* with the pack (km / B 1.006 / 1.012 / 0.923 at 2.5 kWh against 1.012 / 1.037 / 1.031 at 20 kWh, for 10 / 30 / 50 W), as does foresight's (1.015 to 1.072 x B at 10 W, 1.048 to 1.123 x at 30 W). P5 refuted (7 of 12): at 10 and 30 W, (K, 20) is within 1% of the pick's km or better (5 kWh / 30 W: 1.029 against the pick's 1.018, at 0.87 x B's dark hours, failing the criterion only by going further) with at most 1.20 x B's dark hours in all 8 cells; at 50 W it gives 4 to 5% less km than the pick.
- Post hoc (not predicted): at 50 W the deck harvest averages 36 W over the 30 points, below the hotel load, so the boat moves only on the sunny tail. There (K, tau_K)'s km / B falls 4 to 8 points from the selection years to the test years (10 kWh, (K, 30): 1.062 to 0.988; 20 kWh: 1.070 to 1.031) while (L)'s barely moves; the test years' harvest is 1.1% above the 2015 to 2019 climatology at the same points, and (K) holds station whenever the climatology is below the hotel load, so a small low bias in the climatology removes many of the few moving days. This is consistent with the drop but was not tested separately.
- Reading: for a payload the deck can carry on average (10 and 30 W here), 6zo's design rule transfers unchanged across an 8-fold range of pack size: plan speed from the day-of-year sun and close the pack error over about 20 days (10 for a pack of 5 kWh or less); the constant is not set by days of storage. The bigger the pack, the more both the climatology rule and perfect foresight gain over the trailing-mean rule, so a pack that buffers more needs a better plan, not a less careful one. A payload heavier than the mean harvest is a different regime, in which the climatology rule buys payload uptime with distance and is sensitive to a year-on-year sun trend of about 1%.
- Limits: as 6zo (calm water, daily steps, NASA POWER 0.5 by 0.625 degrees, launched full each 1 January, one 4 m boat, strict history one five-year window); the target's 45-day lead and 0.5 C floor were chosen at 10 kWh / 30 W and not re-tuned per configuration; pack and hotel values are a sweep, not sourced vehicle figures; split T only (no space split).
- Next: re-tune the target lead per configuration to see whether it, rather than tau, carries the pack dependence; a de-trended or 30-day-anomaly-corrected climatology for the heavy-payload regime; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj); within-day timing.

## 2026-11-12 · Milestone 6zo: does the climatology power estimate keep its gain out of sample? (predictions in `experiments/run_power_estimate_holdout.py`'s docstring, committed in efd616485 before any split was run)
- Why: 6zn's "Next". 6zn's post hoc rule (K), planning speed from today's climatological harvest instead of the 7-day trailing mean (L), gave 1.022 x km at 0.27 x excess dark hours, and (K30) 1.050 x km, but four variants were read off the 300 point-years they were scored on, with a leave-one-year-out climatology that let a test year see later years' sun. Plan: a fixed grid of 24 variants, estimator {L, L30, K, KA} x pack-feedback tau {10, 20, 30, 45, 60, 90} days, where (KA) is new: climatology scaled by the last 30 days' actual / climatological harvest, clipped to [0.5, 1.5]. Selection rule S: most km with excess dark hours at most the baseline B = (L, 10)'s. 6zm's three splits: T (select 2015 to 2019, score 2020 to 2024, strict-history climatology from 2015 to 2019 only) and space splits S1 / S2 (Pacific / Atlantic transect). (Fh) as the bound on every test set. Same data and 6zm's lead-45 target, built from the same climatology as each variant.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_power_estimate_holdout.txt`, about 3 min on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): **all six predictions held.** Split T picks **(K, 20)**: on the unseen years 2020 to 2024 it covers **1.035 x B's km at 0.56 x B's excess dark hours** (744 against 1,334) and closes 35% of the gap to (Fh) (P1, P2, P6). (K, 10) under strict history keeps 6zn's in-sample result: 1.018 x km at 0.26 x dark hours (P3; in sample 1.022 and 0.27). S1 picks (K, 20): 1.036 x km at 0.54 x; S2 picks (K, 30): 1.055 x km at 1.17 x (P5). (KA) and (K) at their own best tau differ by 0.06% in km (P4), but (KA) has 43% more excess dark hours.
- Descriptive: on every selection and test set each (K) variant dominates the (L) and (L30) variant of the same tau on both counts, and the (K) row is the efficient frontier of the grid; the trailing-mean rules buy km only with dark hours (L, 20 on split T's test: 1.019 x km at 1.79 x). By band (split T test, (K, 20)): 1.026 x km within 20 degrees, 1.03 to 1.04 at 25 to 45, 1.08 to 1.11 at 50 to 60, with mean excess dark hours per year cut from 9 to 18 to 6 to 10. (Fh) has no excess dark hours anywhere and still covers 1.04 x (tropics) to 1.39 x (60 degrees) B's km, so most of the gap at high latitude is left.
- Reading: the climatology power estimate is a real, out-of-sample improvement, not a post hoc artefact, and the feedback constant transfers across time and ocean (20 days on two splits, 30 on the third). Letting recent weather back in, even as a clipped 30-day anomaly, does not help: it gains nothing in km and costs payload uptime, which fits 6zn's finding that weather in the speed costs evenness. The pick on S2 (tau 30) shows rule S's tolerance: it lands 17% over B in dark hours on the test set, so tau 20 is the safer default.
- Limits: as 6zn (calm water, daily steps, NASA POWER 0.5 by 0.625 degrees, launched full each 1 January, one boat, one 10 kWh pack, 30 W hotel); strict history is one five-year window; the target and the power estimate share one climatology, so a biased climatology (a trend, a volcanic year) would hit both; the grid is 24 variants fixed in advance, and S2 shows the pick moving by one step.
- Next: the sweep of pack size and hotel load under (K, 20), to see whether the tau that transfers is a property of the pack's days of storage; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj); within-day timing.

## 2026-11-12 · Milestone 6zn: is foresight's distance edge energy or speed timing, and does a sun-aware speed rule win it back? (predictions in `experiments/run_speed_timing.py`'s docstring, committed in 20d69c30a before any policy was run)
- Why: 6zm's "Next". After 6zl and 6zm the DP plan (F) still covers 6 to 16% more than the causal rule in every band, 5.7% even within 20 degrees where nothing goes dark, and the log called that "the timing of speed (spending on the sunny days)". The load is convex in speed, so by Jensen even spending maximises distance for a given energy, and sun-following should pay only where it saves energy (spill at a full pack, charge left at year end). Plan: account each policy's energy (harvest, spill, end pack, propulsion P), define the even-spend distance K(P) and timing efficiency eta = km / K(P), split the gap to (F) into an energy term K(P_F) - K(P_L) and a timing term, and test persistence (P), a perfect day-ahead forecast (O), spill recapture (S) and (OS), all on 6zm's picked target (lead 45). Bounds (F) and (Fh), the latter required to end the year with at least 0.5 C. Same 300 point-years and data as 6zl; no new data.
- Correction: the committed code sent (S) and (OS) to (O)'s power estimate (`rule[0]`); fixed before any write-up (`DispatchTests`), every other number unchanged. Recorded in the docstring.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_speed_timing.txt`, a few minutes on 4 cores, stdlib, deterministic; 9 new tests). **P1 refuted:** the energy term is **21%** of the (L) to (F) gap (141,777 of 672,506 km), and against (Fh) only 4% (25,300 of 573,886): (Fh) spends 27.13 MWh of propulsion energy, (L) 27.00. **P2 refuted:** what (F) gains in energy is mostly the pack it empties at year end (1.04 MWh), not spill (0.11 MWh); spill is 0.13% of harvest under (L). **P3 refuted:** (S) closes 2% of the gap to (Fh) (km 1.002 x (L), same dark hours). **P4 held:** (O) is worse than (S). **P5 refuted:** persistence loses **4.6%** of (L)'s km and doubles the excess dark hours (5,680 against 2,681). **P6 refuted:** eta (L) 0.748, (F) 0.819; within 20 degrees 0.948 against 0.991. **P7 refuted:** within 20 degrees (S) closes 0.3%.
- Descriptive: a perfect one-day-ahead forecast (O) loses 3.6% of (L)'s km (2.7% in the tropics, 7 to 9% at 40 to 55 degrees) and adds 54% to the excess dark hours. The simple rule (Y) uses 1.1 MWh more energy than (L) because its target sits at 0.5 C, and wins 3% km, at 12 times the dark hours.
- Reading: foresight's edge is not spending on sunny days. It is the opposite: (F) spreads its energy more evenly than any causal rule here, and every rule that tracks the day's sun more closely goes less far, because a convex load makes uneven speed expensive. The 7-day trailing mean in (L) already lets too much weather into the speed.
- Post hoc (`post_hoc()`, written after reading P1 to P7): plan power from a smoother estimate, same target and feedback. (L30) 30-day trailing mean: 1.021 x km, 1.8 x dark hours. **(K) today's climatological harvest** (the leave-one-year-out curve the target is built from): **1.022 x km at 0.27 x (L)'s excess dark hours** (737 against 2,681), better on both. (K30) the same with a 30-day pack feedback: **1.050 x km, 1.09 x dark hours**, half the gap to (Fh); (K60): 1.066 x km, 3.0 x dark hours (at 60 degrees it matches (Fh)'s km). Four variants on the same 300 point-years: in sample.
- Limits: as 6zm (calm water, daily steps, NASA POWER 0.5 by 0.625 degrees, launched full each 1 January, one boat, one 10 kWh pack, 30 W hotel); the climatology is leave-one-year-out over ten years (6zm found five earlier years do as well for the target, not yet checked for (K)); the daily step hides within-day timing, where a real controller also chooses speed against sun hour by hour; (F)'s grid is 100 Wh and 50 Wh/day.
- Next: a held-out check of (K) and the feedback constant (pick tau on 2015 to 2019 with strict-history climatology, score 2020 to 2024, as 6zm); then the sweep of pack size and hotel load; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-12 · Milestone 6zm: does 6zl's led rule curve keep its gain on years and places it was not picked on? (predictions in `experiments/run_led_curve_holdout.py`'s docstring, committed in 9b17a743e before any split was run)
- Why: 6zl's first "Next". Its best variants (lead 30 days, or 10% haircut and lead 20) were chosen post hoc from 12 on the same 300 point-years they were scored on, and every target came from a leave-one-year-out climatology that used later years' sun. Plan: a wider grid fixed in advance (haircut 1.0, 0.9, 0.8 x lead 0, 10, 20, 30, 45, 60 days), one selection rule for every split (most km among variants whose excess dark hours are at most (R)'s on the selection set), and three splits. T: select on 2015 to 2019 with climatologies from 2015 to 2019 only, score on 2020 to 2024 with every target built from 2015 to 2019 (strict history: what a planner launching in 2020 would have had). S1 and S2: select on one meridian, score on the other plus the five corridor sites. Same boat, pack, hotel, causal speed rule and data as 6zk; no new data, no (F).
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_led_curve_holdout.txt`, about 4 min, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests). **P1 held:** split T picks haircut 1.0, lead 45; on the held-out years its excess dark hours are **1,334** against (R)'s 1,409 (0.95 x). **P2 held:** its km at 40 degrees or more is **1.068 x** (R)'s (1.037 at 40 to 45, 1.117 at 50 to 55, 1.115 at 60). **P3 held:** S1 picks (0.8, 10): excess 0.84 x (R), km40 1.041 x; S2 picks (1.0, 30): excess 1.09 x (R), km40 1.084 x. **P4 refuted:** split T picks lead 45, not 20 or 30 (lead 30 had 1,538 against (R)'s 1,526 on the selection years, just over the line). **P5 held:** strict-history (R) has 1,409 excess dark hours on 2020 to 2024 against 1,422 from the leave-one-year-out climatology (0.99 x): five prior years are as good a climatology as nine. **P6 refuted:** in the tropics the haircut-0.8 variants differ from (R) at one point, P-20 (20 S 150 W), in every test year, by **+0.4 to +1.2 thousand km a year (more, not less)**; there the 20% haircut lifts the winter climatology below the 30 W hotel load and the target above the 0.5 C floor on 96 days, and the extra charge carried into the austral winter is spent as distance. The picked variant (1.0, 45) equals (R) in every tropical point-year.
- Descriptive: on every test set the whole lead 30 to 45 row and the haircut-0.9, lead-20 to 30 cells sit within 0.85 to 1.09 x (R)'s excess dark hours and 1.05 to 1.08 x its km40, so the result does not hang on the exact pick. 6zl's in-sample (1.0, 30) on the split-T test years: excess 1.01 x (R), km40 1.074 x; (0.9, 20): 0.95 x and 1.066 x. Unled (lead 0, haircut 1.0) still has 3.6 to 4.0 x (R)'s excess dark hours on every test set, so the lead is what makes the two-sided curve usable.
- Reading: the 6zl post hoc result holds out of sample. A pack target from the finite-reservoir rule curve, led by 30 to 45 days and built from only the five previous years of NASA POWER sun, keeps the payload as lit as 6zk's full-pack curve (0.84 to 1.09 x its avoidable dark hours across three splits) and adds 4 to 8% to the distance a 4 m deck-solar boat covers at 40 degrees or more (11 to 14% at 50 to 60 degrees). That is a rule a planner can program today without a forecast. It still leaves most of the foresight gap (6zl: (F) covers 6 to 16% more than the simple rule in every band), which is in the timing of speed, not the pack target.
- Limits: as 6zl (calm water, daily steps, NASA POWER 0.5 by 0.625 degrees, launched full each 1 January, one boat, one 10 kWh pack, 30 W hotel); the time split has five training and five test years; the space splits share years between selection and test; the 10-day refill constant and the 0.5 C floor are still fixed.
- Next: a speed rule that spends on forecast-sunny days (a 1 to 3 day forecast or the trailing-harvest shape) against (F), now with (1.0, 45) as the pack target; sweep pack size and hotel load to see where the lead stops mattering; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-12 · Milestone 6zl: does a capacity-aware (two-sided) rule curve win back the distance 6zk's reserve curve gave up at high latitude, and what does it cost in payload dark hours? (predictions in `experiments/run_two_sided_curve.py`'s docstring, committed in 105ab869b before any policy was run)
- Why: 6zk's "Next". Its curve clipped the reserve to the pack after the backward recursion, so wherever the winter shortfall exceeds 10 kWh (every point at 40 degrees or more) the target was the full pack all year. Clipping inside the recursion, Rc(d) = min(C, max(0, Rc(d+1) + 24 hotel - hc(d))), is the finite-storage form of the rule-curve calculation: the target rises to C only as many days before the deficit season as the climatological surplus needs to refill it, and falls away again before the spring recovery. Policies, all 6zk's causal structure with only T(d) changed: (C) T = max(0.5 C, Rc) from the leave-one-year-out climatology; (C0) no floor; (Cm) built from a 10% sun haircut; (C1) own-year oracle. Same 300 point-years, data and references ((Y), (R), (R1), idle, (F)) as 6zk; no new data.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_two_sided_curve.txt`, about 4 min, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests). **P1 refuted:** at 40 degrees or more (C) covers **1.071** times (R)'s km, not 1.15 (by band 0.918 to 0.927 of (Y) at 40 to 45, 0.720 to 0.833 at 50 to 55, 0.652 to 0.786 at 60). **P2 refuted:** (C)'s summed excess dark hours are **11,211**, 3.8 times (R)'s 2,937. **P3 held:** still 65% below (Y)'s 31,664. **P4 refuted:** (C) closes **0.217** of the km gap from (R) to (F) at 40 degrees or more, not half. **P5 held:** no floor (C0) adds 2.3% km and lifts excess dark hours to 29,509, close to (Y). **P6 held:** the 10% haircut (Cm) halves (C)'s excess (5,237) and still covers more than (R) (5,799,662 against 5,744,506 km). **P7 held:** the own-year oracle (C1) has 10,729 excess dark hours, 96% of (C)'s. **P8 held:** tropics identical to (R) and (Y).
- Descriptive: in 2024 the full-pack days at 40 degrees or more fall from 207 to 366 under (R) to 109 to 224 under (C); (C)'s target leaves C on day 22 to 71 of the year in the north and day 206 to 258 in the south. At 30 degrees the gain is nil or negative (P+30 211,110 against 213,043 km; A-30 176,193 against 177,676): there (R) is already below C most of the year.
- Post hoc (`post_hoc()`, written after reading P1 to P8 and appended to the results): (C1) shows the dark hours are not a climatology error, so the suspect is the policy's 10-day time constant (planned power h7 + (SOC - T)/240 h) against a target that climbs from 0.5 C to C over the last few weeks before the deficit season. Leading the target by L days (T'(d) = max T over d to d + L) over a 3 x 4 grid of haircut {1.0, 0.9, 0.8} and L {0, 10, 20, 30}: haircut 1.0 and L 30 gives **2,954** excess dark hours (R: 2,937) with **7.4% more km than (R) at 40 degrees or more** (1,108,086 against 1,031,446) and 1.5% more overall; haircut 0.9 and L 20 gives 2,763, below (R), at +1.4% overall. That variant was picked from 12 on the same 300 point-years, so it is an in-sample result and needs a held-out check before it is a claim.
- Reading: putting the pack's capacity inside the rule curve is the right correction in principle, and the descriptive numbers show the target now falls away for 4 to 8 months a year at high latitude. But on its own it bought distance with payload time at a poor rate (+7% km at 40 degrees or more for 3.8 times the avoidable dark hours), because the causal speed rule cannot fill the pack as fast as the new curve rises. A curve led by 30 days recovers (R)'s dark hours and keeps the distance, in sample. Even so, the two-sided curve closes only about a fifth of the gap to foresight. (F) covers 5.7% more than (Y) even within 20 degrees, where no policy ever goes dark, so most of foresight's distance edge is the within-year timing of speed (spending on the sunny days), which no pack target supplies.
- Limits: as 6zk (calm water, daily steps, NASA POWER 0.5 by 0.625 degrees, launched full each 1 January, one boat, ten-year climatology); the lead and haircut were chosen post hoc on the same data; the policy's 10-day time constant and 0.5 C floor were not swept.
- Next: a held-out check of the led curve (pick L and haircut on 2015 to 2019, score on 2020 to 2024, or on one meridian and score the other); a speed rule that spends on forecast-sunny days (a 1 to 3 day forecast or the trailing-harvest shape) to attack the 6 to 16% foresight gap that pack targets leave; the Wave Glider payload budget (open since 6zj).

## 2026-11-12 · Milestone 6zk: can a causal pack-target curve built from other years' sun keep the payload as lit as a vehicle that never moves, and what distance does it cost? (predictions in `experiments/run_reserve_curve.py`'s docstring, committed in 100b7e7a before the 2015 to 2023 transect sun was fetched)
- Why: 6zj's "Next". Its causal rule (Y) kept a fixed 0.5 C buffer and so entered the dark season part-empty: more dark days than the idle vehicle at every point from 30 degrees out. Rules: 6zj's boat, pack, hotel, step and (Y) structure, one mission per year launched full on 1 January, 2015 to 2024 at 6zj's 30 points (300 point-years; NASA POWER, 2015 to 2023 transect fetched by `fetch_sun_years.py`, no fill values). Only the pack target T(d) changes: (Y) 0.5 C; (E) full from the autumn to the spring equinox; (R) a reservoir-style rule curve, T = min(C, max(0.5 C, R(d))) with R the circular backward cumulative shortfall of a leave-one-year-out, 31-day-smoothed climatological harvest below the hotel load; (R1) the same from the test year's own smoothed sun (oracle diagnostic). Before the run, the generic policy with T = 0.5 C was checked to reproduce 6zj's (Y) exactly (also a test).
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_reserve_curve.txt`, about 4 min, stdlib, deterministic; 6 new tests). **P1 held:** summed excess dark hours over the idle vehicle (R) **2,937** against (Y) **31,664** (ratio 0.093). **P2 held:** where the idle vehicle has no down day, (R) has none in 134 of 137 point-years (misses at A+30 and Florida-Straits). **P3 held:** within 20 degrees (R)'s km equals (Y)'s exactly (the curve sits at 0.5 C). **P4 held:** (E) 24,630, a 22% cut. **P5 refuted:** median (R) km / (Y) km at 30 degrees or more is **0.840**, not 0.90 or more; by band 0.967 at 25 to 35, 0.918 at 40 to 45, 0.720 at 50 to 55, 0.652 at 60. **P6 held:** (R1) 2,767, so a leave-one-year-out climatology is within 6% of the year's own sun. **P7 held:** within 24 h of the idle minimum in 119 of 140 point-years at 40 degrees or more. **P8 held:** in 2024 (R) cuts (Y)'s down days at all 18 points with a gap of 10 or more (e.g. P-30 76 to 58, idle 54; P+30 16 to 0; Gulf-of-Maine 101 to 79, idle 77).
- Descriptive: at points where the idle vehicle ever goes dark, its down days vary by 14 to 42 across the ten years (P+30 0 to 14, A-30 16 to 42, P+60 167 to 209; A+30 never dark); 2024 was the worst of the ten at A-40 (133) and one day off the worst at P+40 (102 against 103), so a single-year verdict at 40 degrees can be a bad year's. (F) still covers 6 to 16% more than (Y) in every band while matching the idle minimum, so (R) covers 0.84 (40 to 45 degrees) down to 0.62 (60 degrees) of (F)'s km: the distance gap to foresight is 16 to 38% there.
- Post hoc (`post_hoc()`, written after reading P1 to P8, appended to the results): at 50 to 60 degrees the climatological mean harvest is 17.5 to 24.3 W, below the 30 W hotel load, and the winter shortfall the pack would have to bridge is 109 to 232 kWh against 10 kWh, so (R)'s target is the full pack on all 366 days; at 40 degrees mean harvest is 29.5 to 33.0 W (at the hotel load) and T = C on 207 to 326 days. So at high latitude the rule curve degenerates to "keep the pack full", which is why (R) and (R1) agree there to the hour and why the distance cost is large: the boat only moves on surplus a full pack cannot store.
- Reading: a planner can program the payload-first part of the foresight plan from climatology alone. A ten-year, out-of-sample check says a reservoir-style rule curve gets the payload's dark time to within a day of the physical minimum in most years, where a calendar rule does not, and that the climatology error is small. What it cannot do causally is (F)'s distance: (F) knows when a full pack is no longer needed (the run-up to spring) and spends it; (R) holds the pack full whenever the year as a whole is short. The next lever is a curve that also counts forward to the spring recovery, not a better climatology.
- Limits: as 6zj (calm water, daily steps, NASA POWER 0.5 by 0.625 degree, launched full each 1 January, one boat); ten years is a short climatology; the smoothing window (31 days) and the 0.5 C floor were fixed, not swept; the Wave Glider's payload budget is still not modelled.
- Next: a two-sided curve (target falls from C as the climatological recovery date approaches) scored against (F)'s km; give the Wave Glider the same payload budget (6zj's first "Next", still open); added resistance in waves at 30 to 40 degrees.

## 2026-11-12 · Milestone 6zj: on a year-long mission with one 10 kWh pack and no port call, how far does a deck-solar boat get, how many days is its payload dark, and does the year still go where 6z said? (predictions in `experiments/run_year_pack.py`'s docstring, committed in 93ac8188f before the run)
- Why: 6z's "Next" (a year-long, not monthly-reset, pack campaign along the transect). 6z's (A) reports 0 km/day when harvest is under the 30 W hotel load but never says the payload then drains the pack and goes dark; its (B) gives the boat a full 10 kWh every month, which is a monthly port call. Rules: 6y's central boat and functions, one pack launched full on 1 January 2024, daily energy steps on 6z's committed NASA POWER files at 30 points with all twelve months (25 transect points, A-60 dropped for sea ice, plus the five corridor sites). Payload first: a day the pack plus that day's harvest cannot carry 24 h of hotel is a down day, and the shortfall is dark hours. (Y) causal: hold station when the trailing 7-day harvest is below the hotel load, else plan trailing harvest + (SOC - 0.5 C)/240 h, slowing within the day if the pack would go below 0. (F) foresight: backward DP (100 Wh pack grid, 50 Wh propulsion steps, 1,000 km per dark hour), executed forward on exact energies. Disclosed: before the commit, (Y) was tried on two synthetic sun series (no 2024 data), which led to the hold-station rule; the DP grid was coarsened from 50 / 25 Wh for run time.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_year_pack.txt`, about 40 s, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests). **P1 held:** within 10 degrees (7 points) (Y) covers **1.31 to 1.86 times** the Wave Glider's best-state year. **P2 held:** (F) has **83 to 214** down days at every transect point at 40 degrees or more. **P3 held:** (Y) has no down day within 10 degrees (none within 20 either). **P4 refuted:** (Y) reaches **0.810** of (F)'s km at Florida-Straits, 0.826 at P+30 (median 0.952 over the 14 points where (F) has no down day). **P5 held:** (Y) beats the Wave Glider's best state at all 11 points at 20 degrees or less and loses at all 9 at 50 or more. **P6 held:** (F) is below 6z's summed (B) everywhere (0.79 to 0.88 of it within 20 degrees, 0 to 0.63 at 40 and beyond). **P7 refuted:** (Y) is within 5% of 6z's summed (A) at only **12 of 30** points (min 0.41 at P+50); within 20 degrees it is 0.96 to 1.00. **P8 held, exactly:** a vehicle with the same panel, pack and hotel that never moves has the **same** down days as (F) at all 30 points. **P9 held:** (Y) down days North-Sea 170, Gulf-of-Alaska 200, Florida-Straits 0.
- Descriptive: payload uptime under (Y) by band (transect means) 100% at 0 to 20 degrees, 96.6% at 30, 84.3% at 40, 70.3% at 50, 59.8% at 60. The largest hotel load a full year carries with no dark day (idle vehicle, which equals (F)): 54 and 48 W at the equator, 31 W at 30 degrees (25 to 27 W in the south, launched into its summer), 17 to 22 W at 40, 10 to 13 W at 50, 8 to 9 W at 60. A 20 kWh pack ends (Y)'s dark days at 30 degrees north and cuts them at 40 N from 110 to 121 to 46 to 71, but leaves 120 to 177 at 50 to 60 degrees; at high latitude it also lowers (Y)'s distance, because the policy then holds a larger reserve. A 1 July launch changes (Y)'s down days by up to 36 (P-30: 76 to 40), so the launch date is a first-order input at 30 to 40 degrees.
- Post hoc (`post_hoc()`, written after reading P1 to P9): at P+50, A+60 and Gulf-of-Alaska (F) covers fewer km than (Y) (0 against 2,286 at P+50). That is the payload-first objective, not a solver fault: (F)'s dark hours equal the idle minimum exactly, and at those points the pack never refills between the summer surplus and the autumn deficit, so every metre moved is payload time lost later. With no dark-hour penalty the same DP covers more than (Y) at all 30 points (8,263 km at P+50, with 534 more dark hours than the minimum). The causal policy sits between the two ends.
- Reading: the month-by-month verdict of 6z survives a real year on one pack where the sun covers the payload: within 20 degrees of the equator a 4 m deck-solar boat runs its 30 W payload all year and covers 1.25 to 1.86 times the 2015 Wave Glider's best-state distance. Poleward of about 35 degrees the question changes. The payload is off for 3 to 7 months a year, and the boat's motion has nothing to do with it: a station-keeping buoy with the same deck panel and pack goes dark on exactly the same days. So a year-round line at 40 degrees or more is a power-system choice (panel area, pack, payload duty cycle) before it is a hull or vehicle choice; the dark-free payload budget (17 to 22 W at 40 degrees, 8 to 13 W at 50 to 60, for 1.15 m2 of panel and 10 kWh) is the number a planner needs first. 6z's (B) overstated a year's distance by 1.1 to 1.3 times in the tropics and 1.6 to 5 times at high latitude, because it refilled the pack every month. The same caution now bites the Wave Glider side harder: its payload is also deck-solar and is not modelled, so its distance lead at high latitude says nothing yet about whether its sensors are on.
- Limits: calm-water boat (no added resistance in waves); daily energy steps with no intra-day battery cycle (night hotel on the pack is assumed always covered when the day's total is); NASA POWER is a 0.5 by 0.625 degree product; one year; launched full; one boat (6y's central); (F) is a DP on a 100 Wh grid with linear interpolation, executed on exact energies, so it is feasible and near-optimal, not proven optimal; the Wave Glider's own payload power and downtime are not modelled.
- Next: give the Wave Glider the same payload budget (a sourced SV3 deck panel area and battery), so both sides carry the dark-day count; a causal policy that starts banking the pack for the payload from the autumn equinox, scored against (F); added resistance in waves on the solar side at 30 to 40 degrees, where the tie sits.

## 2026-11-12 · Milestone 6z: month by month along 150 W and 30 W, where does a deck-solar boat go further per day than the 2015 Wave Glider, and where does the ranking flip? (predictions in `experiments/run_flip_map.py`'s docstring, committed in aeffabfa before the 2024 data were fetched)
- Why: 6y's "Next". It compared the two vehicles at five sites in January and July only and found the ranking flips with season and latitude; two months cannot say where the flip sits or how many months a year each vehicle leads, which is what a planner choosing one platform for a year-round line needs. `experiments/fetch_flip_map.py` reads 2024 ERA5 swh and mwp (Open-Meteo `era5_ocean`, CC BY 4.0) and NASA POWER daily all-sky surface shortwave for 26 transect points (150 W and 30 W, 60 S to 60 N every 10 degrees) plus the five corridor sites. 305 of 312 transect point-months kept: 60 S 30 W loses June to December to sea ice. Rules: 6y's 54 boats and functions and 6x's ERA5 law (a_e 0.533, best state s 1.471, worst 0.882), unchanged; winner = central boat (4 m, 30 W, panel 0.20, derate 0.85) sustained speed (A) against the Wave Glider's best state; flip latitude = smallest |lat| on a meridian and side where the Wave Glider wins.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_flip_map.txt`, under 1 s, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests, 154 pass). **P1 held:** in the winter hemisphere's January (north) and July (south) the central boat sustains 0 km/day at all 11 kept points at 40 degrees or more. **P2 held:** within 10 degrees of the equator (A) > 0 in **100%** of 72 point-months. **P3 held:** Spearman(|lat|, months the Wave Glider wins) **0.921** over 26 points. **P4 held:** winter flip latitude **30, 20, 30, 30** (150 W north in January, south in July; 30 W the same). **P5 held:** in the summer peak month the boat wins at 9 of 10 points at 10 to 50 N (July) and 10 of 10 at 10 to 50 S (January). **P6 held:** the Wave Glider's best state wins **40.3%** of transect point-months. **P7 held:** corridor months won by the boat in 2024: Equator-Pacific 12, Florida-Straits 8, Gulf-of-Maine 6, North-Sea 4, Gulf-of-Alaska 3. **P8 held, at the top:** a full 10 kWh pack at the start of each month (B) moves **14.8 pp** of point-months to the boat and none back. **P9 refuted:** dividing the Wave Glider plan by 6x's ERA5/buoy ratio 1.101 changes only **2.6%** of winners, but moves one winter flip latitude by 20 degrees (150 W north in December, 10 to 30, because 10 N in December is a near tie, 67 against 69 km/day).
- Descriptive: months a year the boat wins, mean over the four points at each |lat|: 12 at the equator, 11.75 at 10, 10.75 at 20, 7.5 at 30, 5.5 at 40, 2 at 50. The year-mean of central (A) is 1.5 to 1.9 times the Wave Glider's best-state plan within 10 degrees (85 to 106 against 55 to 64 km/day), about equal at 30 degrees (56 to 70 against 57 to 63) and 0.6 to 0.8 times at 40 (39 to 49 against 64 to 71). The flip latitude swings about 30 degrees through the year: in the north it sits at 30 degrees in January and February and at 50 degrees from April to August, with no flip at all (boat wins to 60 N on 150 W, to 50 N on 30 W) in June; the south mirrors it, 20 to 30 degrees in June and July. Across the 54 boats the share that beats the best-state Wave Glider falls from 67% at the equator to 59% at 20 degrees, 46% at 30, 32% at 40 and 15 to 17% at 50 to 60.
- Reading: the 6y result generalises. A 4 m deck-solar boat with a 30 W payload goes further per day than the 2015 Wave Glider at its best everywhere within about 20 degrees of the equator in every month, and the Wave Glider goes further everywhere poleward of about 40 degrees in its hemisphere's winter half; between, the answer is the season. For a year-round line, the boat leads on the year's mean below about 30 degrees, the Wave Glider above about 35. The pack result says the boat's poor months are energy, not hull: a boat recharged to full at the start of every month (a monthly port call, not a year-long mission) leads on the year's mean to 40 degrees. The flip latitude as defined is fragile to single near-tie points; the winner map and the months-per-latitude counts are the robust outputs, not a single flip number.
- Disclosed after the pre-registration commit: the sun file was renamed from `power_flip_2024.json` to `sun_flip_2024.json` because `tests/test_solar_sites.py` globs `power_*.json`; no rule changed. The 150 W, 60 N point snaps to a coastal ERA5 cell on the Kenai shelf (Wave Glider plan 33 to 50 km/day, well below the other 60-degree points), so its 5 boat-won months are not open-ocean figures.
- Limits: all of 6y's (calm-water solar boat, so the winter storm belts favour the boat more than they should; energy-neutral at the month's mean sun; NASA POWER is a 0.5 by 0.625 degree product; the Wave Glider's own payload power not modelled) and 6x's (one 2015 vehicle sets the law and its best state; ERA5 about 10% high against buoys); one year; two meridians; (B) restarts every month full.
- Next: put both vehicles on the same payload power budget (a published SV3 deck panel area and battery); price added resistance in waves on the solar side at the 40 to 60 degree winter points, where it would push the flip equatorward; a year-long (not monthly-reset) pack campaign along the transect.

## 2026-11-12 · Milestone 6y: with each corridor site's own sun in place of 5c's single 86 km/day, does a deck-solar boat or the 2015 Wave Glider go further per day? (predictions in `experiments/run_wave_vs_sun_sites.py`'s docstring, committed in aef6d913 before the run)
- Why: 6x compared its ERA5 Wave Glider plan with "the 86 km/day deck-solar ceiling", which is the 4 m monohull's 1.0 m/s best-range speed held for 24 h (5c, 6h), the same at every site and season. 5e had already shown the same boat energy-neutral in 7 months at the equator and none at 60 N. NASA POWER daily all-sky surface shortwave for the five corridor sites, January and July 2024, was already on disk (`experiments/data/daily_<site>.json`), so the solar side can use the same site-months as 6x's wave side. Rules: 5e's 54 boats (3/4/5 m, hotel 20/30/40 W, panel 0.15/0.20/0.23, derate 0.70/0.85; 200 kg, calm water); (A) the highest energy-neutral speed at the month's mean insolation, 0 when harvest is below the hotel load; (B) the highest constant speed a full 10 kWh pack (5c's 25% of 200 kg at 200 Wh/kg) carries through the month day by day; (C) (A) at the month's worst 7-day mean; Wave Glider = 6x's plan at the best and worst 2015 state, recomputed by 6x's own functions.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_wave_vs_sun_sites.txt`, under 1 s, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests, 150 pass). **P1 held:** the central 4 m boat (30 W, 0.20, 0.85) sustains **0 km/day** in January at North-Sea (22 W/m2) and Gulf-of-Alaska (14 W/m2), and also at Florida-Straits (136) and Gulf-of-Maine (57). **P2 held:** in July it beats the Wave Glider's best state at **all five** sites (58 to 103 km/day against 43.5 to 54.7). **P3 held:** in January the Wave Glider's best state wins at **4 of 5** (all but Equator-Pacific, 96 against 45). **P4 held:** 86 km/day overstates the central boat in 7 of 10 site-months, and understates it in the other 3 (96 to 103 km/day at the equator in January and at Florida and the Gulf of Maine in July). **P5 held:** over 540 boat-site-months the Wave Glider's best state beats (A) in **61.7%**. **P6 held:** the fastest boat sustains 150 km/day (5 m, 20 W, 0.23, 0.85, Florida-Straits July). **P7 refuted:** the full pack lifts the central boat by **1.17 to 1.64 times** where (A) > 0 (predicted 1.05 to 1.50); the top is Equator-Pacific in July (175 W/m2, 58 to 95 km/day). **P8 held:** the worst week cuts (A) by more than 20% in 3 of 6 site-months, to 0 at North-Sea and Equator-Pacific in July.
- Descriptive: the central boat's harvest equals its 30 W hotel load at **153 W/m2** of daily-mean insolation. Below that it cannot sustain any speed; above it, because propulsion power grows roughly as v^3 at these Froude numbers, speed jumps quickly (175 W/m2 already gives 58 km/day, 247 gives 96). In January no solar boat of the 54 sustains any speed at North-Sea or Gulf-of-Alaska, and the best boat manages 13 km/day at Gulf-of-Maine, where the Wave Glider plans 58 to 75 km/day at its best state and 35 to 45 at its worst. By boat, 5 m hulls with a 20 W load beat the best-state Wave Glider in 70% of the site-months, 4 m with 30 W in 42%, and 3 m hulls with 30 W or more in 2% or none. With a full pack the central boat covers 78 km/day through January at Florida-Straits, where (A) is 0, so near the threshold the battery, not the sun, decides.
- Reading: 6x's "no ocean point reaches the 86 km/day ceiling" is correct as arithmetic but the wrong comparison. The solar boat's distance per day is a threshold function of insolation against hotel load, not a ceiling: in summer and in the tropics a 4 m deck-solar boat at 30 W sustains 1.2 to 2.4 times the 2015 Wave Glider's best-state plan, and in winter above about 40 degrees it sustains nothing while the Wave Glider still plans 58 to 75 km/day. So the ranking flips by season and latitude, not by vehicle. 6x's sentence that a Wave Glider's case against a solar boat is "endurance, weather and payload power, not distance per day" holds in summer and at low latitudes only; in the winter storm belts its case is also distance. One caution against reading that as a free win: the Wave Glider's payload is solar-powered too, and the same 1.15 m2 deck at 22 W/m2 gives about 4 W, so neither vehicle carries a 30 W payload through a North Sea January without a pack.
- Limits: calm-water solar boat (5o onwards price added resistance in waves, which the winter storm belts would raise); daily-mean harvest with no intra-day battery cycle in (A) and (C); NASA POWER is a 0.5 x 0.625 degree satellite-and-reanalysis product, not on-deck measurement, and the panel tilt, soiling and spray sit in the swept derate; one year (2024) and two months; the Wave Glider side carries 6x's limits (one 2015 vehicle, ERA5 about 10% high against buoys); the Wave Glider's own hotel budget is not modelled; boat parameters are 5e's sweeps, not one vendor's datasheet.
- Next: put the Wave Glider's payload power on the same deck-solar budget (a published SV3 panel area and battery, if one can be found) so both vehicles carry the same payload; price added resistance in waves on the solar side at the January storm-belt sites, where the comparison flips; extend both plans to all twelve months with ERA5 and POWER to map the flip latitude by month.

## 2026-11-11 · Milestone 6x: does a Wave Glider's km/day plan from ERA5 transfer to the 2024 buoys, and what does it say over open ocean? (predictions in `experiments/run_waveglider_era5_sites.py`'s docstring, committed in 16e5203f before the 2024 ERA5 data was fetched)
- Why: 6w's reading said 6v's law "can be planned anywhere ERA5 covers, not only at buoys", but that was checked only along the vehicle's own track in open Hawaiian seas. 6v already has a measured plan at the 2024 NDBC buoys (buoy WVHT and APD), so replacing the buoy with ERA5 at each buoy's grid point tests the transfer where a 0.5 degree model does worst (shelf, Gulf, coast). `experiments/fetch_era5_sites.py` reads ERA5 swh and mwp (Open-Meteo `era5_ocean`, CC BY 4.0) for January and July 2024 at the 11 buoys, the programme's five corridor sites and a 10 degree grid (371 ocean points in January, 364 in July). Rules: 6w's ERA5 vehicle law (a_e 0.533, state index s_m from ERA5 at the vehicle; best month June 2015, s 1.471; worst October, 0.882); km/day = 86.4 s mean (Hs/Tm)^a_e.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_waveglider_era5_sites.txt`, under 1 s, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests). The docstring says 22 buoy-months; 6v's table has 20 (9 buoys in January), a miscount in the rules, not a dropped site. **P1 refuted, just:** ERA5 plans a median **1.101 times** the buoy plan (range 0.910 to 1.216). **P2 refuted:** 75% of buoy-months within 15% (predicted 80%). **P3 refuted in January:** Spearman of the buoys' plans **-0.12 in January**, 0.93 in July. **P4 held:** ERA5 site ratios 1.177 (January; 6v 1.123) and 1.644 (July; 6v 1.725). **P5 held:** the July shelf and Gulf buoy-months miss by more (mean |log ratio| 0.132 against 0.081). **P6 held:** no grid point plans above the 86 km/day deck-solar ceiling at the vehicle's best month in either month (maximum 85.6 at 50 N 40 W in January, 85.4 at 50 S 120 E in July). **P7 held:** the fastest point is in the winter hemisphere's 50 degree band in both months. **P8 held:** 32.6% of grid hours lie outside the mission's 5 to 95% q_e range (0.144 to 0.348 m/s). **P9 refuted:** Florida-Straits is lowest of the five corridor sites in July (43.5 km/day) but the Equator-Pacific is lowest in January (44.7; long 8.6 s swell, low Hs/T), and the North Sea is highest in both (74.5 / 54.7).
- Grid, best state: median 63.0 km/day in January and 61.1 in July (10th to 90th 50 to 77 and 48 to 79). By latitude band the winter hemisphere's 40 to 70 degree seas plan 75 (January, north) and 77 km/day (July, south), the tropics 56 to 57, and the summer high latitudes 53 to 70. At the worst state every figure scales by 0.60 (fleet medians 36 / 29 km/day at the buoys).
- Post hoc (`post_hoc()` in the same script, written after reading P1 to P9): the buoy-to-buoy differences in the ERA5/buoy ratio are almost entirely calibration. The ratio implied by each buoy-month's hour-matched median Hs_e/WVHT and Tm_e/APD, with the vehicle-side calibration divided out (Hs_e/Hs_ob 0.960, Tm_e/T_avg 1.285), correlates **0.991** with the observed ratio (residual sd 0.010 against 0.073 raw). The main term is period: ERA5 mwp runs 1.196 times buoy APD over all matched hours, against 1.285 times the vehicle's MOSE average period, so the state index fitted on the vehicle side carries a period bias that does not hold at the buoys.
- Reading: the ERA5 plan transfers to the buoys to about +10% (+8% in mean log), and site-to-site to the size of the July spread: in summer it ranks the buoys as the buoys do and gets the 1.6 to 1.7 site ratio. In winter, when 6v found every buoy within 12% of each other, the ERA5 ranking is noise, because the site-dependent calibration error (sd 7%) is as large as the site differences. So ERA5 can say which season and which ocean, not which of two nearby winter sites. The calibration that matters is the ratio of the model's mean period to the vehicle's own period measure, and it varies with sea type (0.88 to 1.43 at the buoy-months). Over open ocean the plan is 50 to 79 km/day at the 2015 vehicle's best month and nowhere reaches the 86 km/day deck-solar ceiling (the capability wind drone of 6o reached 1.47 times it in January at the buoys). Unless a vehicle is faster than the 2015 one at its best, a Wave Glider's case against a solar boat is endurance, weather and payload power, not distance per day. About a third of the ocean's hours are outside the seas the law was fitted on, mostly the long-swell tropics (low Hs/T) and the winter storm belts (high).
- Limits: one vehicle and mission sets the law and the state index; ERA5 is a reanalysis (no forecast-lead error); the grid uses the nearest 0.5 degree cell to each 10 degree point, so coastal and ice-edge cells are crude; January and July 2024 only; the solar ceiling (86 km/day) is milestone 5c's single figure, not site-specific; the grid mean uses 20 quantile bins (max error 0.29% on the 32 site-months).
- Next: carry the site calibration (Tm_e/APD) into the plan where a buoy exists and test whether it fixes the January ranking out of sample (fit on 2024, test on 2023 buoy files); a second fielded vehicle in calm, short seas (the 33% of open-ocean hours outside the law's range); a site-specific solar ceiling from the corridor sites' NASA POWER files for a like-for-like map of wave against sun.

## 2026-11-11 · Milestone 6w: with ERA5 reanalysis waves at the vehicle's position in place of 6u's on-board oracle, does a model sea state keep the Wave Glider's speed law and its forecast skill? (predictions in `experiments/run_waveglider_era5.py`'s docstring, committed in ec1f8f03 before the run)
- Why: 6u's H3 forecast used the observed target-hour on-board Hs and period (an oracle), and 6v planned km/day only where a buoy exists. A planner has a wave model, not the future sensor record. ERA5 (Hersbach et al. 2020) via Open-Meteo's marine API (`models=era5_ocean`, CC BY 4.0, Copernicus) gives swh, mwp and pp1d hourly on a 0.5 degree grid; `experiments/fetch_era5_waveglider.py` reads it at the 109 grid points the vehicle passed and joins all 3,657 mission hours. A reanalysis carries model error but no forecast-lead error, so it bounds from above what an archived forecast would give. Rules: 6u's S2 hours (3,281), q = Hs/T; a from log u ~ month + a log q; 6u's forecast rules with the ratio law for both the oracle (Mob) and the reanalysis (Me); 6v's monthly plan as a descriptive check.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_waveglider_era5.txt`, about 60 s, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests). Calibration: median Hs_e/Hs_ob **0.960**, Tm_e/T_avg **1.285**, so **P5 held**. **P1 refuted, on the good side:** the within-month correlation of log q is **0.866** (predicted 0.5 to 0.85; the buoy's was 0.20), log Hs 0.887 and log T only 0.587. **P2 refuted:** **a_e = 0.533 (95% 0.447 to 0.618)** against a_ob 0.534 on the same hours (difference +0.001, -0.051 to +0.060). Errors-in-variables predicted 0.494, and there is no attenuation. With ERA5's peak period in place of mwp the exponent falls to 0.329, so the period definition matters. **P3 refuted:** at 24 h Me/M1 is **0.955 (0.812 to 1.135)**. The oracle with the same ratio law gets 0.880 (0.744 to 1.043); 6u's H3 got 0.767 with the free (b, c) pair. **P4 refuted, just:** Me recovers **38%** of the oracle's 24 h gain (predicted 40 to 90%). **P6 held:** at 72 h Me/M1 is **0.845 (0.700 to 1.014)**, against the oracle's 0.772 (0.655 to 0.918), which is **68%** of the oracle's gain. At 6 h neither term helps (1.004, 1.011). Descriptive plan: the ERA5 monthly km/day is within 0.4 km/day of the on-board plan in every month (June 50.4 against 50.2, October 35.5 against 35.2), and both are within 2 km/day of the observed means (August 40.3 against 42.1, the step month).
- Reading: for planning, a 0.5 degree reanalysis replaces the vehicle's own wave sensor at this vehicle's sites. It gives the same exponent and the same monthly km/day, so 6v's law can be planned anywhere ERA5 covers, not only at buoys, using ERA5's mwp (not its peak period). For hour-to-day speed forecasts it is weaker. Nothing helps at 6 h; at a day ahead the reanalysis adds about 4% skill (not significant); at three days it adds about 15%, two thirds of what the oracle gives. The 24 h oracle gain that the ratio law gets is 12%, not 6u H3's quarter, because H3 used the free pair; that sentence in 6u should be quoted with its model. The missing attenuation (lambda 0.92, a_e equal to a_ob) means either the MOSE record is itself about as noisy as ERA5 against the true forcing, or the vehicle responds to the larger-scale sea the model resolves. The data here cannot tell these apart.
- Limits: one vehicle and mission in open Hawaiian and North Pacific seas, where a 0.5 degree model does well; ERA5 is a reanalysis, so real forecast skill at 24 to 72 h will be lower; the ratio law forces b = -c, and the free pair was not pre-registered for ERA5; the grid point is the nearest 0.5 degree cell, with no interpolation; one cell (20.0 N, 156.0 W, 339 hours) sits in the lee of the Big Island, and its hours were left in, as the rules fixed.
- Next: plan km/day from ERA5 at 6v's 2024 buoy sites and compare with 6v's buoy plan, then at open-ocean survey sites with no buoy; for real forecast skill, a vehicle fielded after 2017, when an archived WAVEWATCH III run exists on ERDDAP (`NWW3_Global_Best` starts 2017-01-01).

## 2026-11-11 · Milestone 6v: with 6u's on-board law U = s (Hs/T)^0.53, is a Wave Glider's planned km/day at the 2024 buoys still set more by the vehicle than by the site? (predictions in `experiments/run_waveglider_site_period.py`'s docstring, committed in 9c6975ba before the run)
- Why: 6u's "Next" and its reading: 6r's km/day rows used the buoy-attenuated Hs^0.218 and no period, so they "understate the spread between sites" and must be rerun before the paper quotes them. The 2024 NDBC file already carries APD (average period) every hour, so this is an offline rerun. Rules: vehicle-state index s_m = median of u / (Hs_ob / T_avg)^0.534 over each 2015 month with 100+ on-board hours (June to October; May has no MOSE); km/day = 86.4 x mean of s (WVHT / APD)^0.534 per buoy-month; the free pair (Hs^0.542 T^-0.711) and a sensor-to-buoy calibration (k_H, k_T on hours with the buoy within 150 km) as checks; coverage = share of hours outside the mission's 5 to 95% Hs/T range.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_waveglider_site_period.txt`, under 1 s, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): s_m 1.248 (Jun), 1.154, 0.851, 0.807, 0.745 (Oct), **state ratio 1.676**. Fleet medians **55.4 (Jan) / 45.2 (Jul) km/day** at the best state, 33.0 / 27.0 at the worst (6r: 52.4 / 48.8 and 32.0 / 29.8). **P1 refuted:** the site ratio rose in July (**1.725**, from 1.335) but fell in January (**1.123**, from 1.203). **P2 held** (1.676 in 1.4 to 1.9). **P3 held:** between-state variance share 0.659 (6r 0.825); between months 0.152 (0.058), buoys 0.119 (0.106). **P4 held:** in July 42036, 42040 and 44013 have 95.2%, 87.5% and 94.5% of hours below the mission's 5th percentile Hs/T (0.184 m/s); 41009 92%. **P5 held:** best buoy-month 59.0 (46013, January). **P6 held:** k_H 1.090, k_T 0.988 (486 hours), +5.4% on both fleet medians. **P7 held:** the free pair is within +0.1% (Jan) and +1.4% (Jul); its site ratios 1.079 / 1.667.
- Post hoc (written after reading P1 to P7, disclosed in the script): median u / (s_m (Hs/T)^a) by mission Hs/T bin is 1.083 (below 0.12, 15 hours), 0.985 (0.12 to 0.15, 20 h), 0.905 (0.15 to 0.184, 128 h), then 0.98 to 1.02 in the bulk and 0.94 above 0.475. The law shows no large bias down to Hs/T 0.12, but on 35 hours; the July Gulf and Nantucket medians (0.095 to 0.113) sit at the bottom of that.
- Reading: once period enters, January's Pacific lead mostly goes: its 2.3 to 2.6 m seas come with 8 to 9 s APD, so Hs/T (0.28 to 0.31) is about the Atlantic and Gulf's (0.25 to 0.30), and every January buoy plans 52 to 59 km/day at the best state. That reverses 6s's "the Pacific lead is wave height", which rested on the buoy-attenuated exponent. In July calm short seas (Gulf, Nantucket, 41009) cut the plan to 32 to 38 km/day against 48 to 55 in the Pacific, so site is now as large a lever as the 2015 vehicle's month-to-month state (1.73 against 1.68), and season moves from 6% to 15% of the variance. The planning advice becomes: in winter carry the vehicle's logged speed and ignore the site; in summer the site's Hs/T matters as much, but the slow-site figures rest on 35 mission hours at comparable heave velocity and should be read as a lower region of uncertain size, not a number. 6u's sentence for the paper (speed a sub-linear function of heave velocity) stands; 6r's "mostly the vehicle" holds in January only.
- Limits: one vehicle and mission (Hawaii, Hs 1.2 to 2.3 m); MOSE "average period" and NDBC APD may differ in definition (median ratio 0.99 near buoys, but measured 100 to 150 km apart and only in Hawaiian seas); the a = 0.534 exponent is 6u's post hoc fit; the state index carries whatever made June 68% faster than October, which 6t and 6u leave partly unexplained; no current, no wind, no payload power.
- Next: a second fielded vehicle in calmer, shorter seas (the Gulf or US east coast) to test the law below Hs/T 0.15, where the July plans sit; an archived WAVEWATCH III forecast in place of 6u's H3 oracle.

## 2026-11-11 · Milestone 6u: with the Wave Glider's own on-board waves and wind in place of a distant buoy, is its weak sea-state response real? (predictions in `experiments/run_waveglider_onboard.py`'s docstring, committed in f35590df before the run)
- Why: every Hs in 6p to 6t came from the nearest NDBC buoy (median 144 km, up to 300 km; up to about 1,000 km post hoc). Under errors-in-variables a noisy regressor shrinks a slope toward 0, so the Hs^0.22 response could be an artefact of the match. The same NOAA ERD ERDDAP server carries the vehicle's own Datawell MOSE wave record (`LiquidR_HBG3_2015_mose_v2`), its weather station and telemetry `total_power`, `sub_heading` and `desired_heading`, none used before (6t's note that the telemetry had no power field was wrong: it was simply not fetched). `experiments/fetch_waveglider_onboard.py` builds 3,657 hourly rows, 3,281 usable with MOSE waves.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_waveglider_onboard.txt`, 80 s, stdlib, deterministic; 6 new tests). **P1 held:** on the 246 6q-training hours with both records, b_ob 0.404 (95% 0.153 to 0.469) against b_buoy 0.092 on the same hours. On all 3,281 hours from June, **b_ob = 0.354 (0.299 to 0.415)**. **P2 refuted, worse than predicted:** the within-month correlation of log Hs on board and at the buoy is **0.20** (predicted 0.3 to 0.8); the attenuation factor is 0.52. **P3 refuted:** at a fixed state the MOSE average-period exponent is **c = -0.71 (-0.80 to -0.61)**, with b rising to 0.54; 6s's null (DPD from a distant buoy, coarsely binned) was the proxy, not the physics. **P4 refuted:** headwind d = -0.0012 per m/s (-0.0044 to +0.0022), so a 5 m/s headwind costs 0.6%. **P5 held:** an on-board oracle Hs alone makes the 24 h persistence forecast worse (1.17 of M1; 0.97 to 1.43). **P6 refuted:** across 7 August on-board Hs fell 24% (2.43 to 1.85 m; wind -10%), and the Hs-and-headwind model explains 27% of the log step, under the third predicted. **P7 refuted:** `total_power` +2.9%.
- Post hoc (`experiments/run_waveglider_onboard_posthoc.py`, written after reading the above; exploratory). H1: log u on log(Hs/T_avg) beats Hs alone by 1,430 BIC, with exponent **0.53**. The free pair is better again by 154 BIC, and b + c = -0.17 (-0.24 to -0.10), so the response is close to, but not exactly, a power of heave velocity. H2: `total_power` follows the sun (median 518k at local 08:00, 640k at 14:00), so it is a battery energy level in unlabelled units. The local-day minimum on 6 August (308k) is the lowest of the mission; the step began at 05 local on 7 August, the 6th-lowest day. Median overnight drain was 5,433 per hour in the week before and 5,195 after (-4%; one night before drained 11,310), so no large load was visibly switched off at the step. H3: an oracle Hs-and-period term (b 0.53, c -0.85 fitted on June and July) cuts the persistence error to **0.946 / 0.767 / 0.674** at 6 / 24 / 72 h (24 h 95% 0.674 to 0.874). H4: the Hs-and-period model explains **30%** of the August week-scale log step, **73%** of the June drop and 52% of its recovery.
- Reading: the buoy match hid most of the sea-state signal. With on-board waves this Wave Glider's through-water speed is a sub-linear function of heave velocity, about (Hs/T)^0.5. That is not the quasi-steady model's linear law (6p's refutation stands), but it is far from the "sea state barely matters" of 6q to 6s. For planning, 6q's advice changes: recent logged speed plus an on-board (or good forecast) Hs and period beats recent speed alone by about a quarter a day ahead and a third at three days. The August step stays mostly vehicle-side (about 70% unexplained, abrupt within the hour, GPS-confirmed in 6t), and the battery low the morning before is a lead for the log, not a cause. The June event that 6t called a reversible event is mostly a calm spell: Hs 1.78 to 1.21 m, period up, wind 5.2 to 2.9 m/s. 6r's km/day rows used the attenuated exponent and no period, so they understate the spread between sites; they need rerunning with (Hs/T)^0.53 before the paper quotes them.
- Limits: one vehicle and mission; the MOSE sits on the vehicle's float, whose motion is the measurement, so a speed-dependent bias in it cannot be ruled out (the encounter period shifts by roughly u/c_p, about 4% at 0.5 m/s in 6 s waves, too small to make c = -0.71 by itself); the H3 forecast uses the observed target-hour sea state (an oracle) and is post hoc; `total_power`'s units are not documented; headwind uses `sub_heading` as the course through the water.
- Next: rerun 6r's buoy plan with (Hs/T)^0.53 from the 2024 NDBC period records; replace H3's oracle with an archived WAVEWATCH III forecast to get an honest forecast skill; ask for the MAGI pilot log about 6 to 7 August 2015.

## 2026-11-11 · Milestone 6t: is the Wave Glider's mid-mission speed loss a step (an event) or a ramp (fouling)? (predictions in `experiments/run_waveglider_step.py`'s docstring, committed before the run)
- Why: 6p to 6s found a vehicle state (about 30% between July and August 2015) that decides this vehicle's speed and km/day plan, with no recorded cause. Fouling grows smoothly; a mode or thruster change, damage or a tangle is a step. The shape tells a planner whether to carry a decay rate or watch for an event. Pre-registered: daily median of log u - 0.218 log Hs (hours <= 300 km from a buoy, from 2015-06-01), models constant, ramp R, step S, step + slope SR and hinge H by BIC, a 5-day moving-block bootstrap of the step.
